Beyond the Lascar group
Ehud Hrushovski

Abstract
. We work in a first-order setting where structures are spread out over a metric space, with quantification allowed only over bounded subsets. Assuming a doubling property for the metric space, we define a canonical core 𝒥 \mathcal{J} associated to such a theory, a locally compact structure that embeds into the type space over any model. The automorphism group of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} , modulo certain infinitesimal automorphisms, is a locally compact group 𝒢 {\mathcal{G}} . The automorphism groups of models of the theory are related with 𝒢 {\mathcal{G}} , not in general via a homomorphism, but by a quasi-homomorphism , respecting multiplication up to a certain canonical compact error set. This fundamental structure is applied to describe the nature of approximate subgroups. Specifically we obtain a full classification of (properly) approximate lattices of S ​ L n ​ ( ℝ ) SL_{n}({\mathbb{R}}) or S ​ L n ​ ( ℚ p ) SL_{n}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}) .



1.
Introduction

This paper consists of three successively more concrete parts, logically forming a progression. The first, purely model theoretic, constructs a canonical geometry associated
with any first-order theory. This ‘core space’ was introduced in
[
27
]
; here we recast it in a ‘local’ setting that allows the core, and the attendant automorphism group, to be locally compact rather than compact. Beyond that, the relation between
the core and the automorphism groups of a models of the theory is now brought out explicitly, in terms of a
quasi-homomomorphism; the Lascar-Shelah neighbor relation plays the role of the error set.
The second part uses this construction to give a structure theorem for approximate subgroups. The ‘Lie model theorem’
proved in
[
26
]
for near-subgroups, and used in
[
8
]
, is extended in modified form to
general approximate subgroups; a conjecture of Massicot-Wagner predicting that this should be possible is thus confirmed. The third part specializes to
approximate lattices
in locally compact groups. In the case of semisimple groups,
a full classification can be achieved, solving in particular a problem of Björklund and Hartnick
[
5
]
.

The rest of the introduction follows this outline in more detail. Type spaces - the Stone spaces dual to the Boolean algebra of formulas - occur for instance in the study of countable categoricity.
These spaces contain all information about
T
, but only as a convenient reformulation of the syntax; they do not bring out well the geometry of definable sets; in particular, they are inert and admit no automorphisms. In Morley’s proof of his theorem on uncountable categoricity, type spaces
over models
first played a leading role.
Morley rank is immediately visible from their topology; and they carry a natural action of
Aut(M)
. This does not, however, give a canonical space or group associated with
T
, since a choice of
M
is required. For some purposes, this is alleviated by uniqueness theorems for saturated models, but then the group
becomes large and unwieldy, falling outside any known structured family of groups.

Shelah
[
63
]
recognized the algebraic imaginaries as a critical geometry associated with a first-order theory
T
.
The automorphism group is a profinite quotient of
Aut(M)
(for
M
a sufficiently homogeneous model of
T
), and a
perfect Galois duality holds between them. For T=ACF one recovers Galois’s duality in full. This was for any theory; for
stable
T
, Shelah showed how amalgamation of substructures is controlled by his group.

Lascar and Pillay
[
43
]
defined the
bounded hyperimaginaries
as a generalization
of the algebraic imaginaries; their automorphism group is a compact topological group. (See also
[
36
]
; the term Kim-Pillay group, and space, comes from
[
29
]
.) Once again this is valid for any first-order theory; for simple theories, it controls amalgamation. This equally beautiful theory later became absorbed in continuous logic, and indeed in
[
4
]
is not even terminologically separated from Shelah’s algebraic imaginaries.

It was Lascar, more than a decade earlier, who suggested that a richer Galois group is needed in unstable theories. He defined two candidates; the compact one used by Kim-Pillay, and a a bigger common quotient of the groups
Aut(M)
, called the general Lascar group. The latter was much studied recently, but
was never found to be the automorphism group of any meaningful geometry (see e.g.
[
33
]
for a related negative result.)
Krupiński, Pillay, and Rzepecki
[
38
]
,
[
39
]
, working group-theoretically, showed suggestively that the general Lascar group was in turn a quotient of a compact group;
see the introduction to
[
27
]
for more detailed references.

In
[
27
]
,
a common subspace
\mathcal{J}
of all type spaces over models was identified, generalizing the Shelah space and Kim-Pillay space for stable and simple theories, and having significant structure beyond the topology. While canonical and closely related to definability in
T
, the automorphism groups
Aut(M)
of models of
T
do not naturally act on
\mathcal{J}
. We show here, however, that they do admit a useful
quasi-action
. A
quasi-homomorphism

\phi:G\to H:K

between two groups
G,H
, relative to an ‘error set’
K\subset H
, is a map
\phi
satisfying

\phi(1)=1
and
\phi(xy^{-1})\in\phi(x)\phi(y^{-1})K
for all
x,y\in G
.
This is of interest when
K
is small in some sense.
We find a quasi-homomorphism
\varphi:Aut(M)\to Aut(\mathcal{J}):\vartriangle
. The error set
\vartriangle
derives from a fundamental ‘neighbor’ relation going back to
[
42
]
(on
Aut(M)

for highly saturated
M
) and
[
64
]
(for indiscernible sequences), and defined here on the core
\mathcal{J}
.

Given a quasi-homomorphism
\phi:G\to H:K
, one can of course factor out the group
\langle K\rangle
generated by
K

so as to obtain an ordinary homomorphism
G\to H/\langle K\rangle
. In the case of the quasi-homomorphism
Aut(M)\to Aut(\mathcal{J}):\vartriangle
described above, this would recover the general Lascar group.

The stance taken here is that factoring out by
\langle\vartriangle\rangle
is the wrong thing to do. An alternative way to forget
unwanted information while discarding much less of value is offered by category theory: in place of changing the objects, add (iso)morphisms. This is set out in Appendix
B
. In the paper itself we will use the categorical approach only in spirit.
We will however analyze mathematically the relevant morphisms.

Examples of quasi-homomorphisms include projective representations of a group
G
; here
H
is a unitary group, the group
of automorphisms of a Hilbert space, and
K
is the one-dimensional center of
H
.
Quasi-homorphisms with unitary targets were also studied notably in
[
68
]
(a 1938 paper first cited, according to MathSciNet, in 2011), and in
[
35
]
, with
K
a set of elements of small operator norm in both cases. When
G
is finite (Turing) or amenable (Kazhdan), they were shown to be close to ordinary homomorphisms.

The quasi-homomorphisms of interest to us will have locally compact target groups, and normal,
compact error sets
K
.
The case
H={\mathbb{R}}
has been very well studied; a quasi-homomorphism into
{\mathbb{R}}
, with compact error set, is called a
quasimorphism
; it is significant in several branches of geometry. (Readers unfamiliar with the notion may enjoy reading
the very short introduction
[
41
]
at this point).

Returning to first-order theories, we can get a clear idea of the smallness of
\vartriangle
if we allow a slight generalisation,
to local logic. This is introduced in §2. In many-sorted logic, quantification is allowed only on a given sort, or finitely many sorts. If one thinks of a sort as compact (say via the type space), then a many-sorted model is only locally compact.
Local logic is a slight variation, likewise restricting quantification, but refraining from destroying possible symmetries by naming specific bounded domains as sorts. In the simplest case,
one has a metric
m
(a discrete metric is good enough for our purposes); and quantification is allowed over balls of bounded
m
-radius.
We will assume a ‘doubling’ property - a
2
-ball for the metric
m
is covered by finitely many
1
-balls.
The construction of
\mathcal{J}
generalizes;
Aut(\mathcal{J})
is now locally compact rather than compact; it has a natural locally compact Hausdorff quotient, which is what we will actually work with. The relation
\vartriangle
on
\mathcal{J}
remains local:

x\vartriangle y
implies that their distance is at most
1
. As a results, the set of automorphisms
\vartriangle^{Aut(\mathcal{J})}
is a normal, compact subset of
Aut(\mathcal{J})
. In fact,
compactness alone does not fully capture the tightness of
\vartriangle
. But along with conjugation invariance - arising from the
canonical nature of
\vartriangle
- it is sufficient for most of the group-theoretic applications.

At this point, the reader is encouraged to consult Theorem
3.10
, summarizing the main properties of the locally compact core
\mathcal{J}
, and Theorem
3.16
,
including the basic properties of the quasi-homomorphism
\varphi
. The material is closely parallel to the non-local case of
[
27
]
. The exposition in sections 2 and 3 is a compromise, not repeating every lemma of
[
27
]
, but attempting to be self-contained with respect to the main line and in particular the results that will be critical in the later sections.

In §
4
, we transpose to the case of definable groups. In the local setting, we look at groups that are direct limits of definable sets; in the model theory literature they are known as piecewise-definable, locally definable, or strict ind-definable.
Thus
G=\cup X_{n}
, where each
X_{n}
is a definable set, and multiplication restricts to a definable map
X_{n}\times X_{n}\to X_{n+1}
.
The doubling condition amounts here to the assumption that
X_{n+1}
is covered by finitely many translates of
X_{n}

This makes each
X_{n}
into an
approximate subgroup
of
G
, as defined in
[
66
]
.
At least at first approach, approximate subgroups are best understood up to commensurability, i.e. without distinguishing
X
and
X^{\prime}
, if each can be covered by finitely many translates of the other.

We will call an approximate subgroup
laminar
if it contains a sequence
(X_{n})
as above
indexed by
negative
integers. A
commensurability class
\omega
is
laminar
if it contains a laminar approximate subgroup.
Model-theoretically,
X
is laminar iff it contains a subgroup cut out by an intersection of generic definable subsets.
Quotienting such a a
\bigwedge
-definable subgroup out from a saturated version of
G
leads to a homomorphism
\varphi:G\to\mathsf{H}

into a locally compact group
\mathsf{H}
; and
\omega
can be recovered from
\varphi
and
\mathsf{H}
, since the elements of
\omega
are intertwined
with pullbacks of compact open neighborhoods of
1
in
\mathsf{H}
. We obtain something like a Bourgain system (
[
22
]
) down to an
arbitrarily fine scale. In the non-laminar case, to extend the metaphor, eddies of a certain size prevent further descent.

We call
X

amenable
if there exists a translation invariant measure on
X^{3}
; more generally it is a
near subgroup
if
an appropriate invariant ideal on definable subsets of
X^{3}
exists. For amenable approximate subgroups and and near subgroups it was proved
in
[
26
]
,
[
61
]
,
[
49
]
, in various settings, that the commensurability
class of
X
is laminar; in fact the sequence
X^{n},n\geq 4
can be continued downwards through the negative integers.

Here we prove, with no amenability assumptions, that
a map
\varphi:G\to\mathsf{H}:K
into a locally compact group still exists, with the same property of intertwining compact with definable.
Only now
\varphi
is a quasi-homomorphism, a compact, normal error set
K
is allowed;
and open neighborhoods of
K
(rather than of
1
) are considered on the locally compact side. See Theorem
4.2
.

Model-theoretically, the characteristic definability property of
\varphi
in the laminar case was
definable separation
:
if
C,C^{\prime}
are disjoint compact subsets of
\mathsf{H}
, then
\varphi^{-1}(C),\varphi^{-1}(C^{\prime})
are separated by a definable set.
If we give
\mathsf{H}
a metric,
C,C^{\prime}
are separated by some distance
\epsilon>0
.
In the general setting, we still have the separation property but now assuming
CK,C^{\prime}K
are disjoint; they must be seen to be distinct even if our resolution is limited to the radius of
K
.
In the laminar case
\epsilon>0
is arbitrary, but in general a certain minimum separation is required.

Massicot and Wagner wrote, regarding approximate subgroups: "We conjecture that even without the definable amenability assumption a suitable Lie model exists"
[
49
]
. The term ‘Lie model’ was coined earlier in
[
8
]
, where it was the starting point for the classification of pseudo-finite approximate groups.
The meaning of ‘suitable’ was not further clarified, but
I view Theorem
5.16
as a confirmation of their intuition. It asserts that
any commensurability class of approximate subgroups
arises by pullback of compact neighborhoods under an quasi-homomorphism into a Lie group
L
; moreover, the error set is contained
a bounded subset of a closed, normal abelian subgroup
A\trianglelefteq L
,
A\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, such that
L
-conjugation respects a Euclidean structure on
A
.
(We refer to such actions as
rigid
.)

It was not immediately apparent
whether Theorem
5.16
has real content or merely replaces one kind of approximateness (for subgroups) by
another (for homomorphisms); the problems attacked in part 3, described below, were intended as a testing ground. The latter turned out to be a much tighter condition, thanks to the combined compactness and normality of the error set and the finite dimensionality of the target. To bring this out directly we give two additional formulations within §5,
a group-theoretic and a model-theoretic one. The group-theoretic version, Theorem
5.25
, points out that the quasi-homomorphism

\varphi:G\to\mathsf{H}:K
of Theorem
5.16
can be viewed as an (ordinary) homomorphism from an ‘almost’ central extension
\tilde{G}
of
G
by
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, into the Lie group
\mathsf{H}
. Here ‘almost’ means that the image in
End({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
of conjugation by elements of
\tilde{G}
,
while not trivial, is contained in a compact subgroup.
This resembles the situation with projective representations of Lie groups, seen as representations
of a central extension. The cocycle coding the group extension will be a bounded one, forming one connection with the far-reaching results of bounded cohomology (e.g.
[
23
]
,
[
10
]
,
[
11
]
,
[
52
]
.)

The second formulation is Theorem
5.21
. The model-theoretic avatar of an approximate subgroup in the laminar case is an
\bigwedge
-definable group ‘of bounded index.’ Here we show that in general, an approximate subgroup is associated with
an
\bigwedge
-definable set, obtained as the ‘approximate kernel’ of finitely many (homogeneous) quasimorphisms on an
\bigwedge
-definable group
\Gamma
of bounded index, i.e. the pullback of a compact neighborhood of the error set in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. This connects again, not in quite the same way, to bounded cohomology.

For instance,
since groups of bounded exponent admit neither dense homomorphisms into connected Lie groups nor unbounded quasimorphism,
any approximate subgroup of a bounded exponent group is commensurable to an actual subgroup; the conclusion is the same
as in
[
26
]
, where this was used as an illustration of the theory, but the hypothesis includes no amenability assumption.

The
\bigwedge
-definable group
\Gamma
is the kernel of a continuous homorphism into a locally compact group
with respect to the logic topology;
the quasimorphism
\Gamma\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is also continuous; so that
the bumpiness alluded to above is felt only at the scale of the error set.

The (symmetrized) approximate kernel of a nontrivial quasimorphism is always an approximate subgroup (Proposition
5.12
), but never a laminar one (Proposition
5.29
)

The third part of the paper, consisting of §6, §7 and Appendix A, is concerned with the geometry of numbers; but this will not be obvious at the outset.
Our working environment in §6 is an easily defined class of locally compact groups, that we call
abstractly semisimple
. It has two defining requirements: no abelian normal subgroups, no discrete conjugacy classes other than the trivial ones.
These serve as ambient groups; within them, we consider discrete approximate subgroups. (Perhaps it is worth recalling here, though I am aware of no direct connection, the fundamental role of discreteness in the theory of pseudo-finite approximate subgroups of
[
8
]
.)
We call a commensurability class of discrete approximate subgroups
strictly dense
if the group generated by any element of the class is dense; this is at an opposite extreme from discrete subgroups; under certain assumptions, a decomposition exists into the two kinds. Building on the results of §5, we begin by showing
that, for abstractly semisimple groups,
a strictly dense approximate subgroup is commensurably contained in a laminar one.

See Theorem
6.9
.

Recall that a
lattice
is a discrete subgroup of finite co-volume; it is called
uniform
if it is co-compact. In the case of the Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, a lattice is the accepted mathematical model of a crystal, and their
histories are inseparable. Yves Meyer in
[
50
]
defined, in the setting of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
approximate lattices
;
this later became the mathematical home for quasicrystals.
Meyer proved
that they are laminar as approximate subgroups, with Lie model
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
; he did so in the equivalent, geometrically suggestive language of
model sets
.
On the other hand, lattices in non-commutative locally compact groups such as
SL_{n}({\mathbb{R}})
play a major role in many parts of geometry, ergodic theory and number theory; especially relevant to us are the arithmeticity theorems of
[
48
]
.

Björklund and Hartnick
[
5
]
were first to investigate approximate versions. They defined uniform approximate lattices, and offered two “tentative" definitions in the non-uniform case. We will suggest another definition, defining
an
approximate lattice in the sense of finite covolume
to be a discrete approximate subgroup
\Lambda
of
G
, such that
\Lambda C=G
for some Borel set
C
of finite measure. As in
[
5
]
,
\Lambda
is said to be
uniform
if
C
can be taken compact.
It seems to work;
the basic properties of approximate lattices, with this elementary definition, are developed in Appendix
A
.
After writing this, I ran into
Problem 6.4 of
[
12
]
, asking for the “right" definition of an approximate lattice; Appendix §
A
, then, is our possibly naive proposal.
In this paper, approximate lattice will always mean, by way of abbreviation, ‘approximate lattice in the sense of finite covolume’.

Among the approximate lattices, Björklund and Hartnick consider the
Meyer sets
. The definition (that we naturally extend from the uniform case) inolves not only
G
but also an additional locally compact

\mathsf{H}
, that we will call the complementary group, and a lattice
\Gamma
in
G\times\mathsf{H}
. A Meyer set
M
is defined by the positive primitive formula of
local
logic:
(\exists h\in C)((x,h)\in\Gamma)
, where
C
is a compact neighborhood of
1
in
H
.
This description is equivalent to laminarity of
M
, with
\mathsf{H}
corresponding to the target of the homomorphism into
a locally compact group.

I am grateful to Emmanuel Breuillard for introducing me to this beautiful theory, and explaining this equivalence between the language of laminarity, or Lie models, and of Meyer model sets.

Björklund and Hartnick posed the problem of proving laminarity when possible (
[
5
]
, Problem 1, formulated there for
uniform approximate lattices.) Simon Machado, in
[
44
]
,
[
45
]
, did so in the nilpotent and solvable cases,
and for amenable groups. In §
6
, complementing this work, we solve Problem 1 for semisimple groups
G
.
To do so, we will use a local logic where ‘local’ implies ’discrete’. This contrasts with the natural treatment of
say
SL_{n}({\mathbb{R}})
in local logic, where “local" means "compact" (see e.g.
[
30
]
, section 7.)
Nevertheless the local compactness of
Aut(\mathcal{J})
(as mediated by Theorems 4.11,5.10)
allows us to recover compactness, effectively
finding the complementary group and presenting the
approximate lattice as a Meyer set. See Corollary
6.11
.
The duality between compactness and discreteness seen here is a key feature of this world.

At this point, Margulis arithmeticity becomes available. See arithmodelset for the definition of an
arithmetic approximate lattice
. It is just like the definition of an arithmetic lattice in
[
48
]
, except that
(as if in line with the recommendation of Hensel and Weil) all places are treated as equals; no special provision is made for archimedean primes. If we switch attention
for a moment to rings,

{\mathbb{Z}}
is the simplest lattice (in
{\mathbb{R}}
), while the approximate ring
\{a/p^{n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p]:|a|\leq p^{n}\}
is the simplest
arithmetic approximate lattice (in
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
). In each case, the (approximate) lattice
R
is the set of rationals whose norm in every completion, other than the ambient locally compact group, is at most
1
. And in both cases, appropriately understood,

G(R)
gives rise to arithmetic lattices in
G({\mathbb{R}})
or
G({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
for semisimple groups
G
.
Theorem
7.4
shows that
any approximate lattice in
G
is commensurable with a product of lattices and arithmetic approximate lattices of direct factors of
G
.
Note that the arithmeticity of the strictly approximate lattices is valid even for
n=2
; higher rank assumptions are not required.

To illustrate the concrete character of this answer, let us describe, up to commensurability, the approximate lattices
\Lambda
in
SL_{n}({\mathbb{R}})
.
They depend on a choice of a number field
K\leq{\mathbb{R}}
and an algebraic group
G\leq GL_{N}
defined over
K
,
and isomorphic to
SL_{n}
over
{\mathbb{R}}
(the case
G=SL_{n}
,
n=N
is interesting enough). Let
\Lambda_{K}
be
the set of matrices in
G(K)\leq GL_{N}(K)
of the form
I+M
, where
I
is the identity matrix and
M
is a matrix whose nonzero entries are Pisot numbers
\alpha
with
{\mathbb{Q}}(\alpha)=K
. Then
\Lambda_{K}
is an approximate lattice in
SL_{n}({\mathbb{R}})
.
It follows from Theorem
7.4
that
any approximate lattice in
SL_{n}({\mathbb{R}})
is commensurable to a conjugate of one of these, or to a lattice.

In positive characteristic, strictly approximate lattice seem unnecessary, and in fact, do not exist (Corollary
7.5
).

The class of abstractly semisimple groups was introduced in §6 as simply a convenient home for groups like
SL_{n}(K)
, with
K
a local field, and their more demanding cousins; but it eventually becomes natural to take interest in the class itself. Theorem
7.7

shows that all strictly approximate lattices in abstractly semisimple groups are of arithmetic origin. In particular,
by Remark
7.8
,
an arbitrary abstractly semisimple group containing a strictly approximate lattice is virtually isogenous an adelic product of semisimple groups over local fields
, where
‘virtually isogenous’ allows homomorphisms with co-compact image and compact kernel.
The proof of this recognition theorem
uses Margulis arithmeticity again, and a method of pivoting over the complementary locally compact.

Finally, we take a peek beyond soluble or semisimple groups. We construct a simple example of a non-laminar approximate lattice. Returning to bounded cohmology, we also give a criterion for an approximate lattice
\Lambda\leq G
, where
G
is semisimple, ensuring laminarity of any approximate lattice
\Lambda^{\prime}
lying above
\Lambda
in an algebraic group
G^{\prime}
with semisimple part
G
.

Leitfaden: The appendices are, within the present paper, self-contained.Sections (2,3,4) form a logical sequence. The main results of the later sections require, from this development, only parts (1,2) of Theorem
4.2
. The reader interested mostly in approximate lattices can read Appendix
5
, the first few pages of §
5
, including Theorem
5.16
, and turn to §
6
.

Questions of uneven scope and difficulty are scattered through the text, mostly at the end of the sections.

Thanks to Arturo Rodriguez-Fanlo and Anand Pillay for their reading and comments, and to Yves de Cornulier for explaining
the literature around Proposition
C.3
.

Immediately after writing this paper I became aware that Simon Machado had been working on the structure of
approximate lattices in semisimple groups at the same time. He
obtained similar results including arithmeticity and rigidity results for strong approximate lattices in higher rank Lie groups, via what appears to be a beautiful and completely different approach via Zimmer cocycle rigidity; see
[
47
]
.
See also the introduction to §A. The basic definitions differ slightly and still need to be compared in detail; see in particular the comments added to Questions
7.11
and
A.22
.

Machado also drew my attention, while I was lecturing on these results, to
the paper
[
3
]
of Bader, Furman and Sauer; it is very close in both statement and proof to Theorem
7.7
and Remark
7.8
.
I left the treatment as it was to illustration of the naturality of the approximate lattice approach, and with future generalizations beyond the laminar framework in mind.

While the results that approximate lattices in semisimple groups are close to lattices (in other groups) are very satisfactory,
they also mean that the additional flexibility of approximate lattices must find use outside the semisimple setting, or beyond the lattice framework.
In particular Kazhdan suggested investigating discrete approximate subgroups in
GL_{n}
over local fields, with respect to growth rates of the number of points in increasing balls. Can growth rates be achieved for approximate subgroups that are not possible for subgroups?

Moving to the approximate lattice setting allows investigating an arithmetic lattice in
GL_{n}({\mathbb{R}})
within any
GL_{n}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
;
it suffices to extend to an adelic lattice, cut with neighborhoods of the identity in the other places and project to
GL_{n}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
;
little information is lost, and in particular the commensurability class of the original lattice can be reconstructed.
Taking ultraproducts, and noting that the degree
k
of approximateness does not depend on
p
, one can even obtain
such information within linear groups over equicharacteristic Henselian fields
K
. Can some of the theory of these lattices be seen more clearly when working in
GL_{n}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
or
GL_{n}(K)
?

2.
Local logic

We begin by reviewing the theory of existentially closed and saturated models. This theory belongs to the model theory of the 1960’s, and was already recalled in detail in
[
27
]
. But we need a generalization here, permitting a localization of the quantifiers without disturbing a possible symmetry. The extension is slight in that upon adding a constant symbol
c
in

{\mathcal{D}}
, we return to the previous positive-primitive setting, with balls of various radii around
c
are viewed as sorts.
As usual more care is needed to subtract a constant than to add one.

Throughout this paper, unless explicitly specified otherwise,
the word ‘definable’ will mean: by a formula of the given language, without parameters.

We assume given a relational language (with equality)
{\mathcal{L}}
, with a distinguished sort
{\mathcal{D}}
, and a distinguished family
\{\mu\}
of binary relations on
{\mathcal{D}}
, called
locality relations
. For simplicity, we will think of
{\mathcal{D}}
as the only sort, and
assume we have only one locality relation
\mu_{1}
. For any
{\mathcal{L}}
-structure
A
, we define a metric
\mu
on
A
according to the Cayley graph of
\mu_{1}
:

\mu(x,y)\leq 1\iff x=y\vee\mu_{1}(x,y)\vee\mu_{1}(y,x)

\mu(x,y)\leq n+1\iff(\exists z)(\mu(x,z)\leq 1\wedge\mu(z,y)\leq n)

We write
\mu_{n}(x,y)
for
\mu(x,y)\leq n
(replacing
\mu_{1}
by its symmetrization),
and also
\mu_{n}(a):=\{y:\mu_{n}(a,y)\}
.

By a pp formula,we mean one built from atomic formulas using conjunction and
local existential quantification
: if
\phi(x,y,\cdots)

is a pp formula with a free variable
x
and another variable or constant
y
(both of sort
\mathcal{D}
), then
(\exists x)(\mu_{1}(x,y)\wedge\phi)
is also pp. Thus iterated quantification ranges over a ball of bounded radius. There are no pp sentences, though they do appear as soon a a constant is named.

A
primitive-universal theory

{\mathcal{T}}
includes negations of sentences of the form
(\exists x)(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\psi_{i})
,
where
x
is a tuple of variables, and
\psi_{i}
a tuple of atomic formulas, or equivalently a pp formula; thus a typical sentence
can be written
(\forall x)\neg\psi(x)
.
A
primitive-existential theory
includes sentences of the form
(\exists x)(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\psi_{i})
.
Given a primitive-universal theory
{\mathcal{T}}
, let
{\mathcal{T}}_{\exists}
consist of all existential sentences
(\exists x)\psi(x)
where
\neg(\exists x)\psi(x)\notin{\mathcal{T}}
.

A
weak model
of
{\mathcal{T}}
is a structure
A
where the sentences of
{\mathcal{T}}
hold. It is a
model
of
{\mathcal{T}}
, in the sense of local logic, if it is a weak model where any two elements of
{\mathcal{D}}(M)
are at finite
\mu
-distance. We will sometimes
use the term
local model
for emphasis and clarity.

We say LJEP holds for
{\mathcal{T}}
if for any two local models
A,B
of
{\mathcal{T}}
, there exists a local model
C
of
{\mathcal{T}}

and homomorphisms
A\to C
and
B\to C
. In this case,
{\mathcal{T}}^{\pm}:={\mathcal{T}}\cup{\mathcal{T}}_{\exists}
is consistent,
(and indeed any existentially closed model of
{\mathcal{T}}
is a model of
{\mathcal{T}}^{\pm}
).

In our application to the core, LJEP will hold in a strong form, (LJEP
k
): If
(\exists x)\psi(x),(\exists y)\psi^{\prime}(y)
are in
{\mathcal{T}}_{\exists}
, where
\psi,\psi^{\prime}
are pp and
x,y
are variables
(of sort
\mathcal{D}
), then

(\exists x)(\exists y)(\mu_{k}(x,y)\wedge\psi(x)\wedge\psi^{\prime}(y))\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\exists}
.

In the main application, in fact, we will have LJEP
2
; in case of transitivity, when
{\mathcal{D}}
has a single 1-type, LJEP
0
holds.

LJEP
k
implies LJEP: If
A,B\models{\mathcal{T}}
, pick
a\in{\mathcal{D}}(A),b\in{\mathcal{D}}(B)
;
then the union of the diagrams of
A
and of
B
, along with the sentence
\mu_{k}(a,b)
, is consistent. Any weak model
of this union,
restricted to the universe spanned by
A,B
, will be a model of
{\mathcal{T}}
.

Remarks on LJEP
One can define a distance on maximal pp types, namely
d(p,q)\leq n
if

\mu_{k}(x,y)\wedge p(x)\wedge q(y)
is consistent with
{\mathcal{T}}
. Define an equivalence relation on types,
d(p,q)<\infty
. Then
LJEP holds iff
any two types are
equivalent. Without the joint embedding property, the results of this section still hold, except for the uniqueness of the universal model in Proposition
2.1
;
instead, there is one universal model realizing each equivalence class.

Local logic ultrapowers of a model
M
of
{\mathcal{T}}
are formed by first taking the ordinary ultrapower, then restricting
to the elements at finite distance from some element of
M
. We can similarly define ultraproducts of models
M
of
{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
, i.e. models
M
of
{\mathcal{T}}
with a distinguished element
a=c^{M}\in{\mathcal{D}}(M)
.

A model
A
of
{\mathcal{T}}
is
existentially closed
(abbreviated e.c.) if
{\mathcal{D}}(A)\neq\emptyset
, and for every homomorphism
f:A\to B
, where
B\models{\mathcal{T}}
, and
any
{\mathcal{L}}_{A}
-pp sentence
\phi
, if
B_{A}\models\phi
then
A_{A}\models\phi
. Here
{\mathcal{L}}_{A}
is
{\mathcal{L}}

expanded by constants for the elements
a\in A
; they are interpreted as
a
in
A_{A}
and as
f(a)

in
B_{A}
.

In particular, if
A
is e.c. then
A
is
simple
, i.e. every homomorphism
f:A\to B
is an
\mathcal{L}
- embedding (an isomorphism onto the image.)
Thus
A
is e.c. iff it is simple, and existentially closed with respect to embeddings.

It is clear from Zorn’s lemma that any model
A
of
{\mathcal{T}}
admits a homomorphism into a simple one.
The usual direct limit construction shows that any simple model
A
of
{\mathcal{T}}
admits an embedding into an existentially closed model
B
of
{\mathcal{T}}
, with
|B|\leq|{\mathcal{L}}_{A}|+\aleph_{0}
. Write
M{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
if
M
is an e.c. model of
{\mathcal{T}}
. Also, for any local sentence
\psi
, write
{\mathcal{T}}{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}\psi
if

M\models\psi
whenever
M{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
.

The class of e.c. models admits amalgamation: if
f_{i}:A\to B_{i}
, we may embed each
B_{i}
in an ultrapower
A^{*}
of
A
, then compose with a homomorphism to an e.c. model.

An e.c.-model
\mathcal{U}\models{\mathcal{T}}
is
\lambda
-saturated
if whenever
A
is an e.c. substructure of
\mathcal{U}
and
h:A\to B
an embedding with
B{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
with
|B|<\lambda
, there exists an embedding
g:B\to\mathcal{U}
with
g\circ h=Id_{A}
.

For pp formulas
\phi(x),\psi(x)
we write
\phi\perp\psi
if
{\mathcal{T}}\models(\forall x)\neg(\phi\wedge\psi)
.
If
M{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
, for any pp formula
\phi(x)
and any
a
from
M
, we have
M\models\phi(a)

or
M\models\psi(a)
for some pp formula
\psi\perp\phi
. This is proved just as in
[
27
]
2.3(1),
taking into account that pp formulas now use local quantifiers only.

Write
\phi{\underset{e.c.}{\subset}}{}\psi
if
\theta\perp\phi
whenever

\theta\perp\psi
; in e.c. models we have
\phi{\underset{e.c.}{\subset}}{}\psi
iff
\phi(M)\subseteq\psi(M)
.

We will be interested in a generalized finiteness condition on
{\mathcal{T}}
:

(ecB): For some infinite cardinal
\kappa
, any e.c. model of
{\mathcal{T}}
has cardinality
\leq\kappa
.

Here is a more concrete form of this condition; we will name it positive primitive compactness. Note the similarity, in complete metric setting, to the ’totally bounded’ definition of local compactness;
here one thinks of
\neg\phi
as an open neighborhood of the diagonal, with
\phi
ensuring a distance bounded away from
0
, in any given bounded subset.

(ppC): Let
\phi(x,y)
be a pp formula, implying
\mu_{n}(x,y)
for some
n
, and with
\phi\perp\ =
; i.e.
{\mathcal{T}}\models\neg(\exists x)\phi(x,x)
. Then for some
k=k(\phi)\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

{\mathcal{T}}\models\neg(\exists x_{0},\cdots,x_{k})\bigwedge_{0\leq i<j\leq k}\phi(x_{i},x_{j})
.

Walter Taylor isolated this condition (in the non-local setting) using the Erdos̈-Rado theorem, in connection with the Hanf number for e.c. models; i.e. he proved the equivalence
of (ecB) with (ppC).
If (ppC) fails, it is clear from compactness that there exist arbitrarily large e.c. models of
{\mathcal{T}}
; indeed they contain
arbitrarily long sequences
(a_{i})
with
\phi(a_{i},a_{j})
for all
i<j
, precluding any possible equality
a_{i}=a_{j}
. Conversely if
(ppC) holds, (ecB) follows with
\kappa=2^{\mathcal{L}}
;
This is due (in the non-local setting) to Walter Taylor.
we prove this below by constructing the universal model without any mention of cardinals bigger than
2^{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be a local primitive-universal theory enjoying (ppC).
Then:

(1)

Any
M\models{\mathcal{T}}
admits a homomorphism to a unique
|\mathcal{L}|^{+}
-saturated e.c. model
\mathcal{U}
.
If
M{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
then
\mathcal{U}
is unique up to isomorphism over
M
.

(2)

\mathcal{U}
is maximal: if
\mathcal{U}\leq B{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
then
U=B
. If
f:\mathcal{U}\to B\models{\mathcal{T}}
is a homomorphism,
there exists a homomorphism
r:B\to\mathcal{U}
with
r\circ f=Id_{\mathcal{U}}
.

(3)

Any homomorphism on
\mathcal{U}
is an embedding, and
any endomorphism
f:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
is an isomorphism.

(4)

\mathcal{U}
is homogeneous for pp types.

(5)

Any e.c. model
A
of
{\mathcal{T}}
satisfies
|A|\leq 2^{|\mathcal{L}|}
.

(6)

if
{\mathcal{T}}
has LJEP, then
\mathcal{U}
is universal, i.e. , i.e. any
M\models{\mathcal{T}}
admits a homomorphism
M\to\mathcal{U}
; and

{\mathcal{T}}
is the unique universal e.c. model of
{\mathcal{T}}
.

Proof.

We may assume
M{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
, by finding a homomorphism from
M
to an e.c. model.
Existence of an
|\mathcal{L}|^{+}
-saturated e.c. model
\mathcal{U}
, embedding
M
, with
|\mathcal{U}|\leq 2^{|\mathcal{L}|+|M|}
, now
follows from the usual construction of saturated models, using amalgamation repeatedly and taking direct limits.

Claim .
Let
\mathcal{U}
be an
|\mathcal{L}|^{+}
-saturated e.c. model. Then
\mathcal{U}
is maximal.

Proof.

Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that
B{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
,
\mathcal{U}\leq B
, and
b\in B\setminus U
. By
|\mathcal{L}|^{+}
-saturation, one can find a sequence

(a_{i}:i<|\mathcal{L}|^{+})
with
a_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
such that
a_{i}
has the same pp type as
b
over
\{a_{j}:j<i\}
. Since
a_{i}\in\mathcal{U}

we have
a_{i}\neq b
,
and so since
B
is e.c. there exists a pp formula
R_{i}\perp\ =
with
R_{i}(a_{i},b)
and such that
R_{i}
implies
\mu_{n}

for some
n=n_{i}
. By refining the sequence we may
assume all
R_{i}
are equal to some
R
, and all
n_{i}=n
. Then
R(a_{j},a_{i})\wedge\mu_{2n}(a_{i},a_{j})
holds for all
j<i
, contradicting (ppC).
∎

Thus the first part of (2) holds.
Now uniqueness in (1) follows too: if
M
admits a homomorphism into two such e.c. models
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
, by
amalgamation over
M
they embed over
M
into a third model
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
; but then by the claim the embeddings
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}

and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
are isomorphisms (over
M
.)

(3) Any homomorphism from any
A{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
into a model of
{\mathcal{T}}
is an embedding,
by definition of e.c..

Let
f:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
be an endomorphism,
with image
U^{\prime}\leq U
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is e.c.,
f
is an isomorphism
\mathcal{U}\to U^{\prime}
;
hence
U^{\prime}
is also maximal. Since
U^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{U}
, we have
\mathcal{U}=U^{\prime}
.
Thus
f
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{U}
.

As
{\mathcal{U}}
is universal, if
\mathcal{U}\leq B\models{\mathcal{T}}
there exists a homomorphism
f:B\to{\mathcal{U}}
;
on
{\mathcal{U}}
it induces an isomorphism
g
; so
r=g^{-1}\circ f:B\to{\mathcal{U}}
is as required in the second sentence of (2).

(4)
Homogeneity for pp types: let
C,C^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{U}
and let
g:C\to C^{\prime}
be a bijection preserving pp formulas.
We may assume
C\neq\emptyset
, say
c\in C
.
Using locality of
\mathcal{U}
, any existential sentence
\theta
true in
\mathcal{U}_{C}
is implied by a stronger pp sentence, specifying
also a distance of the witnesses from
c
. Hence
\theta
, transposed via
g
, is also true in
\mathcal{U}_{C^{\prime}}
.
Thus
g
extends to an embedding
G
of
\mathcal{U}
in some elementary extension
\mathcal{U}^{*}
of
\mathcal{U}
(a weak model initially).
Restricting to elements at finite distance from
g(c)
will give a local model
V
including all of
g(\mathcal{U})
.
We also have the usual inclusion of
\mathcal{U}
in
\mathcal{U}^{*}
, and again it is contained in
V
.
Let
r:V\to\mathcal{U}
be a retraction. Then
r\circ G:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
is an automorphism extending
g
.

For (5), we may assume that
A
is
|\mathcal{L}|^{+}
-saturated. By Löwenheim-Skolem,
A
contains an
|\mathcal{L}|^{+}
-saturated e.c. substructure
A^{\prime}
of cardinality at most
2^{|\mathcal{L}|}
. By the Claim we have
A=A^{\prime}
.

(6) Now assume LJEP. Then any two
|L|^{+}
-saturated e.c. models
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\models T
are isomorphic. Indeed
by LJEP
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
both embed into some
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
, and then
\mathcal{U}\cong\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}\cong\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
as in the proof of uniqueness in (1).

Any universal
U{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{U}
: since
U
is universal,
there exists a homomorphism
f:U\to\mathcal{U}
, which must be an embedding; so we may assume
U\leq\mathcal{U}
.
Then there exists a retraction
r:\mathcal{U}\to U
. But endomorphisms of
\mathcal{U}
are isomorphisms, so
\mathcal{U}\cong U
.

∎

In the absence of LJEP, we can define a distance on maximal pp types, namely
d(p,q)\leq n
if

\mu_{k}(x,y)\wedge p(x)\wedge q(y)
is consistent with
{\mathcal{T}}
. Define an equivalence relation on types,
d(p,q)<\infty
.
Then one can generalize (6) to (6’): there is one universal model realizing each equivalence class of types.

2.2.
The pp topology on
{\mathcal{U}}
.

Let us topologize the sorts of
{\mathcal{U}}
(specifically,
{\mathcal{D}}^{m}
for
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
).
We give each sort of
\mathcal{U}
the coarsest topology such that every pp-definable set, with parameters, is closed.
Thus as a pre-basis for
{\mathcal{D}}^{m}
we take the complements of sets of the form
\{x:R(x,c)\}
, with
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
,
c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\in{\mathcal{U}}
, and
R
a pp relation. While the existential quantifiers in
R
must be bounded in line
with the local definition of pp, there is no requirement that
R
imply a finite distance between its variables. In case every pp relation
is equivalent to a conjunction of atomic formulas (as will hold for the pattern language, Theorem
3.10
), we can of course
restrict attention to atomic
R
.
As we included equality in the language, singletons in
{\mathcal{D}}^{n}
are pp-definable with parameters and hence closed, so
each sort of
\mathcal{U}
is T1.

We will now make a further assumption; essentially, it asserts that
\infty
can be separated from any given point
of
\mathcal{U}
by disjoint open sets.

(
{Loc}
) There exists a pp formula
\mu_{>n}(x,y)
such that
\mu_{>n}\perp\mu_{n}
, and for some

N>n
,
{\mathcal{D}}{\underset{e.c.}{\subset}}{}\mu_{>n}\cup\mu_{N}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
a\in{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{U})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
B=\mu_{n}(a)\subset{\mathcal{D}}
be a
\mu
-ball. Then
B^{m}\subset{\mathcal{D}}^{m}
is compact.
Assuming (
{Loc}
) holds,
{\mathcal{D}}
is locally compact;
1
1

1

We use the term to mean that every point has a compact neighborhood; it does not presuppose that
{\mathcal{D}}
is Hausdorff.
and every compact subset of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U})
is contained
in some ball
\mu_{n}(a)
.

Proof.

Consider a family
F_{i}
of basic closed subsets of
B^{m}
with the finite intersection property.

F_{i}
is defined by
R_{i}(x,c_{i})
with
R_{i}
a finite disjunction of pp formulas. In an elementary extension
U^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{U}}
,
one can find
d=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})
with
R_{i}(d,c_{i})
holding for all
i
, as well as
\mu_{n}(a,d_{i})
for each
i\leq m
.
We can initially allow
U^{\prime}
to be a non-local extension, then discard all elements at infinite distance from
a
; this will not effect the truth value of local formulas for the remaining elements, and the
d_{i}
remain in the domain.
By Proposition
2.1
there exists
r:U^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}
,

r|{\mathcal{U}}=Id_{\mathcal{U}}
. Then
R_{i}(r(d),c_{i})
holds for each
i
, so
r(d)\in\cap_{i}F_{i}
and

\cap_{i}F_{i}\neq\emptyset
.

If (
{Loc}
) holds, then
a
lies in the interior of
\mu_{N}(a)
, since it is contained in the complement of
\mu_{>n}(a)
which in turn is contained in
\mu_{N}(a)
. Hence
a
has a compact neighborhood, and so
{\mathcal{D}}
is locally compact. If
Y
is a compact
subset of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U})
, as it is covered by neighborhoods
\mu_{N}(a^{\prime})
, it must be covered by finitely many such neighborhoods,

\mu_{N}(a_{1}),\cdots,\mu_{N}(a_{m})
;
and as the ‘centers’
a_{i}
are at finite distances from each other, it follows that
Y
is contained in a large enough ball.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
A\models{\mathcal{T}}
,
a\in A
. Assume:

(1)

Each ball
\mu_{n}(a)
is compact in the pp-topology.

(2)

For some
n
, if
\phi(x)
is a pp-formula in one variable then either
{\mathcal{T}}\models\neg\exists x\phi
, or
\phi
is realized in
\mu_{k}(a)
.

Then

A
is universal, i.e. every model of
{\mathcal{T}}
admits a homomorphism to
A
; and
{\mathcal{T}}
is pp-compact (ppC).

Proof.

To prove universality it suffices to show that any
B{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
admits a homomorphism into
A
. Let
b_{0}\in B
.
Then any finite number of pp formulas true of
b_{0}
are realized in
\mu_{k}(a)
; the sets of realizations are closed; hence by (1), they are realized
simultaneously, i.e. some
a_{0}\in\mu_{k}(a)
realizes the pp-type of
b_{0}
. Let
f
be a map from
C
into
A
preserving pp-formulas, with maximal
possible domain
C
containing
b_{0}
. To prove universality we have to show that
C=B
. Otherwise, let
b\in B\smallsetminus C
. Let
p
be the pp-type of
b
over
C
.
Then
p
ensures
\mu_{n}(x,b_{0})
for some
n
. By compactness of
\mu_{n+k}(a)
, as in the first step,
f(p)
is realized in
A
. This contradicts the maximality of
C
and
proves that
C=B
, and so
A
is universal.

Let us now prove ppC. Let
\phi(x,y)
be a local pp formula contradicting equality, as in the definition of ppC;
\phi(x,y)
implies
\mu_{m}(x,y)
. We may take
\phi
symmetric
(replacing it by
\phi(x,y)\vee\phi(y,x)
if necessary.)
Suppose there is no finite bound on the size
\phi
-cliques. Then by compactness there exists a weak model
B
of
{\mathcal{T}}
and an infinite
\phi
-clique in
B
; it must
be contained in a
\mu_{m}
-ball, and so we can take
B
to be a local model. Then
B
embeds into
A
, so in fact
A
contains an infinite
\phi
-clique within some ball

\mu_{n}(a)
. Extend it to a maximal
\phi
-clique
I\subset\mu_{n}(a)
. Then
\mu_{n}(c)
is covered by the open sets
\neg\phi(c,x)
, as
c
ranges over
I
. Hence
\mu_{n}(a)

is covered by a finite number of sets
\neg\phi(c,x)
, where now
c\in I_{0}
for some finite
I_{0}\subset I
. If
d\in I\setminus I_{0}
we have however
\phi(c,d)
for each

c\in I_{0}
. This contradiction proves that a bound exists.
∎

It follows from universality that
{\mathcal{T}}
has LJEP; of course, condition (2) really implies LJEP
k
.

2.5.
The pp topology on
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Aut}}}({\mathcal{U}})
.

Let
G={\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Aut}}}({\mathcal{U}})
. Give
G
the topology whose pre-basic open set have the form

\{g:\neg R(ga_{1},\ldots,ga_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})\}

where
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m}\in\mathcal{U}
and
R
is pp. The action
G\times\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
is then continuous in the first variable; for fixed
g
it is also continuous in the second variable, since
g
is an automorphism.
It is also easy to see that multiplication
G\times G\to G
is continuous in each variable, and that inversion
g\mapsto g^{-1}

is continuous.

As
\mathcal{U}
is T1, it follows from left-continuity of the action that
G
is T1.

Let
G(a,n)=\{g\in G:\mu_{n}(g(a),a)\}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

G(a,n)
is compact. Assuming (
{Loc}
),
G
is a locally compact space.

Proof.

Fix
a
and
n
. Let
u
be an ultrafilter on a set
I
, and let
g_{i}\in G(a,n)
,
i\in I
;
we need to find a limit point
g
of
(g_{i})_{i}
along
u
. Let
\mathcal{U}^{*}
be the ultrapower of
\mathcal{U}
along
u
,
and let
g_{*}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}^{*}
be the ultraproduct of the maps
g_{i}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}^{*}
. Since
\mu_{n}(g_{i}(a),a)
,
we have
\mu_{n}(g_{*}(a),a)
and hence
\mu_{n+k}(g_{*}(c),a)
whenever
\mu_{k}(c,a)
. Thus
g
is defined on
\mathcal{U}
into
the local ultrapower. Let
j
be the diagonal embedding

\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}^{*}
, ultrapower of
Id:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
.
As
\mathcal{U}^{*}\models{\mathcal{T}}^{\pm}
,
Proposition
2.1
provides a homomorphism
r:\mathcal{U}^{*}\to\mathcal{U}
with
r\circ j=Id_{\mathcal{U}}
. Let
g=r\circ g_{*}
.
Then
g\in End(\mathcal{U})={\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Aut}}}(\mathcal{U})
. If
R(g_{i}a_{1},\ldots,g_{i}a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})
holds for
u
-almost all
i\in I
,
then
\mathcal{U}^{*}\models R(g_{*}a_{1},\ldots,g_{*}a_{n},jb_{1},\ldots,jb_{m})
so
\mathcal{U}\models R(ga_{1},\ldots,ga_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})
.
Hence
g
is indeed a limit point of
(g_{i})_{i}
along
u
.

Assuming (
{Loc}
),
G
is locally compact: since translations are continuous, it suffices to show
that the identity element
1\in G
has a compact neighborhood. Indeed as in Lemma
2.3
, the compact set
G(a,N)
contains
an open set including
1
, namely the complement of
\{g:\mu_{>n}(a,g(a))\}
.
∎

Let
X
be a
G
-invariant subset of
{\mathcal{D}}
. Define the infinitesimal elements of
G
, in its action on
X
, to be

{{\mathfrak{g}}_{X}}=\{g\in G:\hbox{for every nonempty open }U\subset X,\ \ gU\cap U\neq\emptyset\}

Fix
X
to be either
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{U})
or a complete 0-definable pp type
P\subset{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{U})
; and
let
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{X},{\mathcal{G}}=G/{\mathfrak{g}},\pi:G\to{\mathcal{G}}
the quotient map.

By Lemma C.1 of
[
27
]
,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a closed normal subgroup of
G
, and
{\mathcal{G}}=G/{\mathfrak{g}}
is Hausdorff. Note that
G/{\mathfrak{g}}

remains locally compact, with continuous inversion and right and left multiplication.
It follows from Ellis’ joint continuity theorem
[
18
]

that
G/{\mathfrak{g}}
is a locally compact Hausdorff topological group, acting continuously
on
\mathcal{U}/{\mathfrak{g}}
. (A direct proof in our setting is given in
[
27
]
3.27.)

We have shown the main part of:

Proposition 2.7
.

Assume (ecB) and (
{Loc}
). Let
\mathcal{U}
be the universal e.c. model,
G=Aut(\mathcal{U})
,
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathcal{U}}
,
{\mathcal{G}}=G/{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Then
{\mathcal{G}}
is a locally compact topological group. With
\pi:G\to{\mathcal{G}}
the quotient map, we have:

•

If
Y\subset{\mathcal{G}}
is precompact, then
\pi^{-1}(Y)
is bounded.

•

If
W\subset G
is bounded, then
\pi W
is precompact.

Here
W\subset G
is said to be bounded if for some/any
a\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U})
, for some
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

Wa\subset\mu_{N}(a)
; see Definition
C.1

12
.

Proof.

We saw that
{\mathcal{G}}
is a locally compact topological group, and that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a closed normal subgroup of
G
.
Fix
a\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U})
.
By Lemma
2.3
,
a
has a compact neighborhood
W=\mu_{n}(a)
. Then by definition of
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
if
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}
then
gW\cap W\neq\emptyset
; so
\mu_{n}(a,gb)
holds for some
b
with
\mu_{n}(a,b)
;
hence
\mu_{n}(ga,gb)
, and
\mu_{2n}(a,ga)
. This shows that
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset G(a,2n)
, which is compact by Lemma
2.6
.
Thus
{\mathfrak{g}}
is compact. It follows easily
2
2

2

[Let
\pi:G\to{\mathcal{G}}
be the quotient map. Let
Y\subset G
be closed with
\pi(Y)
compact. Then
Y
is compact:
Let
y_{i}\in Y,i\in I
, and
u
an ultrafilter on
I
. The
\lim_{i\to u}\pi(y_{i})=\pi(b)
for some
b
.
Since
b{\mathfrak{g}}
is compact, it has a compact neighborhood
U=U{\mathfrak{g}}
; and we have
y_{i}\in U
for almost all
i
;
so
y_{i}
approaches a limit along
u
.]
that if
Y\subset{\mathcal{G}}
is compact, then so is the pullback to
G
. Thus
for the first item
it suffices to show that if
Y\subset G
is compact, then
Y\subset G(a,N)
for some
N
. Indeed as
Y
is compact,

Ya
is compact. By Lemma
2.3
,

Ya\subset\mu_{N}(a)
for some
N
;
hence by definition
Y\subset G(a,N)
.

If
W\subset G
is bounded,
W\subset G(a,N)
for some
N
; we saw that
G(a,N)
is compact; hence so is
\pi(G(a,N))
.
So
\pi(W)\subset\pi(G(a,N))
is precompact.

∎

Proposition 2.8
.

Assume (ecB) and (
{Loc}
); also that
{\mathcal{L}}
is countable. Let
P
be a complete pp type in
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{U})
,

{\mathcal{G}}(P)=G/{\mathfrak{g}}_{P}
. Then
{\mathcal{G}}(P)

is a second countable, locally compact topological group. If
P^{\prime}
is another complete pp type in
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{U})
,
then
{\mathcal{G}}(P^{\prime}),{\mathcal{G}}(P)
differ only by a compact isogeny.

Proof.

We have the natural homomorphism
G\to{\mathcal{G}}(P)
, by restriction and then going to the quotient. It is clearly continuous, when

{\mathcal{G}}(P)
is endowed with the topology described above, with respect to the action of
G
on
P
.
The kernel is compact, indeed with
n
as in the proof of Proposition
2.7
, for any
a\in P
, the kernel is contained
in
G(a,n)
. It follows that
{\mathcal{G}}(P)
is locally compact, and (regardless of
P
) differs
by a compact isogeny from
{\mathcal{G}}
. When
{\mathcal{L}}
is countable, the proof of
[
27
]
3.24 shows that
{\mathcal{G}}(P)
is second countable. (using
\sigma
-compactness
via
G=\cup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}G(a,n)
in place of compactness; note that Lemma C.4 remains valid when
{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
is

\sigma
-compact, and
X\times Y
is
{\mathfrak{t}}_{2}
-compact for
{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
-compact
X,Y
, as it the case here.)
∎

3.
The core space

Let
T
be a complete first-order theory, in a language
L
with a distinguished sort
{D}
and locality relation on
{D}
, that we take to be generated by a single reflexive, symmetric binary relation
{m_{1}}
on
{D}
.
Let
{d_{m}}(x,y)
be the Cayley-graph distance formed from
{m_{1}}
, as in the discussion of local logic. A model of
T
will be said to be
{m}
-local

if any two elements of
{D}
are at finite
{d_{m}}
- distance.
We treat
T
in local logic. This means, first of all, that we
restrict attention to
{m}
-local models
M
of
T
, and in particular to the part of the type space consisting of types realized in elementary extensions that are themesleves
{m}
-local. If a weak model
N^{\prime}
is an elementary extension of
M

in the usual sense, and
N
is obtained by simply removing any points of
{D}(N^{\prime})
at infinite distance from points of
{D}(M)
,
then by fiat,
N
is a (local) elementary extension of
T
in the local logic sense.

We will use only local formulas, i.e. formulas built up using local quantifers only; with the one exception that we assume
T
is complete
with respect to sentences
(\forall x)\phi
and
(\exists x)\phi
, where
\phi
is local.

3
3

3

We can assume or arrange by Morleyzation that
T
admits local quantifier-elimination. i.e. every local formula
is equivalent to a quantifier-free one. In this case
T
is determined by its universal part; the models of
T
are e.c. models of
T_{\forall}
. In
[
27
]
it was useful to allow more general universal theories, restricting attention to e.c. models; this could be done here too.
.
Note that the ’local’ restriction on types
p(x)\in S_{D}(M)
amounts simply to saying that some formula
m_{n}(x,b)
be represented in
p
. Following convention in enriched logics, we denote the usual quantifier-free type
space by
S^{weak}(M)
; and the subspace of local types by
S(M)
.

For simplicity we
will concentrate on the space of local
1
-types in
{D}
,
S_{D}
.

S_{{D}}(M)
is an open subset of
S_{D}^{weak}(M)

with the usual topology; hence it is locally compact.

3.1.
The pattern language

Let us recall the construction in
[
27
]
of the pattern language
{\mathcal{L}}
, and
the
{\mathcal{L}}
-structure on the type spaces

S_{x}(M)
,
M\models T
. Here we
concentrate on a single sort
{D}
of
T
, and will be interested only in one sort of
{\mathcal{L}}
, to be denoted
\mathcal{D}
, corresponding to local
1
-types on
{D}
. Let
x,x_{i}
be variables
ranging over
{D}
.
Let
y
be an additional tuple of variables (the
parameter variables
.)
Let
t=(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n};\alpha)
be
an
n
-tuple of formulas
\phi_{i}(x_{i},y)
, and let
\alpha(y)
be a formula.
To each such
t
we associate a relation symbol
{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}
of
{{\mathcal{L}}}
, taking variables
(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})
ranging over
\mathcal{D}
.
For any
M\models T
, we define an
{{\mathcal{L}}}
-structure with universe
S=S_{D}(M)
.
The interpretation of
{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}
is:

{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}^{S}=\{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in S:(\forall a\in\alpha(M))\bigvee_{i\leq n}(\phi_{i}(x,a)\in p_{i})\}

Thus when
n=1
,
{\mathsf{Def}}_{\phi;\alpha}
indicates a partial definition scheme for
\phi
, thus the notation
{\mathsf{Def}}
.
(In
[
27
]
,
Om_{\neg\phi;\alpha}
was used instead, where
Om
stands for
omitting

\neg\phi
.
These are equivalent in the presence of negation in
L
, but even then
{\mathsf{Def}}
is more natural.)
For typographical reasons, we will sometimes write
{\mathsf{Def}}[t]
for
{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}
.

3.2.
Locality structure on
\mathcal{D}

Definition 3.3
.

A model
M
of
T
is
orbit-bounded
(in the sort
{D}
) if for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
for all
a,b\in{{D}}(M)
, there exists
\alpha\in Aut(M)
such that
a,\alpha(b)
are at distance
\leq k
.

We will make two assumptions about
T
as a local theory.

(OB)
T
has an orbit-bounded model
M
.

(DP) For each
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
, for some
m=m(r)\in{\mathbb{N}}
, every
r+1
-ball is a union of at most
m

r
-balls.

The second assumption, (DP), is the
doubling
property for the metric
d_{m}
. Assuming
any two points are connected by a finite chain of points at distance
1
(i.e. the locality is generated by a single relation),
(DP) follows from the same statement for
r=1
.
If it holds for one ball in an orbit-bounded model, then
the uniform version will be valid too.

(DP) is needed in order to define the locality structure for the pattern language, by a pp formula.
It follows from doubling that every
n
-ball is a union of finitely many
1
-balls; hence
a type
p
of
{D}
over a model
M
is local iff it represents the formula
m(x,y)
, i.e.
m(x,c)\in p
for some
c\in{D}(M)
.
( In other words we will not have
{\mathsf{Def}}[{\neg m(x,y);D(y)}]
.)

The terminology orbit-bounded is short for ‘the space of orbits of
Aut(M)
on
M
is bounded, under the induced metric’.
In our applications, (OB) will hold in every local model of
T
. In general, if
T
has one orbit-bounded model, then
every
\aleph_{0}
-homogneous model will be orbit-bounded.

Definition 3.4
.

We define the locality generating relation
\mu_{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
for
{\mathcal{L}}
to be

{\mathsf{Def}}[{\neg m_{1}(x_{1},y_{1}),\neg m_{1}(x_{2},y_{2});\neg m_{5}(y_{1},y_{2})}]

Towards (
{Loc}
), we also define
\mu_{>1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
by the formula:

{\mathsf{Def}}[{\neg m_{1}(x_{1},y_{1}),\neg m_{1}(x_{2},y_{2});m_{5}(y_{1},y_{2})}]

(We used
5
above, where
3
would do, in order to ensure below that close Lascar neighbors have
\mu
-distance at most
1
;
it is not an essential point.)

For the space of types over a model
M
, if
p_{1},p_{2}\in S(M)
are local types, and
S(M)\models\mu_{1}(p_{1},p_{2})
,
by (DP) there exist
b_{1},b_{2}\in D(M)

with
m_{1}(x_{i},b_{i})\in p_{i}
; now
\mu_{1}(p_{1},p_{2})
guarantees that
m_{5}(b_{1},b_{2})
holds, so that any two realizations
of
p_{1},p_{2}
will be at
m
-distance at most
7
.

Conversely if realizations
b_{i}
of
p_{i}
exist with
m_{1}(b_{1},b_{2})
,
then
\mu_{1}(p_{1},p_{2})
holds (even with
3
in place of
5
): we have to show that if
M\models\neg m_{5}(c_{1},c_{2})

then
\neg m_{1}(x_{1},c_{1})\in p_{1}
or
\neg m_{2}(x_{1},c_{2})\in p_{2}
. Otherwise,
m_{1}(x_{1},c_{i})\in p_{i}
so
m_{1}(b_{i},c_{i})
.
Then (initially in an elementary extension of
M
) the chain
c_{1},b_{2},b_{2},c_{2}
shows that
m_{3}(c_{1},c_{2})
holds,
a contradiction.

Lemma 3.5
.

Assume
M
is orbit-bounded; then

S_{D}(M)
is orbit-bounded too, even just with respect to the action of
Aut(M)
.

Proof.

Let
p,q\in S_{{D}}(M)
.
Since
p(x)
is local, for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
p(x)
contains a formula defining an
m
-ball of radius
n
. By the doubling property (DP) it follows that
p(x)
contains a formula defining an
m
-ball of radius
1
; i.e.

m_{1}(x,c)\in p
for some
c\in M
. Similarly
m_{1}(x,d)\in q
for some
d\in M
. Let
\sigma\in Aut(M)
be such that

m(d,\sigma(c))\leq k
. Then
\sigma
induces an automorphism of
S(M)
, and
m_{1}(x,\sigma(c))\in\sigma(p)
.
Let
d=d_{1},\cdots,d_{k}=\sigma(c)
be a chain of elements of
D(M)
, with
m_{1}(d_{i},d_{i+1})
. (Say
k
is even.)
Also write
d_{i}
for the algebraic type
x=d_{i}
. We have
\mu_{1}(q,d_{2}),\mu_{1}(d_{2},d_{4}),\cdots,\mu_{1}(d_{k-2},d_{k}),\mu_{1}(d_{k},\sigma(p))
. Thus
\mu_{k/2+1}(\sigma(p),q)
holds in
S(M)
. ∎

3.6.
The pattern theory of
T

We define
{{\mathcal{T}}}
be the set of local, primitive universal
{\mathcal{L}}
-sentences true in
S(M)
for some
M\models T
.
Defined this way, it is not clear that
{{\mathcal{T}}}
is consistent; but this, along with LJEP, follows from Lemma
3.7
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
M
be an orbit-bounded model of
T
. Then
S(M)\models{{\mathcal{T}}}
.

Proof.

Let
N\models T
. If
S(M)\models(\exists x_{1})\cdots(\exists x_{n})\Theta
, where
\Theta
is a finite conjunction of basic relations of
{\mathcal{L}}
, we have to show that
S(N)\models(\exists x_{1})\cdots(\exists x_{n})\Theta
. For simplicity, let us assume

\Theta
is a basic relation
{\mathsf{Def}}[{\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n};\alpha}]
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in S(M)
be such that
S(M)\models\Theta(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
.
Since
M
and
S(M)
are local, each
p_{i}
is at some finite
m
-distance
d

from some
a\in M
, i.e.
m_{d}(a,x_{i})\in p_{i}
. Thus the following partial type over
M
is consistent:

\{\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}(x_{i},c):c\in\alpha(M)\}\cup\{m_{d}(a,x_{i}):i=1,\ldots,n\}

Hence, for any
\sigma\in Aut(M)
, the same partial type is consistent if we replace
a
by
\sigma(a)
. By the orbit boundedness
of
M
, it follows that for
any

a^{\prime}\in D(M)
, the type below is consistent too:

\{\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}(x_{i},c):c\in\alpha(M)\cup\{m_{d+k}(a^{\prime},x_{i}):i=1,\ldots,n\}

Thus for any
m
, we have in
M
:

(\forall u\in{D})(\forall c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})(\bigwedge_{j}\alpha(c_{j})\to(\exists x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\bigwedge_{i}m_{d+k}(u,x_{i})\wedge\bigwedge_{j}(\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}(x_{i},c_{j})))

This applies equally to
N
(using the completeness of
T
), and so retracing out steps, we find that for any
b^{\prime}\in N
there exist
types
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
with
\mu_{d+k}(b^{\prime},x_{i})\in q_{i}
and
S(N)\models{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})
. The
q_{i}
are local types,
and show that
S(N)\models(\exists x_{1})\cdots(\exists x_{n})\Theta
, as promised.

∎

Regarding (
{Loc}
), clearly
\mu_{1},\mu_{>1}
are incompatible in any local type space. On the other hand, if
p_{1},p_{2}\in S(M)
, then either
\mu_{>1}
holds or
m_{i}(x_{i},b_{i})\in p_{i}
for some

b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(M)
with
m_{5}(b_{1},b_{2})
. Then as in the proof of Lemma
3.5
we have
\mu_{4}(p_{1},p_{2})
.
Thus
S(M)\models(\forall\xi)(\mu_{>1}(\xi)\vee\mu_{4}(\xi))
, so (say by Lemma
3.8
)

{{\mathcal{T}}}{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}(\forall\xi)(\mu_{>1}(\xi)\vee\mu_{4}(\xi))
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
M\models T
be orbit-bounded.
A\models{{\mathcal{T}}}
. Then there exists a homomorphism
f:A\to S(M)
. In particular any
e.c. model of
{{\mathcal{T}}}
is isomorphic to a substructure of
S(M)
.

{{\mathcal{T}}}
has the properties (ecB) and (
{Loc}
).

Proof.

We have to check that
the usual (locally compact) type space topology of
S_{D}(M)
satisfies the hyptheses of Lemma
2.4
. (2) there was already
proved in Lemma
3.5
.

The interpretation of
R={\mathsf{Def}}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n};\alpha]
is closed, since if
p=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\notin R
,
then
\neg\phi_{i}(x_{i},c)\in p_{i}
for some
c\in\alpha(M)
; the set of all tuples
q=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})
with
\neg\phi_{i}(x_{i},c)\in q_{i}

is an open neighborhood of
p
in the product topology, disjoint from
R
.

As for compactness of
\mu_{n}(p)
, where
p\in S_{D}(M)
: if
q\in\mu_{n}(p)
, there exists a chain

p=p_{0},\cdots,p_{n}=q
with
\mu_{1}(p_{i},p_{i+1})
. Pick
a_{i}\in D(M)
with
m_{1}(x,a_{i})\in p_{i}
. Then

m_{5}(a_{i},a_{i+1})
, so
m_{1+5n}(x,a_{0})\in q
. The types
q
with
m_{1+5n}(x,a_{0})\in q
form
a compact set
W
in the usual topology on the weak type space, which is entirely contained in the space of local types;
hence
W
is compact. Now
\mu_{n}(p)
is a subset of
W
defined by a pp relation, hence is closed in
W
and thus
it is compact too.

∎

Proposition
2.1
and Proposition
2.7
are thus valid for
{{\mathcal{T}}}
. We call
\mathcal{U}
the core space of
T
. To summarize:

Let
T
be a local theory in the sort
D
, complete (with respect to local formulas),
and satisfying (DP) and (OB).
We have defined the
pattern language
{\mathcal{L}}
,
a local primitive universal theory in the sort
\mathcal{D}
, valid in
S_{D}(M)

when
M\models T
.

Definition 3.9
.

A
core
for (type spaces of models of)
T
is an
{\mathcal{L}}
-structure
J
such that:

•

For any orbit-bounded
M\models T
, there exists an
{\mathcal{L}}
- embedding
j:J\to S(M)
.

•

For any such
j
, there exists a homomorphism
r:S(M)\to J

such that
r\circ j=Id_{J}
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
T
be a complete local theory in the sort
D
.
Assume
T
has an orbit-bounded model, where every
2
-ball is contained in finitely many
1
-balls. Then:

(1)

A core exists and is unique up to isomorphism. We denote it
\mathcal{J}={\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Core\,}}}(T)
.

(2)

Reflection: let
\Theta
be a
universal sentence
(\forall p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})R
, where
R
is an arbitrary Boolean combination of local pp formulas in the pattern
language
\mathcal{L}
. Let
M
be an orbit-bounded model of
T
.
If
S(M)\models\Theta
then
\mathcal{J}\models\Theta
.

(3)

Quantifier elimination:

\mathcal{L}
-atomic types in
\mathcal{J}
are orbits of
Aut(\mathcal{J})
. Every pp formula is equivalent
(in any
S(M)
as well as in
\mathcal{J}
)
to a certain conjunction of atomic formulas.

(4)

Aut(\mathcal{J})
has a natural locally compact topology:

W(R;a,b):=\{g:{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}(ga_{1},\ldots,ga_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})\}

is a basic closed set. The canonical Hausdorff quotient
{\mathcal{G}}(T):=Aut(\mathcal{J})/{\mathfrak{g}}
is a locally compact topological group.

We have just proved (1) and (4). By definition of a core, there exists an
{\mathcal{L}}
- embedding
j:\mathcal{J}\to S(M)
,
as well as
r:S(M)\to\mathcal{J}
with
r\circ j=Id_{\mathcal{J}}
. It follows that the truth value of any pp
\psi
formula is preserved by
j
;
i.e.
\mathcal{J}\models\psi(a)
if and only if
S(M)\models\psi(j(a))
. Hence for an arbitrary Boolean combinations
\Psi
of pp formulas,
if
S(M)\models(\forall x)\Psi
, then
S(M)\models\Psi(j(a))
so
\mathcal{J}\models\Psi(a)
, this for any
a
from
\mathcal{J}
.
This proves (2). For (3) see
[
27
]
, 3.12 (1).

3.11.
The local Lascar-Shelah relation

We continue to assume
T
has an orbit-bounded model
M
, with bound
k
, and that doubling (DP) holds; and we concentrate
on a single sort
D
.

A
definable family of finite local partitions
is a pair of formulas
(B,E)
, with
B
a non-empty 0-definable subset of
D^{n}
for some
n\geq 1
, and

E(x,y;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\subset D^{2}\times B
, such that for some
f\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with
f
strictly above the radius
k

of
M/Aut(M)
we have:

(1)

E
implies
m_{f}(x,z_{1})
and
m_{f}(y,z_{1})
.

(2)

B
implies
m_{f}(z_{1},z_{i})
for each
i\leq n
.

(3)

For any
b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in B
,
E_{b}(x,y):=E(x,y;b)
defines an equivalence relation on
m_{f}(b_{1})
, with at most
f
classes.

Two elements
a,a^{\prime}\in D
are
Lascar-Shelah neighbors
, denoted
a{\vartriangle}a^{\prime}
, if for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, every non-empty 0-definable set
B\subset D^{n}
, and every definable family
E\subset D^{2}\times B
of finite local partitions,

M\models(\exists z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})E(a,a^{\prime},z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}).

If this holds for a single
E
, we will say that they are Lascar-Shelah neighbors with respect to
E
.
The notation is intended to suggest an isosceles triangle, with
a
and
b
at the distinct endpoints of the equal sides.
We will nevertheless sometimes write
\vartriangle(x,y)
for
x\vartriangle y
.

We call two (local) types
p_{1},p_{2}
over a model
N

Lascar neighbors
if
there exist
a_{i}\models p_{i}
such that
a_{1},a_{2}
are Lascar neighbors, i.e. if
p_{1}(x_{1})\cup p_{2}(x_{2})\cup{\vartriangle}(x_{1},x_{2})

is consistent.

Call
p_{1},p_{2}

close neighbors
if for every definable family of finite local partitions

(E_{b})_{b\in B}
, for some
b\in B(N)
and
d^{\prime}\in D(N)
,
x_{i}E_{b}d^{\prime}\in p_{i}
. This implies that
p_{1}\cup p_{2}\models{\vartriangle}(x_{1},x_{2})
, and
in particular
p_{1},p_{2}
are neighbors.

If
p_{i},p_{i+1}
are Lascar neighbors for
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, we will say that
p_{1},p_{n}
are at Lascar distance at most
n
, and write
\vartriangle^{n}(p_{1},p_{n})
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
M
be an orbit-bounded model of
T
,
a,a^{\prime}\in D(M)
. Then
a\vartriangle a^{\prime}
iff for some elementary extension
M^{\prime}
of
M
,
and elementary submodel
N\prec M^{\prime}
,
N\cong M
, we have
tp(a/N)=tp(a^{\prime}/N)
. (Here by convention
M^{\prime}
is local, and
tp
refers
to the quantifier-free type.)

Also,
a{\vartriangle}a^{\prime}
implies
m(x,y)\leq 2
.

Proof.

Assume
tp(a/N)=tp(a^{\prime}/N)
. We have
D(N)\neq\emptyset
; pick
d\in D(N)
. Since
M^{\prime}
is local,
M^{\prime}\models m_{l}(a,d),m_{l}(a^{\prime},d)
for some
l
. By (DP),

m_{l}(d)
is a finite union of balls
m_{1}(c_{i})
,
c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}\in D(N)
. So
m_{1}(a,c)
holds for some
c=c_{j}\in D(N)
.
Since
tp(a/N)=tp(a^{\prime}/N)
, we have
m_{1}(a^{\prime},c)
too. This proves that
m(x,y)\leq 2
.

Now let
(B,E)
be a definable family of finite local partitions.

As
B\neq\emptyset
, and using (OB),
there exists
b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in B(N)
with
m(c,b_{1})\leq k
. Thus
m_{k+1}(a,b_{1})
and
m_{k+1}(a^{\prime},b_{1})

hold. Since
E_{b}
defines a finite partition of
m_{f}(b_{1})
and
f\geq k+1
, and using that
N
is a model and

tp(a/N)=tp(a^{\prime}/N)
, we have
E(a,a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
. This proves that
a\vartriangle a^{\prime}
.

Along the way we saw that
m(a,a^{\prime})\leq 2
.

Conversely, assume
a\vartriangle a^{\prime}
. Let
y_{c}:c\in M
be variables, and let
\Delta
be the diagram of
M
,
i.e. the set of
L
-local formulas in the variables
y_{c}
that become true in
M
under the assignment
y_{c}\mapsto c
.
Fix one of the variables
y_{c}
, and call it
y_{1}
.
Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be the set of all local formulas
\phi(x,y)\iff\phi(x^{\prime},y)
, where the
y
are some of the
y_{c}
, including
y_{1}
, and
\phi
is a formula of
L
.
Since
a\vartriangle a^{\prime}
, it is easy to see that
T\cup\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}\cup\{m_{k+1}(a,y_{1}),m_{k+1}(a^{\prime},y_{1})\}
is consistent. Hence this set of formulas is realized
in some weak model, and restricting to elements at finite distance from
a
and
a^{\prime}
, it is realized in some model.
This leads to
N\cong M
with
N,M\prec M^{\prime}
for some
M^{\prime}\succ M
.
∎

3.13.
The neighbor relation in the pattern language

Lemma 3.14
.

(1)

(
{\mathcal{L}}
-
\bigwedge
-definability of
{\vartriangle}
.) There exist atomic formulas
\psi_{m}
of
{\mathcal{L}}
such that for any
N\models T
, and any
p,q\in S(N)
,
p{\vartriangle}q
iff for each
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
S(N)\models\psi_{m}(p,q)
.

(2)

The close neighbor relation on
S(M)
is an intersection of
\leq|L|
open sets in the pp topology.

(3)

Let
\rho:S(N)\to S(N)
be a retraction, i.e. a homomorphism with
\rho^{2}=\rho
. Let
p\in S(N)
.
Then
p,\rho(p)
are close Lascar neighbors.

(4)

Assume
T
is orbit-bounded. Let
J
be a copy of
\mathcal{J}
in
S(M)
, and assume
p,q\in J
cannot be separated by disjoint open subsets of
J
.
Then
p,q
are at Lascar distance at most
2
.

Proof.

(1) Let
p,q\in S(N)
. If
p{\vartriangle}q
then for every definable family
(B,E)
of finite local partitions such that

T\models\neg(\exists x,x^{\prime},u,y)(\phi(x,u)\wedge\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime},u)\wedge\alpha(u)\wedge B(y)\wedge E(x,x^{\prime},y))

we have

S(N)\models{\mathsf{Def}}[\neg\phi,\neg\phi^{\prime};\alpha](p,q)

Conversely, if
(p,q)\notin\vartriangle
, then
p(x)\cup q(x^{\prime})
(a set of formulas of
L(M)
, including the diagram of
M
) must be inconsistent with the following set of formulas:

\{(\exists y)(B(y)\wedge E(x,x^{\prime},y)):(B,E)\}

where
(B,E)
ranges over all definable families of finite local partitions,
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
. Note that these formulas imply a bounded distance
between
x,x^{\prime}
and each
y_{i}
, and between them and the elements of
M
(the latter because
p,q
are local types.)
By compactness, for some tuple
c
from
N
,
\phi(x,c)\in p,\phi(x^{\prime},c)\in p^{\prime}
,
\alpha\in tp_{N}(c)
, and for some finite set
(B_{j},E_{j})
of definable families of finite local partitions,

T\models\neg(\exists x,x^{\prime},u,y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})(\phi(x,u)\wedge\phi(x^{\prime},u)\wedge\alpha(u)\wedge\bigwedge_{j}B_{j}(y_{j})\wedge E_{j}(x,x^{\prime},y_{j}))

Let
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})
,
B(y)=\bigwedge_{j}B_{j}(y_{j})
,
E(x,x^{\prime},y)=\bigwedge_{j}E_{j}(x,x^{\prime},y_{j})
.
Then

T\models\neg(\exists x,x^{\prime},u,y)(\phi(x,u)\wedge\phi(x^{\prime},u)\wedge\alpha(u)\wedge\bigwedge B(y)\wedge E(x,x^{\prime},y))

yet
c
shows that
S(N)
does not omit
\phi,\phi^{\prime},\alpha
. Thus

{\mathsf{Def}}[\neg\phi,\neg\phi^{\prime};\alpha](p,q)
holds for each
\phi,\phi^{\prime},\alpha
as above iff
p{\vartriangle}q
.

(2) Let
q=\rho(p)
. Let
\bar{E}=(E_{b})_{b\in B}
be a definable family of local finite partitions.
Consider the atomic formula
\psi=\psi_{\bar{E}}
,

\psi(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})={\mathsf{Def}}[\neg x_{1}E_{y}u,\neg x_{2}E_{y}u;B(y)\wedge D(u)]

It is clear that
p,q\in S(M)
are close Lascar neighbors with respect to
\bar{E}

iff
S(M)\models\neg\psi(p,q)
. And
p,q\in S(M)
close Lascar neighbors iff they are
close Lascar neighbors with respect to each
\bar{E}
.

(3) Since a realization of
q

must be
E_{b}
-equivalent to some
d^{\prime}\in N
(as there are only finitely many classes, all represented in
N
),

S(N)\models\neg\psi(q,q)
. Now if we had
S(N)\models\psi(p,q)
, applying the
homomorphism
\rho
would give
\psi(q,q)
, which we have just ruled out; so
S(N)\models\neg\psi(p,q)
.
This means that for some
b\in B(N)
and
d^{\prime}\in D(N)
,
x_{1}E_{b}d^{\prime}\in p
and
x_{2}E_{b}d^{\prime}\in q
. As this holds for all definable families of finite partitions,
p,\rho(p)
are close neighbors.

(4) By Lemma
3.8
,
{\mathcal{T}}
has Loc.
Let
N
be a bounded open set containing both
p
and
q
.
By compactness, it suffices to show that for each definable family
\bar{E}
of local finite partitions,
there exists
r\in cl(N)
that is a Lascar neighbor to
p
and to
q
with respect to
\bar{E}
. In fact we will show
that
p,q
have a common close Lascar neighbor in
N
, with respect to
\bar{E}
. By (2), the close neighbor
relation with respect to
\bar{E}
is pp-open. Let
N^{\prime}
the
\bar{E}
-close neighbors of
p
within
N
;
then
N^{\prime}
is open and
p\in N^{\prime}
. Similarly let
N^{\prime\prime}
be the set of
\bar{E}
-close neighbors of
p
within
N
, an open neighborhood of
q
. Since
p,q
cannot be separated we have
N^{\prime}\cap N^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Any

r\in N^{\prime}\cap N^{\prime\prime}
is a common close Lascar neighbor of
p,q
in
N
.

∎

Note that the
\psi_{m}
define the same relation on
S(N)
and on any retract, notably any copy
J
of
\mathcal{J}
.
We will refer to the relation they define on any model of
{\mathcal{T}}
as the Lascar neighbor relation, and denote it by
\vartriangle
.
Later we will use the same symbol on
\mathcal{J}
and on
Aut(\mathcal{J})
and
Aut(\mathcal{J})/{\mathfrak{g}}
; when the need arises to clarify, we will use a supersript, such as
\vartriangle^{\mathcal{J}}
.

3.15.
Automorphisms of models and pattern automorphisms

In this section,
M,N
will denote orbit-bounded (local) models of
T
.
We say that a subset
W
of
Aut(M)
is
bounded
if for some
a\in D(M)
,
Wa:=\{w(a):w\in W\}
is
contained in a
d_{m}
-ball of finite radius. Equivalently, for
all

a\in D(M)
,
Wa
has finite radius. (Definition
C.1

12
.)

By Lemma
3.14
(1),
\vartriangle
is defined by a conjunction
\Psi
of atomic sentences in any
S(N)
,
N
an orbit-bounded model of
T
.
We define
\vartriangle
by the same formula on
\mathcal{J}
. Since
\mathcal{J}
embeds into
S(N)
, this does not depend on the choice of
\Psi
.

On
G=Aut(\mathcal{J})
, we let

\vartriangle=\vartriangle^{Aut(\mathcal{J})}=\{g\in Aut(\mathcal{J}):(\forall a\in\mathcal{J})(a,g(a))\in\vartriangle^{\mathcal{J}}\}

Let
\vartriangle^{{\mathcal{G}}}
be the image of
\vartriangle^{Aut(\mathcal{J})}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

\vartriangle=\vartriangle^{{\mathcal{G}}}
is a normal
4
4

4

I.e. conjugation-invariant. See Definition
C.1
for group-theoretic terminology.
,
symmetric, compact subset of
{\mathcal{G}}
. This follows from the same assertion in
Aut(\mathcal{J})
. In
Aut(\mathcal{J})
we saw that
the
2
-ball is compact and contains
\vartriangle
as a closed subset.

Theorem 3.16
.

Assume (DP). Let
M
be an orbit-bounded model of
T
.

\iota:\mathcal{J}\to S(M)
an embedding,
and
\rho:S(M)\to\mathcal{J}
a retraction.
Then there exists a canonical quasi-homomorphism
{\varphi}:Aut(M)\to({\mathcal{G}}(T):{\vartriangle})
. We have:

(1)

When
C\subset{\mathcal{G}}
is precompact,
{\varphi}^{-1}(C)
is bounded in
Aut(M)
.

(2)

When
W\subset Aut(M)
is bounded,
{\varphi}(W)
is precompact in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proof.

Let
J=\iota(\mathcal{J})
, and identify
J
with
\mathcal{J}
; so that
\iota
is the inclusion
map
J\to S(M)
, and
\rho|J=Id_{J}
.
The definitions of
{\vartriangle}
on
J
and on
S(M)
are compatible, via
\iota
and
\rho
.

For
g\in Aut(M)
, define

{\phi}(g)=\rho\circ g\circ\iota\in Aut(\mathcal{J})

And let
{\varphi}=\pi\circ\phi
, where
\pi:Aut(\mathcal{J})\to{\mathcal{G}}
is the quotient map. Since
\pi

is a continuous and proper homomorphism, it suffices to prove the quasi-homomorphism property and (1,2) for
\phi
.

We have
{\phi}(Id_{M})=Id_{J}
since
\rho\circ\iota=Id_{J}
. We have to show, for
g,h\in Aut(M)
,
that
{\phi}(gh)\in{\phi}(g){\phi}(h){\vartriangle}
. For any
q\in S(M)
, we have
\rho(q){\vartriangle}q
in
S(M)
by Lemma
3.14
(3).

Thus

\rho h(q){\vartriangle}h(q)

As
\rho g:S(M)\to\mathcal{J}
is an
{\mathcal{L}}
-homomorphism,

\rho g\rho h(q){\vartriangle}\rho gh(q)
; in particular, for
p\in\mathcal{J}
,
\rho g\rho h\iota(p){\vartriangle}\rho gh\iota(p)
;
so

\rho g\rho h\iota{\vartriangle}^{Aut(\mathcal{J})}\rho gh\iota

i.e. (using
\iota\rho=\rho
)
{\phi}(g){\phi}(h){\vartriangle}{\phi}(gh)
. Since
End(J)=Aut(J)
, we can write
{\phi}(gh)={\phi}(g){\phi}(h)k

for some
k\in Aut(J)
, and we have
{\phi}(g){\phi}(h)k{\vartriangle}{\phi}(g){\phi}(h)
. Applying the
{\mathcal{L}}
-endomorphism

({\phi}(g){\phi}(h))^{-1}
we see that
k\in{\vartriangle}^{Aut(J)}
.

This shows that
{\phi}:Aut(M)\to Aut(\mathcal{J}):{\vartriangle}
is a quasi-homomorphism.
Composing with the Hausdorffization map
\pi:Aut(\mathcal{J})\to{\mathcal{G}}(T)
, we see that
\phi:{\mathcal{G}}(T):{\vartriangle}

is also a quasi-homomorphism.

(1) In view of Proposition
2.7
, it suffices to prove now that if
C\subset G=Aut(\mathcal{J})
is bounded with respect to the action on
(\mathcal{J},\mu)
, then
W:=\phi^{-1}(C)
is bounded with respect to the action on
D(M)
. Identifying
\mathcal{J}

with a subset
J
of
S(M)
, the retraction
\rho
shows every point of
S(M)
is at distance at most
1

from a point of
J
; moreover
since
\rho
changes distances by at most
1
, it is clear that
W
is bounded with respect
to the action on
S(M)
. On the other hand, letting
i(a)
denote the algebraic type
x=a
,
we have an
Aut(M)
-invariant embedding
i:M\to S(M)
, and again we saw
using (DP) that
any element of
S(M)
is at bounded distance from some element of
i(M)
. So if
a\in M
, then

Wi(a)
has finite radius with respect to
\mu
. But immediately following
Definition
3.4
, we saw that for
c,d\in i(M)
, the
m
-distance
m(c,d)
is at most
7\mu(i(c),i(d))
. Thus
Wa

has finite radius with respect to
m
, and so
W
is bounded with respect to the action on
D(M)
.

(2) Similarly, if
W\subset Aut(M)
is bounded with respect to the action on
(M,m)
, then it is bounded with respect to the induced action on types (to check boundedness we may choose a "center"
a
lying in
M
.) It follows as in (1) that the set
\rho\circ W

has bounded action on
J
; so the image in
{\mathcal{G}}
is precompact by Proposition
2.7
. ∎

Remark 3.17
.

If we introduce new sorts
\mathcal{D}_{n}
corresponding to
D^{n}
, and define
\vartriangle_{!}^{Aut(\mathcal{J})}
to be the set of automorphisms
\gamma
of
\mathcal{J}
(on all these sorts) with

\gamma(a)\vartriangle a
for any
a\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
, the proof still shows that
{\phi}:Aut(M)\to(Aut(\mathcal{J}):{\vartriangle_{!}})
.
