As we will have no immediate use for this apparent improvement, we will stick with
\vartriangle
.

Remark 3.18
.

In the various constructions of Theorem
3.16
,
we used the
fact
that the notion of locality is generated by a definable relation
m
, and we used the notion of finite distance
in defining the notion of model and of type space. But
m
itself was not further used. Thus replacing
m
by another relation

m^{\prime}
, where
m_{1}^{\prime}(x,y)
implies
m_{k}(x,y)
for some
k
, and vice versa, will change nothing: the class of models,
the definition of the local type space, of the langauge
\mathcal{L}
, of
\mathcal{J}
, the notion of an embedding and a retraction between
\mathcal{J}

and
S(M)
, and the maps
\phi
and
\varphi
, all remain the same.

3.19.
Choices

The homomorphism of Theorem
3.16
depends not only on
M
, but also on
\iota
and
\rho
.
Letting
\Sigma=S(M)
,
the relevant spaces are
Hom(\mathcal{J},\Sigma)
(for choosing
\iota
), and for
\iota\in Hom(\mathcal{J},\Sigma)
,
J=\iota(\mathcal{J})
, the space of retractions
Hom_{J}(\Sigma,J)
. Their study is of considerable interest; in part, I think they are relevant to
Gromov’s questions about symmetry breaking in Ramsey theory. Here we will only mention a
degree of canonicity that is nevertheless present, despite the choices. This will not be relevant to the main applications
to approximate subgroups and lattices.

As discussed in
[
27
]
, the choice of
\iota
amounts to expanding
M
to a model of a certain universal theory
T_{{}^{def}}
.

T_{{}^{def}}
is no longer a local theory; it has a relation for each element of
\mathcal{J}
, and the action of
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Aut}}}(\mathcal{J})

passes from the models to the langauge itself.

Remark 3.20
.

\mathcal{J}
is itself independent of the choices, and the
quasi-homomorphism
Aut(M)\to\mathcal{J}
(for a saturated
M
) can be made as unique as the saturated model itself:
in an appropriate model of set theory, we fix a cardinal
\kappa\geq L_{{}^{def}}
with
2^{\kappa}=\kappa^{+}
, and let
{\mathsf{M}}_{{}^{def}}
be
an existentially closed model of
T_{{}^{def}}
, realizing every qf type over an existentially closed substructure of cardinality

\leq\kappa
. It was shown in
[
27
]
(see 3.17) that
T_{{{}^{def}}}
is an irreducible universal theory, i.e. the joint embedding property holds; this continues to apply sortwise; so
{\mathsf{M}}_{{}^{def}}
is unique up to isomorphism;
and the reduct
{\mathsf{M}}
to
L
is a
\kappa
-saturated model of
T
.

As for
\rho
, the arbitrariness is mitigated by Proposition
3.21
(1); in the spirit
of Appendix
B
, it implies that
\phi

gives a well-defined homomorphism
Aut(M)\to Aut_{\mathsf{ApprSp}}(\mathcal{J}_{h},\vartriangle_{2})
.

Let us formulate a saturation hypothesis on a model
M
of
T_{{}^{def}}
:

(SH)

(1)

M
embeds all models of
T_{{}^{def}}
of size
\leq|\mathcal{J}|
;

(2)

M
is homogeneous for
L
-elementary embeddings
M_{1}\to M
with
M_{1}\models T
with
|M_{1}|\leq|\mathcal{J}|
. (I.e. for fixed
M_{1}
, all such embeddings are
Aut(M)
-conjugate.) (In particular,
M
is orbit-bounded.)

Note (SH) holds if either
M
is saturated as a model of
T_{{}^{def}}
, or else if it
is ec and saturated for qf types of
L_{{}^{def}}
.

Proposition 3.21
.

In Theorem
3.16
, we further have:

(1)

If
f,f^{\prime}:Aut(M)\to Aut(\mathcal{J})
are obtained from two choices of retractions
\rho
,
\rho^{\prime}
, then
f\vartriangle_{2}f^{\prime}
.

(2)

Take
M
to satisfy (SH).
Then

\rho
can be chosen so that the induced quasi-homomorphism

\phi:Aut(M)\to Aut(\mathcal{J})
(and
\varphi:Aut(M)\to{\mathcal{G}}(T)
) are surjective.

Proof.

(1) Let
\alpha\in Aut(M)
.
We have
\rho\circ\alpha{\vartriangle}\alpha
by Lemma
3.14
(3).
and
\rho^{\prime}\circ\alpha{\vartriangle}\alpha
, so

\rho\circ\alpha\vartriangle_{2}\rho^{\prime}\circ\alpha
and

\rho\circ\alpha\circ\iota\vartriangle_{2}\rho^{\prime}\circ\alpha\circ\iota
.

(3) The
L_{{}^{def}}
-structure on
M
can be viewed as a homomorphism
\iota:\mathcal{J}\to S(M)
.
Let
M_{0}
be a small elementary submodel of
M

as an
L_{{}^{def}}
-structure, and let
r_{0}:S(M)\to S(M_{0})
be
the restriction map. Then
\iota_{0}:=r_{0}\circ\iota:\mathcal{J}\to S(M_{0})

admits, by the retraction property of
\mathcal{J}
, a one-sided inverse

\rho_{0}:S(M_{0})\to\mathcal{J}
, with
\rho_{0}\iota_{0}=Id_{\mathcal{J}}
.
Let
\rho:S(M)\to\mathcal{J}
be the composition
\rho_{0}r_{0}
.
Let
f(\alpha)=\rho\circ\alpha\circ\iota
.

Claim .

f
is surjective.

To see this, identify
\mathcal{J}
with
J=\iota(\mathcal{J})
, so that
\iota
becomes the inclusion map of
J
in
S(M)
,
and
\iota_{0}=r_{0}|J
.
Let
\beta\in Aut(J)
. We can now view
\beta
also as a homomorphism
J\to S(M)
.
According to
[
27
]
A.11(3), there exists
\sigma\in Aut(M)
with
r_{0}\circ\sigma|J=r_{0}\circ\beta
.
So
f(\sigma)=\rho\sigma\iota=\rho_{0}r_{0}\sigma\iota=\rho_{0}r_{0}\beta\iota=\rho_{0}\iota_{0}\beta\iota=\beta
. Since
\beta\in Aut(J)

was arbitrary,
f
is onto.

∎

3.22.
The pullback of
\vartriangle
.

Define the binary relation
\vartriangle_{k}
to be the
k
-fold composition of
{\vartriangle}
with itself; it is defined by a pp formula (the existential quantifiers are local since

\vartriangle(x,y)
implies
m_{2}(x,y)
, Lemma
3.12
.) It is easy to check that the same expression
defines
the
k
-fold iteration of
\vartriangle
on
\mathcal{J}
(or see
[
27
]
3.12.)

For any
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}{{\mathcal{T}}}
, we define a relation
\vartriangle_{k}
on

Hom(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
by

h_{1}\vartriangle_{k}h_{2}\iff(\forall a\in\mathcal{A})(h_{1}(a),h_{2}(a))\in\vartriangle_{k}^{\mathcal{B}}

For an element of
Aut(\mathcal{A})
, we write
\vartriangle_{k}(h)
for
h\vartriangle_{k}1
, where
1=Id_{\mathcal{A}}
.
For
h_{1},h_{2}\in Aut(\mathcal{A})
, we have
h_{1}\vartriangle_{k}h_{2}
iff
(h_{1}(a),h_{2}(a))\in\vartriangle_{k}^{\mathcal{A}}

for all
a
; by definability of
\vartriangle_{k}
, this is iff
(a,h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}(a))\in\vartriangle_{k}^{\mathcal{A}}
for all
a
iff
\vartriangle_{k}(h_{1}^{-1}h_{2})
.
It follows that
\vartriangle_{k}
is a normal, symmetric subset of
Aut(\mathcal{A})
.

The above notation applies to
Aut(\mathcal{J})
and to
Aut(S(M))=Aut(M)
(via its action on
S(M)
.)

Finally, we define
\vartriangle_{k}^{{\mathcal{G}}(T)}
to be the image of
\vartriangle_{k}^{Aut(J)}
under the quotient homomorphism

Aut(J)\to{\mathcal{G}}(T)
.

Note that in the groups
Aut(J)
or
{\mathcal{G}}(T)
we have
\vartriangle_{k}\vartriangle_{k^{\prime}}\subset\vartriangle_{k+k^{\prime}}
, but (presumably) equality need not hold in general.

Proposition 3.23
.

In Theorem
3.16
, one can add:

(1)

\varphi(\vartriangle_{n}^{Aut(M)})\subset\vartriangle_{n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}

(2)

If
\phi(g_{1})\phi(g_{2})^{-1}\in\vartriangle_{n}^{Aut(\mathcal{J})}
, then
g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\vartriangle_{n+4}
.

(3)

\phi^{-1}\vartriangle_{n}^{Aut(\mathcal{J})}\subset\vartriangle_{n+3}^{Aut(M)}

(4)

\varphi^{-1}\vartriangle_{n}^{{\mathcal{G}}(T)}\subset\vartriangle_{n+5}^{Aut(M)}
.

(5)

If
\varphi(g_{1})\varphi(g_{2})^{-1}\in\vartriangle_{n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
, then
g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\vartriangle_{n+6}
.

Proof.

Identify
\mathcal{J}
with
J\leq S(M)
, and let
\rho:S(M)\to J
be a retraction.

(1) If
g\in\vartriangle^{S(M)}
, so that
g(p)\vartriangle p
for all
p\in S(M)
, then for
p\in J
we have
{\phi}(g)(p)=\rho g(p)L_{n}\rho(p)=p
. Thus
{\phi}(\vartriangle)\subset\vartriangle
. Similarly,
\phi(\vartriangle_{n})\subset\vartriangle_{n}
.

(2) Let
g_{1},g_{2}\in Aut(M)
and assume
\phi(g_{1})\vartriangle_{n}\phi(g_{2})
in
Aut({\mathcal{J})}
. Let
p\in S(M)
.
By Lemma
3.14
(3),
p\vartriangle\rho(p)
. Thus (using the fact that
g_{1},g_{2},\rho
are homomorphisms),

g_{1}(p)\vartriangle\rho g_{1}(p)\vartriangle\rho g_{1}(\rho(p))\vartriangle_{n}\rho g_{2}(\rho(p))\vartriangle\rho g_{2}(p)\vartriangle g_{2}(p)

So
g_{1}(p)\vartriangle_{n+4}g_{2}(p)
, and since
p
was arbitrary,
g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\vartriangle_{n+4}
.

(3) The penultimate step in the displayed formula in (2) can be skipped when
g_{2}=Id
, since
\rho\rho p=\rho p
.

(4)
Now suppose
\varphi(g)\in\vartriangle_{n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
; i.e.
\phi(g)=\pi(h)
for some
h\in\vartriangle_{n}^{Aut(J)}
.
If
g\in\ker\pi
then
g(p),p
are inseparable by open sets. By Lemma
3.14
(4), we have
(p,g(p))\in\vartriangle_{2}
so

\ker\pi\subset\vartriangle_{2}
.
So
{\phi}(g)h^{-1}\in\ker\pi\subset\vartriangle_{2}
.
Hence
{\phi}(g)\in\vartriangle_{n+2}
, and by (3),
g\in\vartriangle_{(n+2)+3}
.

(5) Similarly, if
\varphi(g_{1})\varphi(g_{2})^{-1}\in\vartriangle_{n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
, then by (2) we have
g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\vartriangle_{(n+2)+4}
.

∎

The example below is adapted from the basic component of the example of
[
14
]
of a non-compact Lascar group; local logic permits a simpler presentation.
It is also in essence the same as Example 3 of a quasimorphism in
[
41
]
, where one can find an illuminating discussion of its provenance in geometry.

Example 3.24
.

Let
T=Th_{loc;\mu_{1}}({\mathbb{R}},<,S)
. Here
S(x)=x+1
,

\mu_{1}(x,y)\equiv x\leq y\leq S(x)\vee y\leq x\leq S(y),

and the subscript loc designates that we take the theory in local logic, i.e. using quantifiers with bounded range only.

The space
\Sigma
of (local)
1
-types over
M=({\mathbb{R}},<,S)
can be identified with
\{-,0,+\}\times{\mathbb{R}}
;
here
(0,\alpha)
denotes the algebraic type
x=\alpha
;
(-,\alpha)
denotes the type just below
\alpha
,
and dually
(+,\alpha)
. The two non-local 0-definable types, at
\infty
and
-\infty
, are of course not part of the space
\Sigma
. Let
J=\{-,0,+\}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
, a
{\mathcal{L}}
-substructure of
\Sigma
. Pick a cut
\gamma\in(0,1]
and define
a
{\mathcal{L}}
-retraction
r:\Sigma\to J
as follows: for any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\delta\in[0,1)
and
*\in\{-,0,+\}
,
let
r(*,n+\delta)=(*,n)
if
\delta\leq\gamma
and
r(*,n+\delta)=(*,n+1)
if
if
\gamma<\delta
. It is easy to see that no further collapse is possible, and
J
is the universal e.c. model of
{\mathcal{T}}
.
We have
Aut(J)={\mathbb{Z}}
.

Aut(M)
is the much bigger group of order preserving maps
{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
commuting with
S
.
The quasimorphism on
Aut(M)
, associated with the above retraction, is the translation number
5
5

5

I take this term from
[
41
]
. Emmanuel Breuillard pointed out to me that these ideas go back to Poincaré’s
rotation number
.
A homeomorphism
\rho
of the oriented circle
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}=M/S^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
lifts uniquely to an element
r
of
Aut(M)
fixing a given point
m_{0}
;
the rotation number of
\rho
is the translation number of
r
.
.

Restricted to the group of
translations
{\mathbb{R}}
as a subgroup of
Aut(M)
, we obtain a choice of ’nearest integer’ map, which is of course
a quasi-homomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}\to({\mathbb{Z}};\{-1,0,1\})
.

In connection with the discussion in §
3.19
, we note also that in this example, we have

Hom(\mathcal{J},\Sigma)={\mathbb{R}}
and
Hom_{J}(\Sigma,J)
is the space
of cuts in
(0,1)
.

Question 3.25
.

Is it possible to develop an analogous theory uniform in the doubling constant? Assume every
2
-ball
is uniformly a union of a
Y
-parameterized family of
1
-balls, where the definable set
Y
is in some sense
‘small’ compared to
D
; at a minimum, there should exist types over
M
that properly increase
M
without increasing

Y
, so that finite distance still implies distance
1
from some point. According to a theorem of Shelah
[
62
]
, such types exist in pseudo-finite theories provided
|{D}|
is not polynomially bounded by
|Y|
. Thus in theory, this could
give a possible route to various questions of polynomial bounds. Developing a theory on this basis will not be easy, but even much stronger assumptions of relative smallness would be of interest. The theory of locally compact groups would need to be generalized, however, in any case, with the compact/ noncompact dichotomy replaced by a model-theoretic analogue.

4.
Definable groups

Recall Tao’s definition of approximate subgroups.

Definition 4.1

(
[
66
]
)
.

Let
G
be a group. A symmetric subset
\Lambda\subset G
is an
approximate subgroup

if
\Lambda\sim\Lambda^{2}
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
G
be a group, generated by an approximate subgroup
\Lambda
. Then
there exists a second countable
locally compact topological group
\mathsf{H}
,
a compact normal subset
\vartriangle\subset\mathsf{H}

and a quasi-homomorphism

f:G\to\mathsf{H}:\vartriangle

such that:

(1)

For
C\subset\mathsf{H}
compact,
f^{-1}(C)
is contained in some
\Lambda^{i}
.

(2)

For each
i
there exists a compact
C\subset\mathsf{H}
with
\Lambda^{i}\subset f^{-1}(C)
.

(3)

Specifically,
f^{-1}(\vartriangle)\subset\Lambda^{12}
.

(4)

Let
X,X^{\prime}
be compact subsets of
\mathsf{H}
, with
\vartriangle^{2}X\cap\vartriangle^{2}X^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Then there exist disjoint definable subsets
D,D^{\prime}
of
\Lambda^{k}
for some
k
(with parameters in
\Lambda
) such that
f^{-1}(X)\subset D
, and
f^{-1}(X^{\prime})\subset D^{\prime}
.

The purely group-theoretic properties (1,2) will be the main engine of the group-theoretic applications, including Theorem
5.16
and Theorem
6.9
; the second-countability will not be needed.

Note that
\mathsf{H}
is compact iff
\Lambda
generates
G
in a finite number
s
of steps. Even in this case, when
s
is large,
we obtain a nontrivial statement due to (3); in particular,
f:G\to\mathsf{H}:f(\Lambda^{12})
. See Corollary
4.13
.

(4) should be compared to the familiar form of continuity or continuous-logic definability of a map into a compact space,
asserting that the preimages of two disjoint compact sets can be separated by a definable set. Here this is asserted for
two compacts at some discrete distance from each other.

We will begin with a proof of (1,2);
the proofs of (3,4) are postponed till we discuss definability.

Proof.

We define a local structure
M
. The universe
D(M)
of
M
, as a set, is a copy of
G
.
The relations on
D
include the binary relation
yx^{-1}\in\Lambda
, denoted
m_{1}
:
and the quaternary relation
yx^{-1}=zw^{-1}\in\Lambda
. The locality generator is
m_{1}
. There are no further
relations in the structure
M
; thus the action of
G
on
D
from the right,

(g,d)\mapsto dg^{-1}
, is an automorphism of
M
. This gives a homomorphism
\delta:G\to Aut(M)
.
Since
G
is transitive on
D
, it is clear that
M
is orbit-bounded. The balls can be identified
as
m_{1}(a)=\Lambda a
, and
m_{2}(a)=\Lambda^{2}a
. As
\Lambda
is an approximate subgroup,

\Lambda^{2}\subset\cup_{i=1}^{k}\Lambda c_{i}
, so
m_{2}(a)=\cup_{i=1}^{k}m_{1}(c_{i}a)
; this gives the doubling property (DP).
Thus Theorem
3.10
applies:
Th(M)
admits a core
\mathcal{J}
, and
Aut(\mathcal{J})
has a natural locally compact topology;
the canonical Hausdorff quotient
{\mathcal{G}}(T):=Aut(\mathcal{J})/{\mathfrak{g}}_{D}
is a locally compact topological group; we also
defined a canonical compact, normal, symmetric subset
\vartriangle\subset{\mathcal{G}}(T)
. There exists an embedding

\iota:\mathcal{J}\to S(M)
, and a retraction
\rho:S(M)\to\mathcal{J}
.
According to
Theorem
3.16
,
there exists a quasi-homomorphism
{\varphi}:Aut(M)\to({\mathcal{G}}(T):{\vartriangle})
such that
compact subsets of
{\mathcal{G}}
pull back to bounded subsets of
Aut(M)
, and bounded subsets of
Aut(M)
push forward to
precompact subsets of
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Let
f=\varphi\circ\delta
. If
C\subset{\mathcal{G}}
is compact, then
\delta(f^{-1}(C))
is bounded in
Aut(M)
, being a subset of
\varphi^{-1}(C)
.
Picking
d_{0}\in D
to be the identity element of
G
, we have
\delta(f^{-1}(C))(d_{0})=d_{0}f^{-1}(C)=f^{-1}(C)
; so

f^{-1}(C)
is
bounded in
D(M)
. This means that
f^{-1}(C)\subset\Lambda^{i}
for some
i
. Conversely,
\delta(\Lambda^{i})
is by definition bounded, so
f(\Lambda^{i})=\varphi(\delta(\Lambda^{i}))
is precompact, hence
C=cl(f(\Lambda^{i}))
is compact and
\Lambda^{i}\subset f^{-1}(C)
.

To obtain second countability, we modify the end of the construction slightly. Recall
\varphi
was the composition

\pi\circ\phi
, with
\pi:Aut(\mathcal{J})\to{\mathcal{G}}
the quotient map, and
\phi:Aut(M)\to({\mathcal{G}}(T):{\vartriangle})
as constructed in
3.16
.
Let
P
be the complete atomic type of
\rho(d_{0})
.
Let
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{P}
be the infinitesimal subgroup with respect to the action on
P
. By Proposition
2.8
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{P}
is a closed, compact subgroup of
Aut(\mathcal{J})
, and
{\mathcal{G}}(P)

is a second countable, locally compact topological group. Let
\pi_{P}:Aut(\mathcal{J})\to{\mathfrak{g}}_{P}
be the projection.
We let
\varphi_{P}=\pi_{P}\circ\phi
, and again
f=\varphi_{P}\circ\delta
. We still have the above properties, and now
according to Proposition
2.8
,
{\mathcal{G}}(P)

is also second countable.

∎

We will also mention a slightly more general version. The proof is identical once we have a generalization of the local logic of
§
2
, where we do not assume that the locality relation is generated by a single relation; we will not repeat details but will describe the changes needed in the construction.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
G
be a group,
\omega
a family of
commensurable approximate subgroups of
G
. Assume:
if
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\omega
, then
\Lambda\cup\Lambda^{\prime}\in\omega
, and
\Lambda^{2}\in\omega
; and
\cup\omega=G
.
Then there exists a locally compact topological group
\mathsf{H}
,
a compact normal subset
\vartriangle\subset\mathsf{H}

and a quasi-homomorphism

{f}:G\to\mathsf{H}:\vartriangle

such that:

(1)

For
C\subset\mathsf{H}
compact, and
\Lambda\in\omega
,
f^{-1}(C)
is covered by a finite union of right translates of
\Lambda
.

(2)

For
\Lambda\in\omega
there exists a compact
C\subset\mathsf{H}
with
\Lambda\subset f^{-1}(C)
.

(3)

If
\Lambda_{0}\in\omega
and
X\subset\Lambda\in\omega
, we have
f^{-1}f(X)\subset X\Lambda_{0}^{12}
.
If
f(g_{1})\in f(g_{2})\vartriangle_{k}
then
\delta(g_{1}g_{2}^{-1})\in\vartriangle_{12+2k}
.

(4)

Let
X,Y
be compact subsets of
\mathsf{H}
, with
\vartriangle^{2}X\cap\vartriangle^{2}Y=\emptyset
. Then there exists a definable subset
D
of some
\Lambda\in\omega
(with parameters in
\Lambda
) such that
f^{-1}(X)\subset D
, and
f^{-1}(Y)\subset\Lambda\setminus D
.

(5)

If
\omega
is countable, we may take
\mathsf{H}
to be second countable.

Proof.

Define a structure
M
as in Theorem
4.2
, with universe
D=G
; for
each

\Lambda\in\omega
, we posit
a binary relation
m_{\Lambda}:\ yx^{-1}\in\Lambda
; and a quaternary relation
yx^{-1}=zw^{-1}\in\Lambda
. A subset of
D
is viewed
as local if it is contained in a ball
m_{\Lambda}(a)=\{b:m_{\Lambda}(a,b)\}
; or equivalently, in the union of finitely many such balls.
As before, only existential quantifiers of the form
(\exists x)m_{\Lambda}(x,y)\wedge\cdots
are allowed in the formation of pp formulas,
but now all
\Lambda\in\omega
are allowed.
If we pick one
\Lambda_{0}\in\omega
,
it remains true that any ball
m_{\Lambda}(a)
is covered by finitely many balls
m_{\Lambda_{0}}(b_{i})
. A weak model is local if
for any
a,b\in D(M)
,
m_{\Lambda}(a,b)
holds for some
\Lambda\in\omega
. A type
p(x)
is local if
m_{\Lambda}(x,y)
is represented
in
p
for some - or equivalently, by DP, for all -
\Lambda\in\omega
. The construction of
\mathcal{J},Aut(\mathcal{J}),{\mathfrak{g}},f
and proof of their properties is identical.

∎

Remark 4.4
.

Let
\omega^{\prime}
be any family of commensurable approximate subgroups of
G
,
such that if
\Lambda\in\omega
and
g\in G
then
g^{-1}\Lambda g
is commensurable
with
\Lambda
. Then
\omega^{\prime}
may be completed to a class
\omega
satisfying the hypotheses of Proposition
4.3
,
whose union is
G
.

Proof.

If
\Lambda\in\omega
, then any two-sided translate
a\Lambda b
is contained in a finite union of right translates of
\Lambda
:
we have

a\Lambda a^{-1}\subset\cup_{i}\Lambda c_{i}

for some
c_{1},\ldots,c_{l}
; and
a\Lambda b=(a\Lambda a^{-1})ab\subset\cup_{i}\Lambda c_{i}ab
. In particular, each

c_{i}ab\Lambda
is contained in a finite union
\cup\Lambda d_{ij}
, so
a\Lambda b\Lambda c\subset\cup_{i,j}\Lambda^{2}d_{ij}
,
and it follows that
a\Lambda b\Lambda c
is contained in a finite union of right translates of
\Lambda
. From here it is
clear that closing
\omega
under the operations
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\mapsto\Lambda\cup\Lambda^{\prime}
,
\Lambda\mapsto\Lambda^{2}
,
and
\Lambda\mapsto(a\Lambda\cup\{1\}\cup\Lambda a)

will not damage the hypotheses. ∎

4.5.
Definability

We will now place our approximate subgroups in a definable setting more explicitly. A complete first-order theory
T

is given with predicates
\Lambda_{i}
(
i\in I
, where
I
is a partially ordered index set. ) If
i\leq j
, a definable injective map

\Lambda_{i}\to\Lambda_{j}
is given, forming a direct limit system; the direct limit will be denoted
G
. We assume that for each
i,j\in I
for some
k\in I
, a definable map
\Lambda_{i}\times\Lambda_{j}\to\Lambda_{k}
is given; inducing a group multiplication on
G
.
In this situation
G
is called a piecewise definable group of the theory
T
; or a strict Ind-object in Grothendieck’s category of Ind-definable sets.
We identify
\Lambda_{i}
with a subset of
G
, and assume further that any two
\Lambda_{i}
are commensurable in
G
;
in particular each
\Lambda_{i}
is an approximate subgroup.

We say that a subset of
G
is

locally
\bigwedge
-definable
if the intersection with each
\Lambda_{i}
is
\bigwedge
-definable.

Given a theory
T
with a definable commensurable family of approximate subgroups, as above,
we introduce a new local sort
D
, a torsor for
G
. For
M\models T
,
D(M)
is a copy of
G(M)
. The new relations introduced
are binary relations
m_{i}(x,y):\ \ \equiv\ \ (yx^{-1}\in\Lambda_{i})
on
D
, and the relation
yx^{-1}=u\in\Lambda_{i}
on
D^{2}\times\Lambda_{i}
.
We define a locality structure on
D
, using the
m_{i}
. Restricting attention to
the induced (local) structure on
D
, we form
\mathcal{J}
(in the corresponding sort
\mathcal{D}
.) Denote by
L^{+}

the langauge obtained from
L
by adding the local sort
D
and these relations;
M^{+}
the result
of adding the torsor
D
to the structure as above; and
T^{+}:=Th(M^{+})
.

Next recall the universal theory
T^{+}_{{}^{def}}
of
[
27
]
(see 3.17). A model
N^{+}_{{}^{def}}
is nothing more than a model

N^{+}
of
T^{+}
, along with a homomorphism
\iota:\mathcal{J}\to S_{D}(N^{+})
. The language
L^{+}_{{}^{def}}
is obtained from
L^{+}
by adding
a new relation
(d_{p}x)\phi

for each
p\in\mathcal{J}
and each
\phi(x;y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})\in L^{+}
, to be interpreted as a subset of
D^{k}
, namely as

\{(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})\in D^{k}:\phi(x,b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})\in\iota(p)\}
. Fix some
p_{0}\in\mathcal{J}
; the choice will not matter, up
to bi-interpretation. We have in
L^{+}_{{}^{def}}
a
unary relation
m_{k}^{p_{0}}
in
L^{+}
such that
m_{k}^{p_{0}}(b)
holds iff
m_{k}(x,b)\in p_{0}
. We will only consider models of

T^{+}_{{}^{def}}
that omit the partial type:
\neg m_{k}^{p_{0}}(x,b),k=1,2,\cdots
. This amounts to treating the sort
D
of

T^{+}_{{}^{def}}
(a single local sort in
T^{+}
) as the union of infinitely many ‘sorts’ defined by
m_{k}^{p_{0}}
, with inclusion maps among them. Any model of

T^{+}_{{}^{def}}
(in this sense), restricted to an
L
-structure, is a (local) model of
T^{+}
.

Since some model of
(T^{+}_{{}^{def}})^{\pm}

is a model of
T
, if
T^{+}_{{}^{def}}\models\neg(\exists x,y,z)(\alpha(x,y)\wedge\gamma(y,z))
with
\alpha\in L^{+}
,
and
T^{+}\models(\forall y)(\exists x)(\alpha(x,y))
, then

T^{+}_{{}^{def}}\models\neg(\exists y,z)(\gamma(y,z))
.) Thus
T^{+}_{{}^{def}}{\overset{e.c.}{\models}}{}T^{+}
.

In Theorem
4.2
and Proposition
4.3
, we used the action of
G
on
D
by right translation. This action depends on a choice
of an element
d_{0}
of
D
, used to identify
G
with
D
via
g\mapsto gd_{0}
. The homomorphism
\delta:G\to Aut(M^{+})

is thus also dependent on this choice, and may be better denoted
\delta_{d_{0}}
. However when
X
is a normal subset of
Aut(M^{+})
,
\delta^{-1}(X)
is well-defined and does not depend on the choice of
d_{0}
. In particular,

\delta^{-1}(\vartriangle_{k})

is well-defined. (Where
\vartriangle_{k}\subset Aut(M^{+})
is defined above Lemma
3.23
.) Let us compare
\delta^{-1}(\vartriangle_{k})

to
\vartriangle_{k}
, as computed directly in
T
(i.e. in
G
and not in
D
.)

Consider

P:=\{ab^{-1}:a,b\in G(M),a\vartriangle b\}

Here
\vartriangle
is computed in
\aleph_{0}
-saturated models of
T
.

While there is no multiplication on
D
, we can define
ab^{-1}\in G
for
a,b\in D
, so that
(ab^{-1})b=a
. Then

P=\{ab^{-1}:a,b\in D(M),a\vartriangle b\}
. (If
a,b\in D
and
tp(a/N)=tp(b/N)
, then picking
d\in D(N)
we have
ab^{-1}=(ad^{-1})(bd^{-1})^{-1}

and
ad^{-1}\vartriangle^{M}bd^{-1}
.) From this it follows easily that
\delta^{-1}(\vartriangle_{k}^{Aut(M^{+})})\subset P^{k}
.

On the other hand, by locality and the doubling property, if
a,b\in D
and
a\vartriangle^{D}b
then
ab^{-1}\in\Lambda^{2}
. (Since
tp(a/N)=tp(b/N)

implies that
a,b
lie in a common
1
-ball.)

By Proposition
3.23
(2), we obtain:

Proposition 4.6
.

In
4.2
and
4.3
, one can add:
If
f(g_{1})f(g_{2})^{-1}\in\vartriangle_{k}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
then
\delta(g_{1}g_{2}^{-1})\in\vartriangle_{k+6}
so

g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in P^{k+6}\subset\Lambda^{2(k+6)}
. Likewise, if
f(g)\in\vartriangle_{k}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
then
\delta(g)\in\Lambda^{2(k+5)}
.

In particular, this proves Theorem
4.2
(3).

Remark 4.7
.

All conjugates of
P
are contained in
P^{3}
. Hence
P\subset Q\subset P^{3}
for some normal

\bigwedge
-definable set
Q
.

Proof.

Let
c\in{P}
, and
g\in G
. We have
c=c_{1}^{-1}c_{2}
with
tp(c_{1}/N)=tp(c_{2}/N)
. Take
N
to be
\aleph_{1}
-saturated, and
N_{0}
a countable elementary submodel; then
tp(g/N_{0})=tp(m/N_{0})

for some
m\in N
; so
m^{-1}g\in{P}
.
Now
tp(m^{-1}c_{1}m/M)=tp(m^{-1}c_{2}m/M)
, so

m^{-1}cm\in{P}
. Thus
g^{-1}cg=l^{-1}(m^{-1}cm)l\in P^{3}
. Now let
Q
be the intersection of all conjugates of
P^{3}
.∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
G
be a piecewise definable group in a theory
T
, limit of definable approximate subgroups.
Let
M^{+}_{{}^{def}}\models T^{+}_{{}^{def}}
, let
M^{+}
be the reduct to
L^{+}
, and
\iota:\mathcal{J}\to S(M^{+})
the embedding corresponding
to
M^{+}_{{}^{def}}
. Further let
\rho:S(M^{+})\to\mathcal{J}
be a retraction, and let
d_{0}
be an element of
D(M^{+})
,
Let
\delta:G\to Aut(S(M)^{+})
the map induced by right multiplication
if
(D,d_{0})
is identified with
(G,1)
:
\delta(g)(hd_{0})=hg^{-1}d_{0}
.
Finally let

\phi:Aut(S(M)^{+})\to Aut(\mathcal{J})
be defined by
\gamma\mapsto\rho\circ\gamma\circ\iota
,
\varphi
be the composition of
\phi

with
Aut(\mathcal{J})\to{\mathcal{G}}
, and
f=\varphi\circ\delta:G(M)\to{\mathcal{G}}
.
Then:

(1)

Let
W
be a compact subset of
{\mathcal{G}}
. Then there exists a
\bigwedge
-definable

W^{*}\subset G
in
L_{{}^{def}}^{+}(d_{0})
with

f^{-1}(W)\subset W^{*}\subset f^{-1}(W\vartriangle)
.
W^{*}
is defined by universal formulas of
L^{+}_{{}^{def}}(d_{0})
.

(2)

Let
W_{1},W_{2}
be compact subsets of
{\mathcal{G}}
with
\vartriangle W_{1}\cap\vartriangle W_{2}=\emptyset
. Then
there exist definable sets
V_{1},V_{2}\subset G
in
L_{{}^{def}}^{+}(d_{0})
with
f^{-1}(W_{i})\subset V_{i}
and

V_{1}\cap V_{2}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

During the present proof, the word definable and its attendants will refer to
L^{+}_{{}^{def}}(d_{0})
.

We show first that we may assume some saturation of
M^{+}_{{}^{def}}
; this will be needed for (2).
Let
((M^{+}_{{}^{def}})^{*},d)
be an elementary extension of
(M^{+}_{{}^{def}},d)
. Define
\rho^{*}=\rho\circ r
,
where
r:S(M^{+})\to S(M)
is the restriction map. We obtain
\iota^{*},\delta^{*},\phi^{*},\varphi^{*}
; and it is easy to verify that
the various maps commute, and we have:
\phi\circ\delta=\phi^{*}\circ\delta^{*}|M
, and
f=f^{*}|M
. Thus
by passing to an elementary extension
we may assume
M^{+}_{{}^{def}}
is
\aleph_{0}
- saturated, and
M^{+}
is
\aleph_{0}
- homogeneous.

We begin with the proof of (1).

Identify
\mathcal{J}
with
\iota(\mathcal{J})
. Let
{\mathsf{f}}
denote the composition

G(M)\to_{\delta}Aut(M^{+})\to Aut(S_{D}(M^{+}))\to_{\phi}Aut(\mathcal{J})

so that
f=\pi\circ{\mathsf{f}}
, with
\pi:Aut(\mathcal{J})\to{\mathcal{G}}
.
We will write
\hat{g}
for
\delta(g)
or for the image of
g
in
Aut(S_{D}(M^{+}))
or
Aut(\mathcal{J})
.
The homomorphism
Aut(\mathcal{J})\to{\mathcal{G}}
is continuous and proper; so it suffices to show that for each closed bounded subset
V
of
Aut(\mathcal{J})
, there exists a
\bigwedge
-definable set
V^{*}
with

{\mathsf{f}}^{-1}(V)\subset V^{*}\subset{\mathsf{f}}^{-1}(V\vartriangle)\ \ \ \ \

Assuming the displayed statement holds for some family of sets
V_{i}
, closed under finite intersections, it
will also be true for
\cap_{i}V_{i}
, with

(\cap_{i}V_{i})^{*}=\cap_{i}V_{i}^{*}
; this, since by compactness
\cap_{i}V_{i}\vartriangle=(\cap_{i}V_{i})\vartriangle
.
Hence, recalling that basic closed sets are closed under finite intersections,
it sufices to take
V
to be a basic closed set in
Aut(\mathcal{J})
, i.e.

V=\{\gamma\in Aut(\mathcal{J}):R(a,\gamma(a))\}

Here (using Theorem
3.10
(3))
R
can be taken to be a basic relation of
\mathcal{L}
ensuring that
\gamma
lies in a bounded subset of
G
, when
a
is fixed;

a
is a tuple of elements of
\mathcal{J}
, and
\gamma((a_{j}:j\in J))=(\gamma(a_{j}):j\in J)
. While
R
will make use of only finitely many of the
a_{j}
and
\gamma(a_{j})
, it will be convenient to allow
a
to be an infinite tuple, indeed a tuple enumerating all of
\mathcal{J}
. Let
p
be the atomic type of
a
in
\mathcal{J}
. For two tuples
(a_{j}:j\in J),(b_{j}:j\in J)
, write
\vartriangle^{1}(a,b)
if
\vartriangle(a_{j},b_{j})
holds for each
j
.
Define a subset
V^{*}
of
G
thus:

V^{*}=\{g\in G:S(M^{+})\models(\exists\xi)(p(\xi)\wedge(\xi\vartriangle\hat{g}(a))\wedge R(a,\xi))\}

Let
g\in G(M)
,
\gamma={\mathsf{f}}(g)
the image of
g
in
Aut(\mathcal{J})
. We have
\gamma=(\rho\circ\hat{g})|\mathcal{J}
.

If
\gamma\in V
, then letting
\xi=\gamma(a)
,
we see (using the definition of
V
and Lemma
3.14
(3)) that
g\in V^{*}
. Thus
{\mathsf{f}}^{-1}(V)\subset V^{*}
.

In the opposite directionm assume
g\in V^{*}
. Then

\mathcal{J}\models(\exists\xi)(p(\xi)\wedge(\xi\vartriangle\rho\hat{g}(a))\wedge R(a,\xi)).

Let
b
be a witness for
\xi
. Since
p(b)
holds,
a\mapsto b
defines an
\mathcal{L}
-homomorphism
\mathcal{J}\to\mathcal{J}
;
by Proposition
2.1
, it is an automorphism of
\mathcal{J}
, call it
\gamma^{\prime}
. Since
\gamma^{\prime}(a)=b
and
b\vartriangle\gamma(a)=\gamma(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}(b)
, we have
\gamma(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}(b)\vartriangle b
and so
b\vartriangle\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{-1}(b)
.
As
b
enumerates
\mathcal{J}
,
\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{-1}\in\vartriangle^{Aut(\mathcal{J})}
.
But
R(a,b)
shows that
\gamma^{\prime}\in V
. So
\gamma\in V\vartriangle
.

Note that
V^{*}
is bounded. It remains only to see that
V^{*}
is (locally)
\bigwedge
-definable. Using local compactness of
S(M)
we see that
V^{*}
is the intersection of all approximations to it, obtained by replacing
\vartriangle
by a definable approximation inside the matrix of
definition of
V^{*}
; these approximations can be chosen so as to still ensure that the set of
g
they define is bounded.

Hence

V^{*}
is a conjunction of
pp relations concerning
(a,\hat{g}(a))
. By Theorem
3.10
(3),
V^{*}
can also be written as an (infinite) conjunction of
atomic formulas
{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}
; so it suffices to prove that each such formula is locally definable.
To simplify notation, consider the atomic formula
{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}(p,\hat{g}(q))
,

t=(\phi_{1}(x_{1},u),\phi_{2}(x_{2},u))
, with
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
formulas of
T^{+}
. Here
p,q\in\mathcal{J}
are fixed and
g
is the variable. We have

{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}(p,\hat{g}(q))
iff there is no
c
with
\neg\phi_{1}(x,c)\in p
and
\neg\phi_{2}(x,c)\in\hat{g}(q)
.
Now
\phi_{2}(x,c)\in\hat{g}(q)
iff
\phi_{2}(x,\hat{g^{-1}}(c))\in q
.
Thus

{\mathsf{Def}}_{t}(p,\delta(g)(q))\iff\neg(\exists c)(d_{p}x)\neg\phi_{1}(x,c)\wedge(d_{q}x)\neg\phi_{2}(x,\hat{g^{-1}}(c))

The right hand side is definable in
L^{+}_{{}^{def}}(d)
.

(2) Let
W_{1}^{*},W_{2}^{*}
be as in (1). Then
W_{i}^{*}
is
\bigwedge
-definable (in particular contained in a definable
approximation to
G
), and

W_{1}^{*}\cap W_{2}^{*}\subset f^{-1}(W_{1}\vartriangle)\cap f^{-1}(W_{2}\vartriangle)=\emptyset
. If
W_{1}^{*},W_{2}^{*}
were not separated by
definable sets
V_{i}\subset G
(in
L^{+}_{{}^{def}}(d_{0})
), by compactness and since every type over
\emptyset
is
realized , we would have
W_{1}^{*}\cap W_{2}^{*}\neq\emptyset
, a contradiction.

∎

Remark 4.9
.

The Beth-Craig-Robinson definability theory is valid for
\bigwedge
-definable relations. Here is the version we need:
Let
M
be an
\aleph_{0}
-homogeneous
L
-structure. Let
M^{\prime}
be an expansion to an
L^{\prime}
-structure, in which every type is realized. Let
X,Y
be
\bigwedge
-definable subsets in
M^{\prime}
. Assume
X\cap\sigma Y=\emptyset
for every automorphism

\sigma
of
M
. Then
X,Y
are separated by a 0-definable set of
M
. [Proof: Let
SX=\{tp(a;M^{\prime}):a\in X\}
, and similarly
SY
.
Then
SX,SY
are compact sets in the space
S^{\prime}
of
L^{\prime}
-types (over
\emptyset
.) Let
\pi:S^{\prime}\to S
be the projection to the space of
L
-types. Then
\pi SX,\pi SY
must also be compact sets, and they are disjoint since if
tp_{L}(a)=tp_{L}(b)
then there exists
an
L
-automorphism
\sigma
with
\sigma(a)=b
. Hence there exists a 0-definable set
D
of
L
separating
\pi SX,\pi SY
and thus also
X,Y
.]

It follows in particular that if
X
is
Aut(M)
-invariant, then
X
is
\bigwedge
-definable in
L
.

Definability in the following corollary (and beyond) refers again to
L
, not to
L_{{}^{def}}
.

Corollary 4.10
.

In Proposition
4.8
, and hence also in
4.2
and
4.3
, one can add that

f^{-1}(cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle))
is locally

\bigwedge
-definable in
L
. In fact it is locally an intersection of 0-definable sets commensurable with
\Lambda
,
and the smallest subgroup with this property.

Proof.

As in Proposition
4.8
, we may assume
M
is
\beth_{2}^{+}
-saturated in
L_{{}^{def}}
, and
\aleph_{0}
-homogeneous in
L
.
We will show that
f^{-1}(cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle))
is
Aut(M)
-invariant.

We can identify
Aut(M)
with a subgroup of
Aut(M^{+})
, namely the subgroup fixing
d_{0}
. Under this identification,
we have, for
\sigma\in Aut(M)
and
g\in G(M)
,

\delta(\sigma(g))=\sigma^{-1}\delta(g)\sigma

We have
f=\varphi\circ\delta:G(M)\to{\mathcal{G}}:\vartriangle
; where
\varphi:Aut(M)\to{\mathcal{G}}:\vartriangle
and
\delta:G\to Aut(M)
.

Let
\sigma\in Aut(M)
,
g\in G(M)
, and let
Y
be a normal subset of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
Note first that
\sigma^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(Y))\sigma\subset\varphi^{-1}(Y\vartriangle^{3})
. Composing with
\delta
, it follows that

\sigma(f^{-1}(Y))\subset f^{-1}(Y\vartriangle^{3})
. In case
Y=Y\vartriangle
, this shows that
f^{-1}(Y)
is
Aut(M)
-invariant.

If in addition
Y
is closed, by Proposition
4.8
,
f^{-1}(Y)=Y^{*}
is also locally
\bigwedge
-definable
in
L_{{}^{def}}
; i.e.
\Lambda\cap f^{-1}(Y)
is
\bigwedge
- definable in
L_{{}^{def}}
, for any
\Lambda\in\omega
. By Remark
4.9
,
(using the
Aut(M)
-invariance),
f^{-1}(Y)
is then locally
\bigwedge
-definable in
L
.

In particular, this applies to
f^{-1}(cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle))
.
Since
\Lambda/f^{-1}(cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle))
has cardinality
\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
, it is clear that any definable subset of
\Lambda
containing
f^{-1}(cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle))
is commensurable with
\Lambda
.

In the other direction, let
H
be a locally
\bigwedge
–definable subgroup of
G
of bounded index. Then
H
induces
a locally
\bigwedge
–definable equivalence relation
E
on the
G
-torsor
D
, namely
yx^{-1}\in H
; and thus an
equivalence relation (also denoted
E
) on the type space
S_{D}(M)
. It is easy to see that
E
is an intersection of

\mathcal{L}
-definable relations, so that
E
is closed in the pp topology too; and we have
\rho(p)E\rho(q)
iff
pEq
. The elements of
Aut(J)
preserving each
E
-class thus form a closed subgroup of
Aut(J)
. Identifying

\mathcal{J}
with the image
J
of
\rho
, we see that the image of
H
in
Aut(\mathcal{J})
contains a closed subgroup that in turn
contains
\vartriangle^{Aut(\mathcal{J})}
; so the image contains
cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle)
. This clearly remains true modulo
{\mathfrak{g}}
too.
∎

See
[
19
]
for a systematic treatment of piecewise hyper-definable groups.
G^{00}_{0}
is defined there to
be the smallest locally -
\bigwedge
-definable subgroup of
G
of bounded index. In this notation, we have

f^{-1}(cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle))=G^{00}_{0}
.

4.11.
Proof of Theorem
4.2
(4)

Unlike Proposition
4.10
, the conclusion here allows for parameters. Let
M_{0}
be an elementary submodel
of the given structure, with

|M_{0}|\leq|\Omega|
. Let
M
be a highly saturated model of
T^{+}_{{}^{def}}
, with
\aleph_{0}
-homogeneous reduct
to
L(M_{0})
. We have the map
\varphi:Aut(M)\to{\mathcal{G}}
. By Proposition
3.23
(1),
\varphi(\vartriangle^{Aut(M)})\subset\vartriangle^{{\mathcal{G}}}
.
Now
Aut(M/M_{0})\subset\vartriangle^{Aut(M)}
, by definition of
\vartriangle
.
Thus if
\sigma\in Aut(M/M_{0})
and
a\in\varphi^{-1}(X)
then
\sigma^{-1}a\sigma\in\varphi^{-1}(X\vartriangle^{2})
. Let

X^{*},Y^{*}
be as in Proposition
4.8
. Then
X^{*}\subset\varphi^{-1}(X\vartriangle)
so
\sigma^{-1}(X^{*})\sigma\subset\varphi^{-1}(X\vartriangle^{3})
.
Since
\vartriangle Y\cap\vartriangle^{3}X=\emptyset
,
we have
\sigma^{-1}(X^{*})\sigma\cap Y^{*}=\emptyset
. Thus by Beth, as formulated above,
X^{*},Y^{*}
are separated by
L(M_{0})
-definable sets.

It follows that the pullback of closed,
\langle\vartriangle\rangle
-invariant sets is
\bigwedge
-definable with parameters. Thus:

Corollary 4.12
.

The induced homomorphism
G/f^{-1}(cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle))\to\mathsf{H}/cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle)
is
continuous, i.e. the inverse image of any compact subset is
\bigwedge
-definable with parameters.

Proposition
4.3
applies, in particular, to the top commensurability class of a group
G
, namely the generic definable subsets.
Here the results of
[
27
]
apply directly, there is no need for locality,
and
\mathsf{H}
is compact; nevertheless, the distance between
\vartriangle
and
\mathsf{H}
reflects the level of approximateness.
For instance we have, from Theorem
4.2
(3):

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
G
be a group, let
X
be a symmetric definable
subset commensurable to
G
. Let
T^{+}

be the associated theory of a
G
-torsor
D
,

\mathcal{J}
the core space of
T^{+}
on the torsor sort
D
, and let
C=Aut(\mathcal{J})/{\mathfrak{g}}
be the canonical
compact Hausdorff group associated to
(G,\cdot,X)
. If
C=1
then
X^{12}=G
.

It suffices of course that the image of
G
in
C
be trivial. The corollary gives, in principle, a
compact model for
slowly converging approximate subgroups
\Lambda
, i.e. where
\Lambda^{m}=G
but the smallest such
m
is large.
Whether it can be improved to a Lie model depends, in part, on an effectivization of the theory of normal conjugacy classes
in locally compact groups.

Question 4.14
.

How far can the 12 in Theorem
4.2
be improved?

Question 4.15
.

Describe the image of
f
in Theorem
4.2
. Is it equal to
Aut((\mathcal{J}_{D})_{h}/(\mathcal{J}_{G})_{h})
?

5.
Approximate subgroups

Let
G
be a group.
For
\Lambda\subset G
, define
\Lambda^{-1}=\{a^{-1}:a\in\Lambda\}
,
\Lambda^{2}=\{ab:a,b\in\Lambda\}
.

\widetilde{\Lambda}=\cup\Lambda^{n}
will denote the group generated by
\Lambda
.
\Lambda
is

symmetric
if
1\in\Lambda=\Lambda^{-1}
.

If
Y\subset G
is covered by finitely many right translates
of
X
,
Y\subset\cup_{j=1}^{n}Xb_{j}
, we will say that
X

commensurably covers

Y
.
Two subsets
X,Y\subset G
are
commensurable
,
X\sim Y
, if each is covered by finitely many translates
of the other:
X\subset\cup_{i=1}^{m}Ya_{i}
,
Y\subset\cup_{j=1}^{n}Xb_{j}
.

Let
\Lambda
be an approximate subgroup of a group
G
(Definition
4.1
.)
The
commensurator

{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)
of an approximate subgroup
\Lambda\subset G
is the group of elements
g\in G
with
g\Lambda g^{-1}
commensurable to
\Lambda
. The
commensurability class
\omega
of
\Lambda
is the
set of approximate subgroups commensurable to
\Lambda
. The
commensurator of
\Lambda
depends only on the commensurability class, and can be denoted
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\omega)
or
\langle\omega\rangle_{max}
.
it is the pullback of the subgroup of
Aut(G)
fixing
\omega
, under the natural map
G\to Inn(G)\leq Aut(G)
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\Lambda
be an approximate subgroup of
G
,
\omega
the commensurability class. Then

\langle\omega\rangle_{max}=\cup\omega
;
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)
is the union of all approximate subgroups commensurable to
\Lambda
.

Proof.

Note first that
\Lambda\subset{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)
: if
g\in\Lambda
then
g^{-1}\Lambda g\subset(\Lambda)^{3}
, so

g^{-1}\Lambda g
is commensurably covered by
\Lambda
, and conjugating back by
g
we obtain the converse.
If
\Lambda^{\prime}\sim\Lambda
then
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda^{\prime})={\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)
, so
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)
. Conversely,
if
g\in{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)
, then
g\Lambda\cup\Lambda\cup\Lambda g^{-1}
is an approximate subgroup in
\omega
;
see the proof of Remark
4.4
.
∎

Let
\mathsf{H}
be a locally compact topological group. Let
U
be a compact neighborhood of
1
. Then
U
is an approximate subgroup.

The compact, symmetric neighborhoods of
1
form a unique commensurability class of approximate subgroups; we call it the
fundamental commensurability class
of
\mathsf{H}
. If
f:G\to\mathsf{H}
is a homomorphism,
the commensurability class in
G
of the pullback of any compact open neighborhood of the identity in
\mathsf{H}
is
denoted
\chi(f)
, and again called the fundamental commensurability class of
f
.

If
H
is a subgroup of
G
, any commensurability class
\omega^{\prime}
of approximate subgroups of
H
extends to a commensurability class
\omega
of approximate subgroups of
G
. We say in this case that
\omega

belongs to

H
. (This terminology does not preclude belonging to still smaller subgroups.; in fact if
\omega
belongs to
H
, then it belongs to any finite index subgroup of
H
. In many cases of interest, there does exist a unique smallest commensurability class of subgroups to which
\omega
belongs; see
Proposition
5.10
.)

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
H\leq G
. If
X,Y\subset H
are commensurable as subsets of
G
, they are commensurable in
H
.

Proof.

Say
X\subset\cup Ya_{i}
; then
either
Ya_{i}
can be deleted, or
a_{i}\in Y^{-1}X\subset H
. thus
X
is also contained in a union of
H
-translates of
Y
; and dually. ∎

5.3.
Laminar commensurability classes

A commensurability class
\omega=\chi(f)
arising from a homomorphism
f
on a subgroup of
G
into a locally compact group

\mathsf{H}
will be said to be
laminar
. It is shown in
[
26
]
that in this case,
\mathsf{H}
can be taken to be a connected Lie group
with no compact normal subgroups; this Lie group is then uniquely determined by the class
\omega
.
This uses Yamabe’s theorem, that guarantees that any locally compact group has an open subgroup
compactly isogenous to a Lie group. This subgroup however is neither canonical nor normal, and making use of it exacts a technical toll later on, before one regains a measure of normality.

In the opposite direction, it is sometimes desirable to extend the domain of
f
as much as possible. It follows
from Lemma
5.1
that the domain of any
f
with
\chi(f)=\omega
is contained in the commensurator group
\omega
;
and indeed it is possible to find
f
with this domain.

Lemma 5.4
.

If
\omega
is a laminar commensurability class of approximate subgroups, there exists
a homomorphism
F
with domain
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\omega)
into a locally compact group, with
\chi(F)=\omega
.

Proof.

This can be seen via Proposition
4.3
. More directly, view
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\omega)
as an ind-definable group,
where the inductive system is the family of all approximate subgroups in
\omega
; by Lemma
5.1
,
their union of
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\omega)
. Let
G*
be a saturated elementary extension,
\lambda^{*}=\lambda(G^{*})
,
and

\omega^{*}=\cup_{\lambda\in\omega}\lambda^{*}
. Let
I
be the set of
\lambda_{1}\in\omega
such that there exist

\lambda_{2}\cdots\in\omega
with
\lambda_{k+1}^{2}\subset\lambda_{k}
,
\lambda_{k}^{-1}=\lambda_{k}
. If
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\omega
,
then we saw that
\lambda^{2}\cap(\lambda^{\prime})^{2}\in\omega
. Hence if
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\in I
and
\lambda_{k},\lambda^{\prime}_{k}

are as in the definition of
I
, since
\lambda_{k+1}^{2}\cap(\lambda^{\prime}_{k+1})^{2}\in\omega
, we have

\lambda_{k}\cap\lambda^{\prime}_{k}\in\omega
for each
k
, and so
\lambda_{1}\cap\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\in I
. It is clear that

I
is conjugation-invariant. Let
N
be the

\bigwedge
-definable subgroup of
\omega^{*}
, formed by the intersection of all
\lambda\in I
. Then
N
is a normal subgroup of
\omega^{*}
,
N\subset\lambda_{1}
(for any choice of
\lambda_{1}\in I
), and
\omega^{*}/N
is locally
compact in the logic topology. Let
F
be the natural map
\omega^{*}\to\omega^{*}/N
, restricted to
\omega
.
∎

For example, the approximate subgroup
\{a/2^{n}:n\in{\mathbb{N}},a\in{\mathbb{Z}},-2^{n}\leq a\leq 2^{n}\}
of
{\mathbb{Q}}
has commensurability class
\chi(f)
, where
f:{\mathbb{Z}}[1/2]\to{\mathbb{R}}
; and also
\chi(g)
, with
g:{\mathbb{Q}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times\Pi_{p\neq 2}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
the natural inclusion.

We note immediately a uniqueness statement for the Lie group explanation of a given commensurability class of approximate subgroups, with a given subgroup as domain.

If
N
is a compact normal subgroup of
\mathsf{H}
,
\pi:\mathsf{H}\to\mathsf{H}/N
the quotient map, then the composed map
\pi\circ f:G\to\mathsf{H}/H

determines the same commensurability class of approximate subgroups as
f
.
This is however
the only ambiguity, i.e.
f:G\to\mathsf{H}
is unique up to compact isogeny:

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
h_{i}:G\to H_{i}
be homomorphisms with dense image into locally compact groups, and assume

\chi(h_{1})=\chi(h_{2})
. Then there exist compact normal subgroups
N_{i}
of
H_{i}
and an isomorphism

\phi:H_{1}/N_{1}\to H_{2}/N_{2}
, with
{p}_{2}h_{2}=\phi\circ{p}_{1}h_{1}
(where
{p}_{i}:H_{i}\to H_{i}/N_{i}
is the natural projection.)

Proof.

Let
F_{0}\leq H_{1}\times H_{2}
be the image of
G
under the map
(h_{1},h_{2}):\Lambda\to H_{1}\times H_{2}
.
Let
F
be the closure of
F_{0}
. Then
F
is a closed subgroup of
H_{1}\times H_{2}
.
Let
\pi_{i}:F\to H_{i}
be the projection, let
N_{1}=\{x\in H_{1}:(x,1)\in F\}
,
N_{2}=\{y\in H_{2}:(1,y)\in F\}
.

Claim 1.

N_{i}
is compact; and indeed
\pi_{i}
is proper.

Proof.

Let
U_{i}
be a precompact open neighborhood of
1
in
H_{i}
. Then
h_{2}^{-1}(U_{2})
is commensurable with
\Lambda
,
and hence with
h_{1}^{-1}(U_{1})
; so
h_{2}^{-1}(U_{2})
is covered by finitely many translates of
h_{1}^{-1}(U_{1})
; hence

h_{1}h_{2}^{-1}(U_{2})
is covered by finitely many translates of
U_{1}
, so it is precompact.

So
N_{1}\subset\pi_{1}(F\cap(H_{1}\times U_{2}))\subset\pi_{1}cl(F_{0}\cap(H_{1}\times U_{2}))\subset cl(h_{1}h_{2}^{-1}(U_{2}))
and so
N_{1}
is precompact. Being closed it is compact, and likewise
N_{2}
.
Along the way we showed properness:
\pi_{2}^{-1}(U_{2})\subset cl(h_{1}h_{2}^{-1}(U_{2}))\times U_{2}
so it is pre-compact.

∎

Claim 2.

F
projects onto
H_{1}
and onto
H_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
U
be an open subset of
H_{2}
, with compact closure
{\mathbf{U}}
. By Claim 1,
\pi_{2}
is proper,
so
\pi_{2}^{-1}({\mathbf{U}})
is compact. Thus
\pi_{2}(\pi_{2}^{-1}({\mathbf{U}}))
is compact. By the density assumption,

\pi_{2}(\pi_{2}^{-1}(U))
is dense in
U
. Thus
\pi_{2}(\pi_{2}^{-1}({\mathbf{U}}))
is closed, and contains a dense subset of
U
,
hence it contains
U
. It follows that
F
projects onto
H_{2}
, and similarly
H_{1}
.
∎

Now
N_{i}\trianglelefteq H_{i}
, and
F
induces a group isomorphism
\phi:H_{1}/N_{1}\to H_{2}/N_{2}
.
By the same argument as in Claim 1,
\phi(X)
is compact iff
X
is compact.
Let
V_{i}
be a compact neighborhood of
1
in
H_{i}
, and
W_{1}=V_{1}\cup\phi^{-1}(V_{2})
. Then
W_{1}
is compact, as is
W_{2}=\phi(W_{1})=\phi(V_{1})\cup V_{2}
.
Thus
\phi
restricted to
W_{1}
is a homeomorphism near
1
, which suffices.

∎

There is a one-sided version of Proposition
5.5
:

Remark 5.6
.

If
h:G\to H
and
h^{\prime}:G\to H^{\prime}
are homomorphisms with dense image into locally compact groups,
and
\chi(h)
lies below
\chi(h^{\prime})
in the sense that there exist
\Lambda\in\chi(h),\Lambda^{\prime}\in\chi(h^{\prime})
with
\Lambda\leq\Lambda^{\prime}
,
then there exists a compact normal subgroup
N^{\prime}
of
H^{\prime}
and a homomorphism
\phi:H\to H^{\prime}/N^{\prime}
such that

\phi\circ h=p^{\prime}\circ h^{\prime}
.

This could be proved in the same way, but also follows from the statement of Proposition
5.5
: define
h_{2}:G\to H\times H^{\prime}

by
h_{2}(x)=(h(x),h^{\prime}(x))
, and let
H_{2}
be the closure of the image of
h_{2}
. Then
\chi(h_{2})=\chi(h)
and Proposition
5.5
applies, and proves the remark.

Given a commensurability class
\theta
of approximate subgroups, if it arises from a homomorphism
f:G\to\mathsf{H}
, there is always a
universal
one with class
\theta
, mapping with compact kernel onto any other.
Often, there is also a minimal one, namely
f:G\to\mathsf{H}
such that
\mathsf{H}
has no compact normal subgroups.
Both are unique up to a unique isomorphism under
G
.

5.7.
Intersections

(See
[
46
]
).
Let
\omega
be a
commensurability class of approximate subgroups of a group
G
, and let
H
be a subgroup of
G
.
If
X,Y\in\omega
, then
Y\cap H
is commensurably covered by
XX^{-1}\cap H
. To see this, say
Y\subset\cup_{i=1}^{m}Xc_{i}
. Then
Y\cap H\subset\cup_{i=1}^{m}(Xc_{i}\cap H)
. Now if
Xc_{i}\cap H\neq\emptyset
,
let
h_{i}\in Xc_{i}\cap H
. Then
c_{i}\in X^{-1}h_{i}
so
Xc_{i}\subset XX^{-1}h_{i}
. Hence the
H
-commensurability class of

XX^{-1}\cap H
does not depend on the choice of
X\in\omega
. In particular,
as
(XX^{-1}\cap H)^{2}\subset(XX^{-1})^{2}\cap H
and
(XX^{-1})^{2}\in\omega
,
XX^{-1}\cap H
is an approximate subgroup of
H
. We denote this class by
\omega\cap H
.
If
H
is normal,
we also have the class
\omega/H
, represented by
X/H
for any
X\in\omega
.
Then
\omega
belongs to
H
iff
\omega\cap H\sim\omega
. When
H
is normal, this is equivalent to:
\omega/H\sim\{1\}/H
.

One can similarly define an intersection of commensurability classes: if
X,Y
are approximate subgroups of
G
,
then so is
XX^{-1}\cap YY^{-1}
, and the commensurability class of
XX^{-1}\cap YY^{-1}
depends only on the classes of
X
and of
Y
. In case
X,Y
are commensurable,
XX^{-1}\cap YY^{-1}
is commensurable to both. This can be seen directly using a Rusza-style argument, starting from a maximal subset
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
of
X
such that the sets
a_{i}Y
are disjoint. A more conceptual derivation will soon be available: we will see below (Theorem
4.2
) that any commensurability class of approximate subgroups can be written as
\chi(f)
, for some approximate homomorphism into a locally compact group; then it is easy to check that
\chi(f\times g)=\chi(f)\cap\chi(g)
, and
\chi(f\times f)=\chi(f)
.

5.8.
Minimal commensurability class of supporting subgroups

Proposition
5.10
was obtained only for laminar approximate lattices in an earlier version of this paper, as a corollary of Proposition
A.9
. Thanks to Simon Machado for discussions regarding the general case below.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\omega=\chi(g)
,
g:G\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}:[-1,1]^{n}
a homogeneous quasimorphism.
Let
S\leq S^{\prime}
be subgroups of
G
to which
\omega
belongs, such that
g(S),g(S^{\prime})
span the same
{\mathbb{R}}
- subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then
S
has finite index in
S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\in S
be such that the
g(s_{i})
form a basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
for the span
V
of
f(S^{\prime})
.
Now any point of
V
is at bounded distance from a lattice point
\sum m_{i}v_{i}
; and
\sum m_{i}v_{i}
is at bounded distance from
g(s_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots s_{n}^{m_{n}})
, as
g
is a homogeneous
quasimorphism. Thus we can find a ball
B
in
V
such that
f(S)+B=V
, and
B
contains the error set of
g
.
Let
x\in S^{\prime}
. Then for some
s\in S
we have
g(s)\in g(x)+B
and so
g(xs^{-1})\in BB
. Thus
g(x)\in g^{-1}(BB)S
. Now
g^{-1}(BB)
is covered by finitely many
cosets of
S
. Hence
G
is covered by finitely many
cosets of
SS=S
, i.e.
[S^{\prime}:S]<\infty
.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

For any commensurability class
\omega
of approximate subgroups of a group
G
, there exists a unique
commensurability class
{\langle\omega\rangle}_{min}
of subgroups of
G
such that
\omega
belongs to a subgroup
S
iff

S
contains some element of
{\langle\omega\rangle}_{min}
.

We will use several times the statements on intersections of approximate subgroups above.
In particular, the class
\Omega
of subgroups containing (commensurably) an element of
\omega
is closed under finite intersections.

We will
use Theorem
5.16
in the version of supplement (i). We may assume
G=\widetilde{\Lambda}
,
so we have a connected Lie group
L
, a rigid abelian subgroup
A\trianglelefteq L
,
A\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
a normal compact subset
K
of
A
,
and a quasi-homomorphism

f:G\to L:K

with
\omega=\chi(f)
. Let
\bar{f}
be the homomorphism obtained as a composition of
f
with the quotient map
L\to L/A
.

Let
H
be an element of
\Omega
such that
L_{H}=cl(\bar{f}(H))
has least possible dimension.
The connected component

L_{H}^{o}
of
L_{H}
is a connected Lie group of the same dimension, and is open in
L_{H}
; so for a compact
U\subset L/A
,

U\cap L_{H}
is contained in finitely many cosets of
L_{H}^{o}
. Thus if
X\in\omega
and
X\subset H
,
then
X
is contained in finitely many translates of
H^{\prime}:=\bar{f}^{-1}(L_{H}^{o})
. So
H^{\prime}\in\Omega
; and
\bar{f}(H^{\prime})
is
dense in
L_{H}^{o}
. Changing notation, and replacing
H
by
H^{\prime}
, this shows that we can find
H
with
cl(\bar{f}(H))

connected; we may change notation to
cl(\bar{f}(H))=L/A
. By minimality of the dimension, for any
S\leq H
with
S\in\Omega
, we have
cl(\bar{f}(S))=L/A
.

G
is considered
as a piecewise-definable group in a structure
M
(where a piece is an element of
\omega
). We can take all subgroups
S
of
G
to be locally definable. We take a saturated extension
M^{*}
of
M
,
and write
S^{*}
for
S(M^{*})
.
By local compactness
of
L
and
L/A
,
f
and
\bar{f}
extends to
G(M^{*})
.
Let
\Gamma=\ker(\bar{f})=f^{-1}(A)
. So
\Gamma
is a
\bigwedge
-definable group.
Note that if
cl(\bar{f}(S))=L/A
, we have
\bar{f}(S(M^{*}))=L/A
.

Since
\Omega
is closed under finite intersections, to find a minimal element (up to commensurability) it suffices to show
that there is no infinite descending chain of subgroups
H>H_{1}>H_{2}>\cdots
in
\Omega
,
and with
H_{i}/H_{i+1}
infinite. We show more:

Claim .
Let
H>H_{1}>\ldots>H_{n+1}
be elements of
\Omega
. Then
[H_{i}:H_{i+1}]
is finite for some
i
.

Proof.

Let
g
be the homogeneous quasimorphism at bounded distance from
f|\Gamma
. Note that
\chi(g)

belongs to
H_{i}^{*}\cap\Gamma
for each
i
(a bounded subset of
A
is still a bounded subset of
L
.)
The spans of
g(H_{i}^{*})
form a descending sequence of subspaces, so
g(H_{i}^{*}\cap\Gamma)
has the same span as
g(H_{i+1}^{*}\cap\Gamma)
for some
i
.
By Lemma
5.9
,
[\Gamma\cap H_{i}^{*}:\Gamma\cap H_{i+1}^{*}]
is finite.

So
\Gamma\cap H_{i}^{*}=(\Gamma\cap H_{i+1}^{*})F
for some finite
F\subset H_{i}^{*}\Gamma
.

Now let
x\in H_{i}^{*}
. Let
y\in H_{i+1}^{*}
be such that
\bar{f}(y)=\bar{f}(x)
. Then
y^{-1}x\in H_{i}^{*}\cap\Gamma
.
So
y^{-1}x\in H_{i+1}^{*}F
and thus
x\in H_{i+1}^{*}F
. This shows that
H_{i}^{*}\subset H_{i+1}^{*}F
.
So
H_{i}^{*}
does not intersect
|F|+1
distinct cosets of
H_{i+1}^{*}
. Thus
H_{i}
does not intersect
|F|+1
distinct cosets of
H_{i+1}
; so
[H_{i}:H_{i+1}]\leq|F|
. This proves the claim and hence the Proposition.

∎

5.11.
The fundamental commensurability class

More generally, if
f:G\to\mathsf{H}:K
is a quasi-homomorphism, with
\mathsf{H}
locally compact and

K=K^{-1}
compact, we consider pullbacks
Y=f^{-1}(W)
of compact neighborhoods
W
of
K
in
\mathsf{H}
.
We will see below that

YY^{-1}
is an approximate subgroup, whose
commensurability class is uniquely determined. We continue to use the notation
\chi(f)
for this class, and to refer to it as the fundamental class of
f
.
We will write
X\sim\chi(f)
to mean that the commensurability class of
X
equals
\chi(f)
.
Uniqueness again holds but now in the category of compact correspondences.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
H
be a locally compact topological group,
K
a symmetric compact subset of
H
,
and assume

f:\Lambda\to\mathsf{H}:K

is a quasi-homomorphism, with

f(xy^{-1})\in f(x)f(y)^{-1}{K}

Then all sets
X=f^{-1}({U}{K})
, with
{U}
a compact neighborhood of the identity in
\mathsf{H}
, are
right- commensurable in
\Lambda
. Thus any symmetric set sandwiched between two such is an approximate subgroup.
In particular
XX^{-1}
is one.

Proof.

We begin by comparing translation in
\Lambda
and in
\mathsf{H}
.

Claim 1.
For any
{V}\subset\mathsf{H}
and
a\in\mathsf{H}
,
f^{-1}({V}a)\subset f^{-1}({V}{V}^{-1}{K})c
for some
c
.

If
f^{-1}({V}a)=\emptyset
, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, let
c
be such that
f(c)\in{V}a
.
If
f(x)\in{V}a
, then
f(xc^{-1})\in({V}a)({V}a)^{-1}{K}={V}{V}^{-1}{K}
; so
xc^{-1}\in f^{-1}({V}{V}^{-1}{K})
and

x\in f^{-1}({V}{V}^{-1}{K})c
.

Claim 2.
Let
Z\subset\mathsf{H}
be compact and let
U
be a neighborhood of
1
in
\mathsf{H}
. Then
finitely many right cosets of
f^{-1}(U{K})
cover
f^{-1}(Z)
.

Proof.

Let
V
be a neighborhood of
1
, with

VV^{-1}\subset U
. As
Z
is compact,
it is covered by finitely many cosets
Va
;
so it suffices to show that
f^{-1}(Va)
is covered
by a right coset of
f^{-1}(VV^{-1}{K})
; this follows from (2) above. ∎

Let
U
be a compact neighborhood of
1
in
\mathsf{H}
,
X=f^{-1}(UK)
and
Y=XX^{-1}
. Note
Y
is symmetric (
1\in Y=Y^{-1}
).
We have
Y\subset f^{-1}(UK(UK)^{-1}K)
; taking
Z=UK(UK)^{-1}K
in Claim 2, we see that
Y
is covered
by finitely many right translates of
X
.
Claim 2 also implies the right-commensurability of any two such
X
, and hence of any set sandwiched
W
between two such.
Thus any such
W
is an approximate subgroup; in particular this applies to
Y
.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

(1)

Proposition
5.12
did not assume normality of
K
.

(2)

If we do assume that
K
is normal and that
f(xy)\in Kf(x)f(y)
(as is the usual assumption for quasimorphisms),
we obtain (with
x,y
replaced by
x,y^{-1}x
) that
f(x)\in f(y)f(y^{-1}x)K
,
and so changing sides and using
K=K^{-1}
, we have
f(y^{-1}x)\in f(y)^{-1}f(x)K
, and similarly
f(xy^{-1})\in f(x)f(y)^{-1}K
.

(3)

It is worth noting that although we began with an approximate homomorphism into a locally compact group in the general sense of a
compact
error set
K
, the associated commensurability class in
\Lambda
consists of approximate subgroups in
Tao’s original sense of a
finite
set of translates.

(4)

Responding to a question of Kobi Peterzil during a talk, we consider the case of quasimorphisms, i.e. take

\mathsf{H}={\mathbb{R}}
. we may take
K=[-1,1]
. Modifying

f
to be homogeneous, it becomes symmetric,
f(x^{-1})=-f(x)
and
f(1)=0
.
Given
e>0
, let
k(e)
be the smallest natural number such that
X=f^{-1}(-1-e,1+e)
is a
k
-approximate subgroup
(Proposition
5.12
). What kind of function is
k(e)
?

Following the above proof, we have

XX\subset f^{-1}(-3-2{e},3+2{e})
; this interval is covered by
m
translates of
(-{e}/2,{e}/2)
, if
m>4(1+6/e)
;
the pullback of each such translate is contained in a translate of
X
. Hence
X
is a
5
-approximate subgroup if
e>24
.

Question 5.14
.

Is there an absolute bound on the level of approximateness, for small
e>0
?

In Example
3.24
, with
\Lambda
the central extension of the homeomorphism group of the circle, the
translation number
f
commutes with translation by
{\mathbb{Z}}
; for any
e>0
, if
X=f^{-1}(-1-e,1+e)
, then

XX\subset f^{-1}(-3-2e,3+2e)\subset X\pm\{0,1,2,3\}
. What about Brooks’ examples,
[
41
]
, Example 1?

Question 5.15
.

What is the minimal
k
such that the
approximate kernel of any quasimorphism is commensurable to a
k
-approximate subgroup?

By the above discussion, the answer is one of the numbers 2,3,4,5.

We have already proved the principal structure theorem for approximate subgroups, Theorem
4.2
; we will now refine it.
See Definition
C.1
(6) for the definition of rigidity, and §
5.11
for
\chi
.

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
\omega
be a commensurability class of approximate subgroups of a group
G
. Then there exists a subgroup
\widetilde{\Lambda}\leq G
, a connected Lie group
\mathsf{L}
, a rigid abelian subgroup
A\trianglelefteq\mathsf{L}
, a normal compact subset
K
of
A
,
and a quasi-homomorphism

f:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{L}:K

with
\omega=\chi(f)
.

Some supplements:

(i)

One can demand that
A\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{N}

for some
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and that
\langle f(\widetilde{\Lambda})\rangle A
be dense in
\mathsf{L}
.

(ii)

One can add that
\mathsf{L}
has no compact normal subgroups.

(iii)

In place of connectedness of
\mathsf{L}
, and as an alternative to (i), one can ask that
\langle f(\widetilde{\Lambda})\rangle
is dense in
\mathsf{L}
,
and
A\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
for some
n,k
.

(iv)

The induced homomorphism
\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{L}/A
is definable in the sense of continuous logic.

Following the proof, we will give two ways to decompose the quasi-homomorphism
f
into better studied kinds.
In Theorem
5.21
, we will describe the situation in terms of a
\bigwedge
-definable group
H
and
n
definable quasimorphisms
on
H
, i.e. a definable quasi-homomorphism on
H
into
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. In Proposition
5.24
, we will instead decompose

f
in terms of a rigid group extension
\widehat{\Lambda}
of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
, and a group homomorphism
\hat{f}:\widehat{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{L}
.

5.17.
Refining the target.

The proof of Theorem
5.16
will start with a quasi-homomorpism
{f}:\Gamma\to\mathsf{H}:K
as in Theorem
4.2
,
and gradually seek to simplify it, without changing the commensurability class
\chi({f})
associated with
{f}
.
Let us review in advance a few of the allowed modifications, along with an explanation of why the pullback class
\chi(f)
does not change. Some of the steps parallel
[
20
]
, where Fujiwara and Kapovich proved that any quasi-homomorphism with discrete target can be perturbed to one with central error. Here the best we can aim for is a rigid error set.

(1)

Replace
\mathsf{H}
by an open subgroup
\mathsf{H}_{1}
containing
K
; let
{\Gamma}_{1}={f}^{-1}(\mathsf{H}_{0})
,
{f}_{1}={f}|{\Gamma}_{1}
;
K_{1}=K\cap\mathsf{H}_{1}
.
Then
{\Gamma}_{1}
is a subgroup (if
x,y\in{\Gamma}_{1}
then
{f}(xy)\in{f}(x){f}(y)K\subset{\Gamma}_{1}
.)
Since a compact open neighborhood of the identity in
\mathsf{H}_{1}
is also one in
\mathsf{H}
, we have
\chi({f}|{\Gamma}_{1})=\chi({f})
.

(2)

Factor out a compact normal
N
subgroup of
\mathsf{H}
: let
{f^{\prime}}:\Gamma\to\mathsf{H}/N:K/N
be the composition of
{f}

with the quotient map
\pi:\mathsf{H}\to\mathsf{H}/N
. We have
({f^{\prime}})^{-1}(YN/N)={f}^{-1}(YN)
for any
compact neighborhood
Y
of
K
in
\mathsf{H}
, so
\chi({f})=\chi({f^{\prime}})
.

(3)

Let
\mathsf{H}_{2}
be a closed subgroup of
\mathsf{H}
, with
\mathsf{H}_{2}K^{m}=\mathsf{H}
. Let
K_{2}=\mathsf{H}_{2}\cap K^{3m+1}
.
For any
x\in{\Gamma}
pick
c(x)\in K^{m}
with
f(x)c(x)\in\mathsf{H}_{2}
,
c(1)=1
, and define
g:{\Gamma}\to\mathsf{H}_{2}
by
g(x)=f(x)c(x)
.
For any
x,y\in{\Gamma}

we have, for some
k=k(x,y)\in K
:

g(xy)=f(xy)c(xy)=f(x)f(y)kc(xy)=g(x)c(x)^{-1}g(y)c(y)^{-1}kc(xy)=

=g(x)g(y)c(x)^{-g(y)}c(y)^{-1}kc(xy)\in g(x)g(y)K^{3m+1}

so
g(xy)\in g(x)g(y)K_{2}
.

For a compact neighborhood
U
of
1
in
\mathsf{H}
,
UK^{m}\cap\mathsf{H}_{2}
is a compact neighborhood of
1
in
\mathsf{H}_{2}
,
and
f^{-1}(U)\subset g^{-1}(UK^{m}\cap\mathsf{H}_{2})\subset f^{-1}(UK^{2m})
. Thus
\chi(f)=\chi(g)
.

(4)

Let
H_{0}
be an open subgroup of
\mathsf{H}
of finite index. Let
\mathsf{H}_{3}=H_{0}\langle K\rangle
. Then
\mathsf{H}_{3}=H_{0}K^{m}

for some
m
. Let
{\Gamma}_{1}=f^{-1}(\mathsf{H}_{3})
.
Then
{\Gamma}_{1}
is a finite index subgroup of
{\Gamma}
, and by (1)
\chi(f)=\chi(f|{\Gamma}_{1})
.
By the previous step (3), we can find
g:{\Gamma}_{1}\to H_{0}
with
\chi(f)=\chi(g)
.

Remark 5.18
.

To a model theorist, the apparent arbitrariness in the choice of
f_{0}
in step (3) is disturbing.
It will only be used via (4), so in a finite index setting, where only finitely many parameters need be chosen. Note also
in general
that if
\langle K\rangle
is centerless, the correction function
c_{0}
, and hence
f_{0}
, are uniquely defined in the construction.
The general case could be made definable using a relational presentation of the notion of a quasi-homomorphism,
namely the category
\mathsf{CoGr}
of Appendix
B
,
in effect allowing
f(x)
to be a compact subset rather than an element;
and taking
k(x)=f(x)^{-1}H_{2}\cap K^{m}
.

Proof of Theorem
5.16
.

Let
\Lambda_{0}
be an approximate subgroup in
\omega
, and
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\langle\Lambda_{0}\rangle
.
By Theorem
4.2
,
there exists
f:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{H}:K
with
\mathsf{H}
locally compact,

K
normal, symmetric, compact,with
\Lambda_{0}\sim\chi(f)
.

By Proposition
C.3
, there exist closed normal subgroups
N\leq A\leq H_{1}\leq\mathsf{H}
with
\mathsf{H}/H_{1}
finite,

N
compact, with
A/N
basic abelian and rigid in
H_{1}/N
, such that
\langle K\rangle\cap H_{1}\leq A
.
By
5.17
(4), we may pass from
\mathsf{H}
to the finite index subgroup
H_{1}
(replacing
K
by
K^{m}\cap H_{1}
for appropriate
m
); so we may assume
K\subset A
,
N\leq A\leq\mathsf{H}
. Since
N
is compact, by
5.17
(2) we may factor it out.
Now
A
is basic abelian and rigid in
\mathsf{H}
, and
K\subset A
.

We have
A=DV
with
D\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{l}
central in
\mathsf{H}
, and
V
a rigid vector group. Let
D_{\mathbb{R}}=D{\otimes}{\mathbb{R}}
, so that

(A,D)\cong({\mathbb{Z}}^{l},{\mathbb{R}}^{l})
. Let
\bar{\mathsf{H}}=\mathsf{H}\times_{D}D_{\mathbb{R}}:=(\mathsf{H}\times D_{\mathbb{R}})/D
, with
D
embedded into

\mathsf{H}\times D_{\mathbb{R}}
by
d\mapsto(d,-d)
. This diagonal image of
D
is a closed, discrete subgroup of
\mathsf{H}\times D_{\mathbb{R}}

and so the quotient is still locally compact. Now
\mathsf{H}
embeds into
\bar{\mathsf{H}}
as a closed subgroup. Let
\bar{A}=A\times_{D}D_{\mathbb{R}}
.
If
U
is a compact neighborhood of
1
in
\bar{\mathsf{H}}
, then
U\cap\mathsf{H}
is a compact neighborhood of
1
in
\mathsf{H}
.
Hence we may replace
\mathsf{H},A,K
by
\bar{\mathsf{H}},\bar{A},K
; we have the same situation, and in addition
\bar{A}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
for some
N
. We are of course not claiming any density at this point.

By Yamabe’s theorem
[
70
]
,
\mathsf{H}
has an open subgroup
H^{\prime}
and a compact subgroup
N^{\prime}
, normal in
H^{\prime}
,
with
H^{\prime}/N^{\prime}
a Lie group.
As
V
is connected and
H^{\prime}
is open,
V\subset H^{\prime}
; i.e. by the above paragraph,
A\subset H^{\prime}
. By

5.17
(1), we may pass from
\mathsf{H}
to
H^{\prime}
; so we may assume
\mathsf{H}=H^{\prime}
.
Factoring out the compact normal subgroup
N^{\prime}
using
5.17
(2)
again, we have that
\mathsf{L}
is a Lie group.

Replacing
\mathsf{H}
by the closed subgroup

\mathsf{L}=cl(f(\widetilde{\Lambda})A)
, we may assume
f(\widetilde{\Lambda})A
is dense in
\mathsf{L}
. Now
\mathsf{L}^{0}
is an open subgroup of
\mathsf{L}
;

\mathsf{L}^{0}\cap f(\widetilde{\Lambda})A
is thus still dense in
\mathsf{L}^{0}
: since
A
is connected,
A\subset\mathsf{L}^{0}
; by
5.17
(1), we may replace
\mathsf{L}
by
\mathsf{L}^{0}
, so that we may assume
\mathsf{L}
is connected. Finally, by
5.17
(2), we may, without changing
\chi(f)
, factor out the maximal normal compact subgroup of
\mathsf{L}
, to obtain that
\mathsf{L}
has no nontrivial normal compact subgroups.

Now the continuity (iv) comes from Corollary
4.12
; the various modifications made
to the quasi-homomorphism of Theorem
4.2
, to wit restricting the domain to a subgroup, factoring out a compact normal subgroup of the image, and modifying by a map into
A
, will not effect this.

∎

Remark 5.19
.

If desired, say in order to connect with cohomological results phrased for unitary modules,
we can make the error set contained in a complex (unitary) rather than real rigid space.
Take first the semi-direct product of
\mathsf{L}
with the complexifization
A_{{\mathbb{C}}}
(where
\mathsf{L}
acts on
A
by conjugation, and
using the derived action on
A_{{\mathbb{C}}}
), and then factoring out the image of the homomorphism
A\to\mathsf{L}\times A_{{\mathbb{C}}}
,

x\mapsto(-x,x)
, which becomes a normal subgroup. The three variants
\mathsf{L},\mathsf{L}^{\prime},\mathsf{L}^{\prime\prime}
do not detract from the canonicity of the construction, but give three canonical forms that may be convenient for different purposes.

5.20.
Model-theoretic formulation

Let us recall some definitions. As usual, we interpret
\bigwedge
and
\bigvee
-definable
sets in a sufficiently saturated model of the theory.

(1)

Let
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\cup H_{n}
be a
\bigvee
-definable group.
A set
X\subset\widetilde{\Lambda}
is
locally
\bigwedge
-definable
if for each
N
,
H_{n}\cap X
is
\bigwedge
-definable.

(2)

A subgroup
\Gamma
of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is
locally
\bigwedge
-definable
if
H_{n}\cap\Gamma
is
\bigwedge
-definable, for all
n
.

(3)

A
\bigwedge
-definable approximate subgroup
W

is said to
determine a commensurability class
\omega
if
W=\cap_{n}W_{n}
, with
W_{1}\supset W_{2}\supset\cdots

all in
\omega
.

(4)

Let
X
be a
\bigwedge
-definable set. A function
\phi:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
is said to be
continuous
(or:
definable in the sense of continuous logic
) if it is bounded, and for any two disjoint compact sets
C,C^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
there exists a definable
D

with
\phi^{-1}(C)\subset D
and
D\cap\phi^{-1}C^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

(5)

Let
X
be a locally
\bigwedge
-definable subset of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
. Then
\phi:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
continuous
if

\phi|(X\cap H_{n})
is continuous for each
n
.

Theorem 5.21
.

Let
G
be a group,
\omega
a commensurability class of approximate subgroups of a group
G
.
Then there exists a
\bigvee
-definable group
\widetilde{\Lambda}
, a locally
\bigwedge
-definable subgroup
\Gamma\leq\widetilde{\Lambda}
of bounded index, and finitely many continuous
quasimorphisms
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}:\Gamma\to{\mathbb{R}}
, such that the
approximate kernel

\Gamma_{\phi}:=\cap_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}^{-1}[-1,1]

is a
\bigwedge
-definable approximate subgroup, and

\omega
is the commensurability class determined by
\Gamma_{\phi}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.16
, there exists a quasi-homomorphism
f:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{L}:K
, where
K
is a compact subset of a rigid abelian subgroup
A\trianglelefteq\mathsf{L}
,
A\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
\omega=\chi(f)
.
Let
\bar{\mathsf{L}}=\mathsf{L}/A
, let
\bar{f}:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\bar{\mathsf{L}}
be the composed homomorphism
\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{L}\to\bar{\mathsf{L}}
;
and let
\Gamma
be the kernel.
By Corollary
4.12
, since
A
contains
cl(\langle\vartriangle\rangle)
, the induced homomorphism
\widetilde{\Lambda}/\Gamma\to\mathsf{L}/A
is continuous, and
\Gamma
is a
locally
\bigwedge
-definable group.

Let
\psi=f|\Gamma
.
Identifying
A
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and projecting
to coordinates, we can view
\psi
as an
n
-tuple of quasimorphisms
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n}
.
Let
\phi_{i}
be the homogeneous
quasimorphism at bounded distance from
\psi_{i}
; and let
\phi=(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n})
.
We have:

(1)

\phi^{-1}(-m,m)^{n}
is an approximate subgroup, for large enough
m
(by Proposition
5.12
).

(2)

If
C\subset A
is compact, then
\psi^{-1}(C)\subset f^{-1}(C)

so this set is contained in a definable subset of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
. (Theorem
4.2
(4)).

(3)

for some
m
, the pullbacks under
\psi
of two compact subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
whose Euclidean distance is at most
m
are separated by a definable set

(4)

\psi^{-1}(-m,m)^{n}
has bounded index in
\widetilde{\Lambda}
(even if we work in a large saturated
M
, if
(a_{i}:i<(2^{\aleph_{0}})^{+}

are elements of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
, then some
a_{i},a_{j}
with
i<j
must have the same image in
\mathsf{L}
, hence
a_{i}^{-1}a_{j}\in A
,
and
\psi(a_{i})\in\psi(a_{j})\vartriangle\subset(-m,m)^{n}
.) )

Properties (2-4) of
\psi
above are all also inherited by
\phi
.

Replacing
\phi
by
\frac{1}{m}\phi
we may assume
\phi^{-1}((-1,1)^{n})
is an approximate subgroup, and the inverse images of two compacts that are
1
-separated (i.e.
[-1,1]^{n}C\cap C^{\prime}=\emptyset
) are separated by a definable set.
Along with the homogeneity of
\phi
, this implies that
\phi
is continuous: if
C,C^{\prime}
are disjoint compact subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
they are
\epsilon
-separated for some
\epsilon>0
; let
N
be an integer with
N\epsilon>1
. Note

\phi^{-1}(NC),\phi^{-1}(NC^{\prime})
are contained in a definable subset of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
. As
NC,NC^{\prime}
are
1
-separated,
there exists a definable set
D
with
\phi^{-1}(NC)\subset D,D\cap\phi^{-1}(NC^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Let

D^{*}=\{g\in\Gamma:g^{n}\in D\}
. Then
\phi^{-1}(C)\subset D^{*}
,
D^{*}\cap\phi^{-1}(C^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Thus the definable sets
separate pullbacks of an arbitrary pair of disjoint compacts, proving the continuity of
\phi
.
Since
\phi^{-1}[-1,1]^{n}
has bounded index in
\widetilde{\Lambda}
, any definable set containing
\phi^{-1}[-1,1]^{n}
must be commensurable with the definable approximations to
\widetilde{\Lambda}
.

∎

Remarks 5.22
.

(1)

In case
n=0
,
\Gamma
is
\bigwedge\omega
-definable. Existence of such a
\Gamma
is assured in this case by Theorem 3.5 of

[
26
]
.

(2)

We may choose
\widetilde{\Lambda},\Gamma
so that
\widetilde{\Lambda}/\Gamma
is a connected
Lie group with no nontrivial compact normal subgroup.

Corollary 5.23
.

Let
G
be a group of bounded exponent. Then any approximate subgroup of
G
is commensurable to a subgroup.

Proof.

Let notation be as in Theorem
5.21
; by Remark
5.22
, we may take
\widetilde{\Lambda}/\Gamma
to be a connected
Lie group. Since it has bounded exponent, it must be trivial. Thus by the Theorem,
\omega
is
the commensurability class associated with a quasimorphism
\widetilde{\Lambda}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
; we can take the quasimorphism to be homogeneous.
In this case, the bounded exponent again implies that the image is
0
. So
\omega
is the commensurability class of the group

\widetilde{\Lambda}
. ∎

5.24.
Group-theoretic formulation

We restate Theorem
5.16
in more standard group-theoretic terms, somewhat in the spirit
of the Bargmann-Wigner theorem on projective representations of Lie groups.

Let
G
be a group, let
E_{n}
be Euclidean space,
O_{n}
the group of isometries of
E_{n}
fixing
0
. Assume
a homomorphism
\rho:G\to O_{n}
is given;
\rho
makes
E_{n}
into a
G
-module
V
; we call a
rigid

G
-module
to indicate that the image of
\rho
preserves the Euclidean norm.

Let
C^{n}(G,\rho)=C^{n}(G,V)
be the
{\mathbb{R}}
-space of functions from
G^{n}\to V
view this as a complex with the usual differential,

\left(d^{n+1}\varphi\right)(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n+1})=g_{1}\varphi(g_{2},\dots,g_{n+1})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}\varphi\left(g_{1},\ldots,g_{i-1},g_{i}g_{i+1},\ldots,g_{n+1}\right)+(-1)^{n+1}\varphi(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})
.

The kernel of
d^{n+1}
is denoted

Z^{n}
, the group of
n
-cocycles. In particular any
\alpha\in Z^{2}(G,V)
determines canonically
a group
G\underset{\alpha}{\times}V
with universe
G\times V
, and multiplication:
(g,e)(h,d)=(gh,\rho(h)e+d+\alpha(g,h))
. It fits into an exact sequence

0\to E_{n}\to G\underset{\alpha}{\times}V\to G\to 1

In case
\rho
is trivial, this is a central extension of
G
by
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
; in general we may call it a rigid extension of
G
.

When
G
is discrete, we give
G\underset{\alpha}{\times}V
the product topology of
G\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Let
C_{bdd}
be the subcomplex of the bounded chains,
and
H^{*}_{bdd}
or
H^{*}_{b}
the cohomology.

Theorem 5.25
.

Let
\omega
be a commensurability class of approximate subgroups of a group
G
. Then there exists a subgroup
\widetilde{\Lambda}\leq G
, a connected Lie group
\mathsf{L}
without compact normal subgroups,
a rigid
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-module
V
,
a cocycle bounded
2
-cocycle
\alpha:\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}\to V
,
and a continuous homomorphism
\phi:\widetilde{\Lambda}\underset{\alpha}{\times}V\to\mathsf{L}
, with
\omega

commensurable to
\{g\in\widetilde{\Lambda}:\phi(g,0)\in U\}
, for
U
a compact open neighborhood of
1
in
L
.

Above, continuity refers to the product topology with
\widetilde{\Lambda}
discrete; it amounts to continuity on
1\times V
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.16
there exists a subgroup
\widetilde{\Lambda}\leq G
, a connected Lie group
\mathsf{L}
,
a normal compact subset
K
of a rigid abelian subgroup
A\leq\mathsf{L}
,
A\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
and a quasi-homomorphism

f:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{L}:K

with
\omega=\chi(f)
. We will identify
A
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, and write multiplication additively within
A
.
Rigidity of
A
means that there exists a homomorphism
\rho^{\prime}:\mathsf{L}\to O_{N}
with
x^{-1}ax=\rho^{\prime}(x)a
,
for all
x\in\mathsf{L},a\in A
. For
g\in\widetilde{\Lambda}
, let
\rho(g)=\rho^{\prime}(f(g))
. As
K\subset A
,
\phi(K)=1
and hence
\rho:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to O_{N}
is a homomorphism.
Let
V
be
A
with this
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-module structure.
Define
\alpha:\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}\to A={\mathbb{R}}^{N}
by
\alpha(x,y)=f(xy)-f(x)-f(y)
. It is easy to check that
d^{3}\alpha=0
in
C^{3}(\widetilde{\Lambda},\rho)
.
Since
\alpha(x,y)\in K
, it is bounded, i.e.
\alpha\in Z^{2}_{bdd}(\widetilde{\Lambda},\rho)
.

Define
\phi:\widetilde{\Lambda}\underset{\alpha}{\times}V\to\mathsf{H}
by
\phi(g,a)=f(g)a
. It is easy to check that
\phi
is a homomorphism, and that

\chi(f)\sim\{g\in\mathsf{L}:\phi(g,0)\in U\}
, for
U
a compact open neighborhood of
1
in
L
.

∎

Let
\beta
be the bounded cohomology class represented
by
\alpha
. If
\beta=0
, then
\omega
is
laminar: we have, above,
\alpha=d^{2}(b)
for some bounded
b:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to A
. Define
f^{\prime}(x)=f(x)b(x)^{-1}
.
Again it is easy to check that
f^{\prime}:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{L}
is a homomorphism. As
b(\widetilde{\Lambda})
is a bounded subset of
A
, and hence
a precompact subset of
\mathsf{L}
, we have
\chi(f)=\chi(f^{\prime})
. This shows that
\omega
is laminar.

Similarly, if
\beta
is represented by
\alpha
and also by
\alpha^{\prime}
, then the corresponding approximate subgroups are
commensurable.

An alternative description can be given in terms of cohomological data on
G
and on the quotient group
\bar{L}=\mathsf{L}/A
.
In this case, the
data consists of a homomorphism
\bar{\phi}:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\bar{L}
, a finite-dimensional Euclidean space
V
, a homomorphism

\rho:\bar{L}\to O_{n}=Aut(V)
, a class
\beta\in H^{2}_{b}(G,V)
, agreeing in
H^{2}(G,V)
with the
\bar{\phi}
-pullback
of the class describing the extension
L
of
\bar{L}
. More precisely:

Corollary 5.26
.

Let
G
be a group,
\omega
a commensurability class of approximate subgroups of
G
.
Then
\omega=\chi(\bar{f},\bar{L},L,\beta)
is determined by:

•

A connected Lie group
L
with no compact normal subgroup;

•

A rigid abelian normal subgroup
A
of
L
; let
\bar{L}=L/A
, and let
M
be the
\bar{L}
-module
A
.
Let
\alpha\in H^{2}(\bar{L},A)
be the cohomology class corresponding to the extension
1\to A\to L\to\bar{L}\to 1
,
viewed as discrete groups.

•

A subgroup
H
of
G
, to which
\omega
belongs.

•

A homomorphism
\bar{f}:H\to\bar{L}
.

•

A class
\beta\in H^{2}_{b}(H,M)
, such that the image of
\alpha
in
H^{2}(H,M)
coincides with the image

(\bar{f}|H)^{*}\beta
of
\beta
in
H^{2}(H,M)
.

\omega
is laminar if
\beta=0
.

In particular if
H^{2}_{b}(H,M)=0
for all
H
such that
\omega
belongs to
H
, then
\omega
is laminar.

Except perhaps for the choice of
H
, that is determined only up to finite index,
