is an open subgroup of
cl({\Gamma A})
. In particular,
C=cl({\Gamma A})^{0}=S^{0}\leq S
and so
C
is solvable.

In particular the first projection
\pi_{1}(C)
is soluble. Since
C
is normalized by
{\Gamma A}
,
\pi_{1}(C)

is normalized by
{H}
. By assumption (i),
\pi_{1}(C)=(1)
. Thus
C=(1)\times I
for some
I
, which is clearly
a closed subgroup of
L
.
I
is normalized by
\pi_{2}(\Gamma)Z(L)
, which is dense in
L
by assumption. So
I
is normal
in
L
; and
I
is soluble.

*

It remains only to prove that the induced homomorphism
f_{1}:{H}\to L/I
is discrete-on-compact.
By Claim 1,
f^{-1}(I)
is soluble, and is normalized by
{H}
; hence by assumption (i),
f^{-1}(I)=(1)
and so
f_{1}
is
injective. So we are in position to use Lemma
A.6
(3); we thus need to show, for one compact neighborhood
W^{\prime}\subset G
, that
f_{1}(W^{\prime}\cap{H})
is discrete in
L/I
.

Let us write also
{\Gamma}I
for
{\Gamma}(1\times I)
.
Since
cl({\Gamma A})
contains
cl({\Gamma})
and
(1\times I)
, and is closed; and on the other hand
A\subset I
, we
have
cl({\Gamma A})=cl({\Gamma}I)
.

We saw that
cl({\Gamma A})^{0}=1\times I
. The group
cl({\Gamma A})/(1\times I)

is thus totally disconnected. So it has a profinite open subgroup, that we can write as
P^{\prime}/(1\times I)
, with
P^{\prime}
an open subgroup of
cl({\Gamma A})
.
The image of
P^{\prime}
in
L/I
, namely
\pi_{2}(P^{\prime})/I
, is also profinite, in particular totally disconnected; but by the Von-Neumann-Cartan theorem, being a closed subgroup of
L/I
, it is itself a Lie group.
Thus
\pi_{2}(P^{\prime})/I
is finite; so
\pi_{2}^{-1}(I)\cap P^{\prime}
has finite index in
P^{\prime}
; being closed, it is an open subgroup,
hence open in
cl({\Gamma A})
too. Let
P=\{a:(a,1)\in P^{\prime}\}
; then
P\times I
is a clopen subgroup of
cl({\Gamma}I)
.

Let us now show that
f_{1}(W^{\prime})
is discrete. Otherwise, some subsequence of distinct points of
f_{1}(W^{\prime})
has a limit point
in
L/I
; and it follows that some sequence of distinct points from
(W^{\prime})(W^{\prime})^{-1}
converges to
1
in
L/I
.
Refining further ,using compactness of
W^{\prime}
and of
W^{\prime\prime}=(W^{\prime})(W^{\prime})^{-1}
, we find
a sequence
(a_{i})
of elements of
{H}\cap W^{\prime\prime}
, converging to some
a\in W^{\prime\prime}
, such that
f_{1}(a_{i})\to 1
.
Let
U^{\prime}
be a precompact open neighborhood of
1
in
L
; then
U^{\prime}I/I
is an open subset of
L/I
, containing
\bar{b}
;
so
f(a_{i})\in U^{\prime}I
for almost all
i
;
f(a_{i})=u_{i}c_{i}
, with
c_{i}\in I
and
u_{i}\in U^{\prime}
. We have
(a_{i},u_{i}c_{i})\in\Gamma
,
so
(a_{i},u_{i})\in{\Gamma}I
. Since
U^{\prime}
is precompact, we may assume
u_{i}\to u\in cl(U^{\prime})
. Then
(a,u)\in cl({\Gamma}I)
.
Since
P\times I
is open in
cl({\Gamma}I)
, almost all pairs
(a_{i},u_{i})
lie
in a single coset of
P\times I
. In particular, the
u_{i}
lie eventually in a single coset
of
I
; this implies that modulo
I
they are finite in number. This contradicts the assumption of distinctness of the
elements
f_{1}(a_{i})=f(a_{i})I=u_{i}I
, and
ends the proof of Claim 2.

∎

*

We return to the proof of the Proposition.
The composed map
{H}\to L\to L/A
is a group homomorphism, injective by Claim 1. Since
I/A
is solvable,
f^{-1}(I)
is
solvable. By (i) we have:

f^{-1}(I)=1
. Let
A_{1}=I
and let
f_{1}:{H}\to L/A_{1}
be the composition
{H}\to L\to L/A_{1}
. So
f_{1}
is injective. By Claim 2,
f_{1}
is discrete-on-compact.

We have now achieved the main goal, of replacing the quasi-homomorphism
f
by an actual homomorphism
f_{1}
,
still discrete-on-compact. Moreover
f_{1}
is injective. We still wish to change it further so that the target Lie group becomes semisimple
and centerless. To this end we repeat the procedure inductively, constructing a sequence
A_{1}\leq A_{2}\leq\cdots

of closed normal subgroups of
L
, such that
f
induces a discrete-on-compact, injective homomorphism
f_{k}:G\to L/A_{k}
.

Assume
A_{k},f_{k}
have been defined and satisfy these conditions. We proceed as follows.
In case
L/A_{k}
has a nontrivial center, let
A_{k+1}/A_{k}
be the center of
L/A_{k}
.
Then the composed homomorphism
f_{k+1}:{H}\to L/A_{k+1}
is discrete-on-compact by Claim (2),
applied to
f_{k}
.

The kernel of
f_{k+1}
is an abelian normal subgroup of
{H}
, hence trivial by assumption (i).

Assume then that
A_{k+1}/A_{k}
has trivial center.
If
L/A_{k}
has a nontrivial normal compact subgroup, choose
A_{k+1}
to be such a subgroup. Then
f_{k+1}:{H}\to L/A_{k+1}

remains discrete-on-compact trivially, since a pullback of a compact from
L/A_{k+1}
to
L/A_{k}
is compact.
The kernel, being the pullback of a compact, is discrete in
G
, and normal in
H
, hence must by trivial by (ii). (Note

f_{k}
is an injective homomorphism at this point, i.e.
K=1
.)

Assume therefore that
L/A_{k}
has no such normal subgroups.

If
L/A_{k}
has a nontrivial normal closed connected abelian subgroup, let
A^{\prime}/A_{k}
be such a subgroup.
Then
A^{\prime}/A_{k}

is a connected locally compact abelian group so it is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times B
with
B
a maximal compact subgroup.
If
B^{\prime}
is a
G
-conjugate of
B
, then
BB^{\prime}
is also compact, so
BB^{\prime}=B
. It follows that
B
is normal in
G
,
so should have been chosen in the previous step, unless
B=1
. Thus
B=1
so
A^{\prime}/A_{k}
is a vector group.
In this case the hypotheses of the Proposition apply to
G,{H}
with
f_{k}:{H}\to L/A_{k}
and
A^{\prime}/A_{k}
in place of
f
and
A
.
By Claim 2 (now with
K=1
), there exists a closed, solvable normal subgroup
A_{k+1}
of
L
containing
A^{\prime}
,
such that the induced map
f_{k+1}:{H}\to L/A_{k+1}
is discrete-on-compact. Again the kernel of
f_{k+1}
is soluble and so trivial by assumption (i).

Assume
L/A_{k}
has no normal subgroups of the types considered so far. If
L/A_{k}
has a nontrivial normal closed abelian subgroup
A^{\prime}
, the connected component of
A^{\prime}
is trivial. Since
A^{\prime}
is a Lie group, it is discrete, and so central, returning to a previously considered case.

The remaining possibility is that
L/A_{k}
has no nontrivial closed normal subgroups that are either abelian or compact. When this
occurs the construction terminates.

Note that
\dim(L/A_{k})\geq\dim(L/A_{k+1})
; equality holds only in case
A_{k+1}/A_{k}
is discrete; this cannot recur twice in a row, since if
A_{k+2}/A_{k+1}
and
A_{k+1}/A_{k}
are both
discrete then so is
A_{k+2}/A_{k}
, and it is central in
L/A_{k}
by connectedness, so
A_{k+2}
would have been chosen
already at stage
k
. Thus
\dim(L/A_{k})
descends and strictly so at every other step, so the construction proceeds at most
2\dim(L)
times.

If
A_{k}
is the last stage, then
f_{k}:{H}\to L/A_{k}
is a discrete-on-compact homomorphism to a centerless Lie group, with no normal abelian subgroups, hence semisimple, and with no compact normal subgroups.
∎

When
\Gamma
is a discrete subgroup of a connected Lie group
G
, Claim 2 of
Proposition
6.12
is the “Zassenhaus Lemma” of
[
1
]
.
Curiously, though Auslander explicitly invokes an effective version of (**),
his proof appears to make no use of it; he uses rather the exponential shrinking within
A
due to (2).
In the approximate situation, (2) cannot be used in this way since
upon iterated approximate commutation, the
A
-component remains bounded but need not
approach
0
. We used instead, after some rearrangement, the convergence of the elements
u_{i}
towards
1
.

7.
Arithmeticity, recognition, extensions

Let
K
be a global field.
Let
{\mathbb{A}}={\mathbb{A}}_{K}
be the adele ring of
K
. If
S
is a
finite set of places of
K
, we may write

{\mathbb{A}}={{\mathbb{A}}_{S}}\times{\mathbb{A}}^{\prime}_{S}
, where
{{\mathbb{A}}_{S}}=\Pi_{v\in S}K_{v}
, and
{\mathbb{A}}^{\prime}_{S}
is the restricted direct product
of the
K_{v},v\notin S
. Let
\pi_{S}:{\mathbb{A}}\to{{\mathbb{A}}_{S}}
be the projection.

Definition 7.1

(Arithmetic approximate lattice)
.

Let
K
be a global field,
H

a connected, almost simple algebraic
K
-subgroup of
SL_{n}
.
Let
C
be a compact open neighborhood of
1
in
H({\mathbb{A}}^{\prime}_{S})
, and

\Lambda=\pi_{S}(H(K)\cap({{\mathbb{A}}_{S}}\times C)).

If
\psi:G\to H({{\mathbb{A}}_{S}})
is a topological group isomorphism,
we refer to
\psi^{-1}(\Lambda)
as an
arithmetic approximate lattice
.

An
arithmetic Meyer set
(in
H({{\mathbb{A}}_{S}})
) is a subset of an arithmetic approximate lattice, commensurable to it.

Remark 7.2
.

If one demands that
S
contain all archimedean primes
v
where

H(K_{v})
is not compact, one may (without changing the commensurability class) choose
C
to be a subgroup;
in that case one obtains the definition of an arithmetic lattice. (
[
48
]
p.1)

Remarks 7.3
.

(1)

If interested only in the approximate subgroup
\Lambda
, one can take a finite set
S^{\prime\prime}=S\sqcup S^{\prime}
; let
{\mathcal{O}}_{K,S^{\prime\prime}}
be the ring of
S^{\prime\prime}
-integers in
K
; let
C
be a compact neighborhood of
1
in
H({\mathbb{A}}_{S^{\prime}})

and let
\Lambda=\pi_{S}(H({\mathcal{O}}_{K,S^{\prime\prime}})\cap({{\mathbb{A}}_{S}}\times C))
. One can take
S^{\prime}
to consist just of archimedean primes, if desired.
Since
K
embeds into
{{\mathbb{A}}_{S}}
, and also into
{\mathbb{A}}_{S^{\prime}}
, we have a map
f:H({\mathcal{O}}_{K,S^{\prime\prime}})\to H({\mathbb{A}}_{S}^{\prime})
,
showing incidentally that
\Lambda
is laminar.

(2)

On the other hand, consider a pair
(\Lambda,\widetilde{\Lambda})
, where

\widetilde{\Lambda}
is any subgroup of
G
commensurating
\Lambda
and containing
\Lambda
. If
(G,\Lambda)
is arithmetic,
laminarity is also shown by a homomorphism
f:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to H({\mathbb{A}}_{K})
, with
\Lambda\in\chi(f)
,
and
f(\widetilde{\Lambda})\leq H(K)
.
This is because

\widetilde{\Lambda}\leq H(K)
(Lemma
A.19
), and by Definition
7.1
,
\Lambda=j^{-1}(C)
where
j:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to H(K)
is the inclusion.

For this, if
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is not finitely generated, a finite product would not suffice.

Note here that if
C
is chosen to contain
H({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{v}})
whenever
v
is non-archimedean, then
f(\widetilde{\Lambda})

contains
H({\mathcal{O}}_{K})
.

Take the Weil restriction of scalars
H^{\prime}
of
H
from
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
to
{\mathbb{Z}}
.
9
9

9

{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}

as an abelian group, and so a choice of basis gives a polynomial interpretation of the ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
over the ring
{\mathbb{Z}}
,
with universe
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
. Any affine scheme
W\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{N}
over
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
becomes interpreted as
W^{*}\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{Nm}

over
{\mathbb{Z}}
, in such a way that
W({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
can be identified with
W^{*}({\mathbb{Z}})
.
We obtain a homomorphism
h:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to H^{\prime}({\mathbb{A}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}})

with
\Lambda\in\chi(f)
, and
H^{\prime}({\mathbb{Z}})\leq h(\widetilde{\Lambda})\leq H^{\prime}({\mathbb{Q}})
.

For simplicity we restrict to groups of adjoint type below. This allows us to use the simpler definition of ‘arithmetic lattice’
in
[
48
]
, p.1; and it is convenient to have centerless groups.
For classification up to commensurability this is not a real issue;
If
G
is a group with finite center
Z
,
\pi:G\to G/Z
the projection, then any
X\subset G
is commensurable with

\pi^{-1}(\pi(X))
.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
G
be a finite product of noncompact, adjoint,
simple algebraic groups over local fields. Then any approximate lattice
\Lambda_{0}
in
G
is commensurable with a
product
\Lambda
of lattices and arithmetic approximate lattices of direct factors of
G
.

Supplements

•

We may choose
\Lambda\subset\langle\Lambda_{0}\rangle
.

•

In case no lattices appear in the product,
\Lambda_{0}
is an arithmetic Meyer set.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.11
,
\Lambda_{0}
is commensurable to a cut-and-project set of a lattice
\Gamma\leq G\times\mathsf{L}
.
Here
\mathsf{L}
is a centerless semisimple Lie group, and can be viewed as the connected component (of finite index) in a semisimple
adjoint algebraic group over
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Passing to a finite
index sublattice of
\Gamma
if necessary,
we can express
\Gamma
as a product of irreducible lattices
\Gamma_{i}\leq G_{i}\times\mathsf{L}_{i}
, where
G=\Pi_{i}G_{i}

and
\mathsf{L}=\Pi_{i}\mathsf{L}_{i}
; in the sense of
[
48
]
, p. 1. If
U_{i}
is a compact open neighborhood of
1
in
\mathsf{L}_{i}
,
the cut-and -project sets in
G
for
\Gamma
and
\Pi_{i}U_{i}
is the product of the corresponding sets for
\Gamma_{i}
and
U_{i}
.
Thus
\Lambda_{0}
is commensurable to the cut-and-project set of
\Gamma
with respect to
\Pi_{i}U_{i}
. From this it is also
clear that
\Lambda_{0}
is commensurable to
\Pi_{i}(\pi_{i}(\Lambda_{0}))
; moreover evidently
\Gamma\subset\Pi_{i}(\pi_{i}(\Lambda_{0}))
.
Thus proving the result for each
\Lambda_{i}
will suffice, so
we may restrict attention to a single such factor; renaming, we take
\Gamma
to be an irreducible lattice
in
G\times\mathsf{L}
.

In case
\mathsf{L}
is trivial,
\Gamma
is a lattice in
G
, commensurable with
\Lambda_{0}
, and there is nothing further
to show. Assume from now on that
\mathsf{L}
(and
G
) are nontrivial. Then,

G\times\mathsf{L}
has rank at least
2
, and so Theorem 1 of
[
48
]
, p. 2 applies: in the notation of Definition
7.1
, we may identify
G
with
H(K_{S})
,
\Gamma
with
H(K)
as embedded diagonally in
H(K_{S})\times H({\mathbb{A}}^{\prime}_{S})

and
\Lambda
with
\pi_{1}(\Gamma\cap(H(K_{S})\times C))
, with
C
a compact open neighborhood of
1
in
H({\mathbb{A}}^{\prime}_{S})
.
This yields the arithmeticity of
\Gamma
.

It remains to show that
\Lambda_{0}
, is contained in a cut-and-project set
\Lambda
of
\Gamma
.
We have in any case

\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda F_{0}
for some finite set
F_{0}
. Note that one can take
F_{0}\subset\Lambda_{0}\Lambda
.
By Lemma
5.1
,
F_{0}\subset\Lambda_{0}\Lambda\subset{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)
.

By Borel density (§
A.12
),
\pi_{1}(H(K)\cap(H(K_{S})\times C))
is Zariski dense in
H
. (It is here
that we use the assumption that
G=H(K_{S})
has no compact factors.) By Lemma
A.19
,

{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)\leq H(K)
.
So
F_{0}\subset H(K)
.
Now we may view
F_{0}
as a subset
of
H({\mathbb{A}}^{\prime}_{S})
as well; and it is clear that
F_{0}C
is compact and
\Lambda_{0}\subset\pi(H(K)\cap(K_{S}\times F_{0}C))
.

∎

Corollary 7.5
.

Let
G
be a semisimple algebraic group over a local field of positive characteristic, or a finite product of such groups. Then every approximate lattice in
G
is commensurable with a lattice.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.4
, any approximate lattice in
G
is commensurable with a product of lattices, and arithmetic approximate subgroups. In the arithmetic case, the field
K
will also be of positive characteristic (say by Borel-Tits), and so
the complementary
H({\mathbb{A}}_{S}^{\prime})
will be totally disconnected. One can thus take the compact open neighborhood

C\subset H({\mathbb{A}}_{S}^{\prime})
to be a subgroup; and in this case, the cut-and-project set is a lattice. ∎

7.6.
Recognition

We will find that an abstractly semisimple locally compact group, possessing a strictly dense
approximate lattice, is itself of adelic origin. The method of proof involves two uses of complementarity, relating two locally compact groups by bouncing off an
approximate subgroup of a finite dimensional Lie group.
Margulis arithmeticity is invoked, this time also via the commensurator criterion. Abstract semisimplicity is only used
in ordre to invoke Theorem
7.4
; laminarity of the approximate lattice could be assumed instead.

As mentioned in the introduction, Theorem
7.7
and Remark
7.8
essentially go over earlier results of
Bader, Furman and Sauer
[
3
]
. They use lattices in place of approximate lattices, and make the
semisimplicity assumption only on the archimedean factor, but assume the lattice embeds into that factor.
Using Theorem
7.4
, it is possible deduce a version of Theorem
7.7
from
[
3
]
. We retain it as an example of the flexibility of the approximate lattice framework.

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
G
be an abstractly semisimple locally compact group.
Let
{\lambda}
be a strictly dense commensurability class of approximate lattices in
G
. Then
there exists a semisimple algebraic group
H
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, and a subgroup
M
of
H({\mathbb{Q}})
containing

H({\mathbb{Z}})
, such that
G
is compactly isogenous to the closure
T
of
M
in

H({\mathbb{A}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.9
there exists a connected semisimple real Lie group
\mathsf{L}
and a lattice

\Gamma\leq G\times\mathsf{L}
such that
\pi_{1}:\Gamma\to G
is injective,

\pi_{2}:\Gamma\to\mathsf{L}
is injective with dense image; and with
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
and

f=\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1}^{-1}
, we have
{\lambda}=\chi(f)
. Moreover
\mathsf{L}
is centerless has no nontrivial compact normal subgroups.

By the strict density of
{\lambda}
,
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is dense in
G
.
Let
\widetilde{\Omega}=f(\widetilde{\Lambda})
. Then
\Gamma
is also
the graph of a map
f^{-1}:\widetilde{\Omega}\to\widetilde{\Lambda}\subset G
.

Now let
W
be a compact neighborhood of
1
in
G
, and let
\Omega=f(W\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})
. By Lemma
A.8
,
applied on the right this time,

\Omega
is an approximate lattice in
\mathsf{L}
.

We now apply Theorem
7.4
to
(\mathsf{L},\Omega)
. We find a direct product decomposition
\mathsf{L}=\Pi_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}
, and approximate
lattices
\Omega_{i}\leq L_{i}
with
\Pi_{i}\Omega_{i}
commensurable with
\Omega
, such that each
\Omega_{i}
is an irreducible lattice,
or an arithmetic approximate lattice. Let
\pi_{i}:\mathsf{L}\to L_{i}
be the projection.

Since
f
has dense image, so does
\pi_{i}f
for each
i
. As
\Omega_{i}
is discrete,
\pi_{i}\widetilde{\Omega}
and
\Omega_{i}

cannot be commensurable. Hence in case
\Omega_{i}
is a lattice, while it may have rank one, it is still arithmetic,
by the commensurator criterion of Theorem 1 of
[
48
]
(p.2; note finite generation is automatic in characteristic zero, according to point (b)).

The fundamental commensurability class of the locally compact group
G
is preserved under
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-conjugation;
hence the commensurability class of
\Omega
is preserved under
\widetilde{\Omega}
-conjugation. Thus
\widetilde{\Omega}\subset{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Pi_{i}\Omega_{i})
. Let
\widetilde{\Omega}_{i}=\pi_{i}(\widetilde{\Omega})
. It follows that
\widetilde{\Omega}_{i}\leq{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Omega_{i})
.

By Remark
7.3
(2), there exist homomorphisms
h_{i}:\widetilde{\Omega}_{i}\to H_{i}({\mathbb{A}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}})
with image between
H_{i}({\mathbb{Z}})

and
H_{i}({\mathbb{Q}})
, and with

\Omega_{i}\in\chi(h_{i})
.
Let
H=\Pi_{i}H_{i}
and
h=\Pi_{i}h_{i}
; then
\Omega\in\chi(h)
, and
H({\mathbb{Z}})\leq h(\widetilde{\Omega})\leq H({\mathbb{Q}})
. Let

M=h(\widetilde{\Omega})
and
T=cl(M)
. Then
h:\widetilde{\Omega}\to T
is a homomorphism with dense image and
\Omega\in\chi(h)
.

But we already know a homomorphism with dense image from
\widetilde{\Omega}
to a locally compact group and canonical commensurability class
\Omega

namely
f^{-1}:\widetilde{\Omega}\to G
. By Proposition
5.5
,
T
and
G
are compactly isogenous, by an isogeny respecting
\widetilde{\Omega}
.

∎

*

Let us refer to abstractly semisimple locally compact groups
T
of the form appearing in Theorem
7.7

as
Margulis groups
.

Remark 7.8
.

Up to compact isogeny,
and up to subgroups of profinite index, any Margulis group is itself a restricted product of semisimple groups over
{\mathbb{R}}
and the
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, with respect to the open subgroups
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
.

Proof.

By the proof of Remark
6.6
(
5
), after factoring out a compact normal subgroup,
we have
T=T_{na}\times T_{ar}
, where
T_{ar}
is a connected semisimple Lie group,
and
T_{na}
is the closure of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
in
H({\mathbb{A}}_{fin})
, where
A_{fin}=\Pi^{\prime}_{p}H({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
are the finite adeles. It suffices to prove the statement for
T_{na}
.

T_{na}
contains the closure of
H({\mathbb{Z}})
in
H({\mathbb{A}}_{fin})
, which in turn contains an open subgroup of
\Pi_{p}H({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
.
Hence
T_{na}
is open in
H({\mathbb{A}}_{fin})
. We may take
H
to be centerless, and a product of groups
H_{1},\ldots,H_{m}

that are almost
{\mathbb{Q}}
-simple; rewrite the restricted product as
\Pi^{\prime}_{q}H_{q}
, where
q=(p,j)
and
H_{q}=H_{j}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.
Let
\pi_{q}:{\mathbb{A}}\to H_{q}
be the projection.
Let
I
be the set of
q

such that
\pi_{q}(T)
is not compact.
Let
T^{\prime}
be the projection of
T
to
\Pi^{\prime}_{q\in I}H_{q}
,. The kernel of
T\to T^{\prime}
is compact. For
q=(p,j)\in I
, the projection
\pi_{q}(T)
is open and unbounded in
H_{j}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. Hence by the theorem of Tits in the title of
[
60
]
, Theorem T,

\pi_{q}(T)
contains
H_{j}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{+}
. Since the groups
H_{j}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{+}
are simple and non-isomorphic,
the image of
T^{\prime}
in any finite product
\Pi_{q\in I_{0}}H_{q}
must contain
\Pi_{q\in I_{0}}H_{q}^{+}
. But
T^{\prime}
is open, hence closed, and so it contains the full restricted product
\Pi^{\prime}_{q\in I}H_{q}^{+}
.
∎

This gives in particular an analogue for approximate lattices of some of the results of
[
57
]
.

As I learned from
Emmanuel Breuillard, many examples of abstractly semisimple groups not embeddable in a
restricted product of semisimple algebraic groups are known, including certain automorphism groups of trees and non-
p
-adic
analytic pro-
p
groups. Such groups thus do not contain a strictly approximate lattice.

7.9.
A non-laminar approximate lattice

Let
\Lambda=SL_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}[1/p])
, a lattice in
G=SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\times SL_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.
We will define a central extension
\hat{G}
of
G
by
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and
a certain extension of
\Lambda

by a laminar approximate lattice in
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, yielding an approximate lattice in
\hat{G}
that is not laminar.

The group
SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
has a nontrivial central extension
\widetilde{SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}
, determined by a bounded
2
-cocycle
\beta
taking values
in
\{-1,0,1\}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
. (See e.g.
[
31
]
for an o-minimal approach; see also the general theorem on characteristic classes in
[
23
]
1.3, p. 231.) We will at different points view
\beta
as taking values in
{\mathbb{R}}
, in
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

and in
{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p]
.

Claim .
For any index subgroup
\Lambda^{\prime}
of
\Lambda
,
\beta
induces a nontrivial class in
H^{2}_{bdd}(\Lambda^{\prime},{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p])
.

Proof.

We will prove the stronger claim, that
\beta
induces a nontrivial class in
H^{2}_{bdd}(\Lambda^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})
.

Consider the exact sequence:

1\to{\mathbb{Z}}\to\widetilde{SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\to SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\to 1

If we change coefficients from
{\mathbb{Z}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}
, the extension

1\to{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\widetilde{SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\to SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\to 1

remains nonsplit: the commutator subgroup of
\widetilde{SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}
is the entire group so

\widetilde{SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}
admits no nontrivial
homomorphisms to
{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus
\beta
represents a nonzero cohomology class in
H^{2}(SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}}),{\mathbb{R}})
;
and a fortiori in the bounded cohomology group
H^{2}_{bdd}(SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}}),{\mathbb{R}})
.

Define
\beta_{1}
on
G=SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\times SL_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
by
\beta_{1}(x,y)=\beta(x)
. Then
\beta_{1}
represents
a nonsplit extension

1\to{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\widetilde{SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\times SL_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\to G\to 1

so
\beta_{1}
has a nonzero class in

H^{2}_{bdd}(G,{\mathbb{R}})
. By Theorem 1.4 of
[
51
]
, since
2<4=2rk(G)
,
and
\Lambda
is an irreducible lattice, the restriction map

H^{2}_{bdd}(G,{\mathbb{R}})\to H^{2}_{bdd}(\Lambda,{\mathbb{R}})

is an isomorphism. Hence
the restriction of
\beta
to
\Lambda
remains nonzero in
H^{2}_{bdd}(\Lambda,{\mathbb{R}})
, and by the same token, in
H^{2}_{bdd}(\Lambda^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})

for any finite index subgroup
\Lambda^{\prime}
of
\Lambda
(which is also an irreducible lattice.)
∎

Let
\hat{\Lambda}
be the central extension of
\Lambda
by
{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p]
determined by
\beta
,

1\leftarrow\Lambda\leftarrow\hat{\Lambda}\leftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p]\leftarrow 1

Concretely, we can let
\hat{\Lambda}=\Lambda\times{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p]
, with multiplication

(\lambda_{1},a_{1})(\lambda_{2},a_{2})=(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2},a_{1}+a_{2}+\beta(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))

Let
{\delta}(\lambda,a)=-a
, where now
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p]
is viewed as a real number. Then
{\delta}:\hat{\Lambda}\to{\mathbb{R}}:[-1,1]
is a quasimorphism. Let

\Delta
be the (symmetric) approximate kernel
\Delta=\{x:-2<{\delta}(x)<2\}
. By Proposition
5.12
,

\Delta
is an approximate subgroup of
\hat{\Lambda}
. We have
\pi_{1}(\Delta)=\Lambda
,
so if a subgroup
S
of
\hat{\Lambda}
contains an approximate subgroup commensurable to
\Delta
,
then
\pi_{1}(S)
has finite index in
\Lambda
, and thus
ZS
has finite index in
\hat{\Lambda}

(where
Z
is the central image of
{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p]
in
\hat{\Lambda}
). Let
\Lambda^{\prime}
be the image of
S
in
\Lambda
;
then
\Lambda^{\prime}
has finite index, and we saw in the Claim that
\beta
is not trivial in
H^{2}_{bdd}(\Lambda^{\prime})
.

Now if we had
\delta=b+h
on
S
, where
b
is bounded and
h
is a homomorphism, then
for
x,y\in\Lambda^{\prime}
, choosing
s,t
with
(x,s),(y,t)\in S
, we would have:

\beta(x,y)=\delta((x,s)(y,t))-\delta(x,s)-\delta(y,t)=b((x,s)(y,t))-b(x,s)-b(y,t)
, so that
\beta=db=0
as a class
in
H^{2}_{bdd}(\Lambda^{\prime})
. Hence

{\delta}
is not at bounded distance from a homomorphism on
S
.

By Proposition
5.29
,
the commensurability class of the approximate subgroup
\Delta
of
\hat{\Lambda}
is not laminar.

Now view
\beta
as a
2
-cocycle with values in
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, and construct in the same way a central extension

1\to{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\to\widehat{SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}}\to SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\to 1.

Here
\widehat{SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}}
is obtained from the universal covering group of
SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
by taking a fiber product with
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

over
{\mathbb{Z}}
. This also describes the locally compact group topology on
\widehat{SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}}
; it has an open subgroup isomorphic
to the inverse limit of coverings of
SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
of order
p^{n}
.

Taking products with
SL_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, we obtain

1\to{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\to\hat{G}\to G\to 1.

We can view the universe of
\hat{G}
as being
G\times{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, with multiplication twisted by
\beta
as above.
Then the product measure is a Haar measure; translation by an element of
\hat{G}
involves a certain transvection
on
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\times G
, and transvections are measure preserving. Thus the product measure is a Haar measure.

\Delta
is discrete in
\hat{G}
: suppose
(\lambda_{i},a_{i})\in\Delta
and they approach a limit point
(\lambda,a)\in\hat{G}
.
Them
\lambda_{i}\to\lambda\in G
; since
\Lambda
is discrete in
G
, we have
\lambda_{i}=\lambda\in\Lambda
for large
i
;
we may arrange by translation that
\lambda_{i}=\lambda=1
. In this case we have
a_{i}\to a\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
But
\{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p]:|a|_{\mathbb{R}}<2\}
is discrete in
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. Thus
a_{i}=a
for large
i
.

\Delta
is a lattice in
\hat{G}
:
if
C_{1}\subset G
is a Borel set of finite measure with
C_{1}\Lambda=G
, then
(C_{1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\hat{\Lambda}=\hat{G}
,
and of course
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\subset{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is compact and hence of finite measure.

Thus
\Delta
is a non-laminar approximate subgroup of

\hat{G}
.

7.10.
Approximate lattice extensions

Let
G
be a connected linear algebraic group over a local field
k
(the discussion extends naturally to a finite product of such groups.) Then
G
has soluble radical
R=R(G)
; it is a soluble Zariski closed normal subgroup, with
{\mathbf{G}}:=G/R
semisimple. One can either choose
R
connected, or such that
G/R
is centerless; the latter will be a little more convenient for us.
We define
G_{ss}=G/R
.

Trying to reformulate the Björklund-Hartnick problem, given the amenable and semisimple answers, we may first ask:

Question 7.11
.

Is
\Lambda/R
above always an approximate lattice in
G/R
?
For connected
G
at least, I am fairly sure, a slight modification (and simplification) of Lemma
6.12
gives a positive answer.

N.B.: Machado has answered this positively at least over characteristic zero local fields.

Let us consider the case where
{{\bf\Lambda}}:=\Lambda/R
is an approximate lattice; then we know it is a product of
arithmetic approximate lattices, and lattices. In case
\langle\Lambda\rangle
is dense in
G
(as is natural to assume),

\langle{{\bf\Lambda}}\rangle
will be dense in
G/R
, and then all the components of
{{\bf\Lambda}}
will be arithmetic. By Proposition
5.10
,
\langle{{\bf\Lambda}}\rangle
contains an element
\Gamma
of
{\langle\bar{\omega}\rangle}_{\min}
; and by pulling back such an element, we may
reduce to the case that
\langle\Lambda\rangle\in{\langle\bar{\omega}\rangle}_{\min}
. In this case,
\langle\Lambda\rangle
is an
S
-arithmetic group
(of course it is not a lattice of
G/R
, but of some other product of semisimple groups.) Now we may further ask:

Question 7.12
.

Let
{\mathbf{G}}
be a product of semisimple groups over local fields, and
{{\bf\Lambda}}
an approximate lattice in
{\mathbf{G}}
,
with
\langle{{\bf\Lambda}}\rangle
isomorphic to a given
S
-arithmetic group
\widetilde{\Lambda}
. When is it true that for all
G

with
G_{ss}\cong{\mathbf{G}}
,
every approximate lattice
\Lambda
of
G
with
\Lambda/R={{\bf\Lambda}}
is laminar?

7.13.

We sketch a possible argument that the answer to Question (
7.12
) is: just when:

(
\diamond
)

H^{2}_{b}(<{{\bf\Lambda}}>,V)=(0)
for all rigid actions of
<{{\bf\Lambda}}>
on Euclidean space
V={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, induced from continuous action of
G
.

If
\diamond
holds, taking into account that
H^{2}_{b}(<\Lambda>,V)=H^{2}_{b}(<{{\bf\Lambda}}>,V^{R})

(by the mapping theorem
[
23
]
p.40, extended to nonconstant coefficients,
[
58
]
, cf.
[
51
]
.),
the laminarity follows from Proposition
5.26
(using Proposition
5.10
for
H
.)

Conversely if
H^{2}_{b}({{\bf\Lambda}},V)\neq(0)
, let
\beta
be a nontrivial bounded
2
-cocycle, and use it to form an extension
\hat{\Lambda}
of
{{\bf\Lambda}}
by the module
V
. By the mapping theorem we have

H^{2}_{b}(\hat{\Lambda})=H^{2}_{b}(\Lambda)
canonically since
V
is amenable. Thus
pulled back to
\hat{\Lambda}
,
\beta
still represents a nontrivial class in
H^{2}_{b}(\hat{\Lambda})
;
but now it maps to
0
as a class in
H^{2}(\hat{\Lambda})
. Hence by the the discussion below
5.26
,
we find a nonlaminar approximate subgroup in a central extension of
\hat{\Lambda}
. It remains to
embed this in an appropriate algebraic group, as in
7.9
, so as to produce a negative answer for this instance of Question
7.12
.

The bounded cohomology of
S
-arithmetic groups, as
{{\bf\Lambda}}
must be here, is studied in depth in
[
11
]
,
[
51
]
, providing I believe both
instances where
H^{2}_{b}({{\bf\Lambda}},V)
is zero for all
V
, and otherwise.

Appendix A
Approximate lattices

In this Appendix,
G,H
denote second countable, locally compact groups;
\mu
will denote a left invariant measure
on
G
.

We develop the basics of approximate lattices under a slightly different (and more elementary) definition than given in
[
5
]
.
In the case of strong or cocompact approximate lattices, these results appear in
[
5
]
and
[
46
]
.

Definition A.1
.

An approximate subgroup
\Lambda\leq G
is an
approximate lattice
in
G
if it is discrete, and the equivalent conditions (1)-(3) of Proposition
A.2
hold.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
\Lambda
be a discrete approximate subgroup. Then the following properties are equivalent:

(1)

\Lambda D=G
for some Borel set
D
of finite measure.

(2)

There exists a finite bound on
\mu(C)
, over all Borel sets
C\subset G
with
\Lambda^{2}\cap CC^{-1}=(1)
;
i.e. such that multiplication is 1-1 on
\Lambda\times C
.

(3)

There is no Borel
C\subset G
with
\mu(C)=\infty
, and
\Lambda^{2}\cap CC^{-1}=(1)
.

If these conditions hold, then
G
is unimodular, i.e.
\mu
is also right invariant; and so the three dual conditions are also equivalent.

Proof.

Note that as
G
is second countable, the discrete subset
\Lambda
is countable.

Assume (1). Let
C
be a Borel set, with
\Lambda^{2}\cap C^{2}=(1)
.
We have
C\subset\cup_{a\in\Lambda}C_{a}
, where
C_{a}=(aD\cap C)
. By the invariance assumption,
\mu(C_{a})=\mu(a^{-1}C_{a})
.
The sets
a^{-1}C_{a}

are disjoint Borel subsets of
D
; so

\mu(C)\leq\sum_{a\in\Lambda}\mu(C_{a})=\sum_{a\in\Lambda}\mu(a^{-1}C_{a})\leq\mu(D).

(2) trivially implies (3).

Now assume (3). By Lemma
A.3
(applied to
X=\Lambda^{2}\smallsetminus(1)
,
Y=G
, with opposite multiplication) there exists a Borel
M
with
MM^{-1}\cap\Lambda^{2}=(1)
, and
G=\Lambda^{2}M
. By (3),
M
has finite volume. But
\Lambda^{2}\subset\Lambda F

for some finite
F
, so
G=\Lambda FM
, and
FM=\cup_{f\in F}fM
has finite volume.

To prove unimodularity, let
\rho(g)
be the constant, given by the uniqueness of the Haar measure,
such that for all Borel
C
we have
\mu(Cg)=\rho(g)\mu(C)
.
Pick a symmetric compact open neighborhood
C
of
1
, with
\Lambda^{2}\cap C^{2}=(1)
; this is possible since

\Lambda^{2}
is discrete. Then for any
g\in G
, we have
(Cg)(Cg)^{-1}=CC^{-1}
, so
\Lambda^{2}\cap(Cg)(Cg)^{-1}=(1)
. By (2)
there is an absolute bound on
\mu(Cg)
over all
g
. Hence
\rho
is bounded, and the image of
\rho
is a bounded subgroup of
{\mathbb{R}}^{>0}
, i.e.
1
.

∎

Unimodularity was also proved in
[
5
]
for strong approximate lattices, in the sense defined there.

The following statement on a Borel set of approximate representatives will be essential. See
[
40
]
for a study of much deeper
questions along this line.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
G
be a discrete or second countable locally compact group,
Y
a Borel subset, and
X
a discrete subset of
G
with
1\notin X
, and
X=X^{-1}
. A subset
B\subset Y
is called
X
-free if
B^{-1}B\cap X=\emptyset
, and maximally
X
-free if it is maximal with this property, i.e.
Y\subset BX
. Then there exists a Borel set
B\subset Y
that is maximally
X
-free.

Proof.

Claim 1.
Assume
Y
is a countable union of Borel subsets
Y_{n}
, and for each
n
, any Borel
Y^{\prime}\subset Y_{n}
has a maximally
X
-free Borel set. Then we can solve the problem inductively: having defined
B_{m}\subset Y_{m}
, let
Y_{n}^{\prime}=Y_{n}\setminus\cup_{m<n}B_{m}X
, and let

B_{n}
be a maximally
X
-free subset of
Y_{n}^{\prime}
. Then
B=\cup B_{n}
will be maximally
X
-free in
Y
.

In view of this, since
G
can be written as a countable union of compact neighborhoods, we may assume
Y
is precompact.

For any Borel
Z\subset Y
,
b^{-1}Z\cap X
is finite, since
X
is discrete in
G
and
Y
precompact. For a given
finite subset
H
of
X
, let

Z_{H}=\{b\in Z:b^{-1}Z\cap X=H\}.

Claim 2.
For any Borel
Z\subset G
, and any finite
H\subset X
, we can find
a maximally
X
-free Borel subset of
Z_{H}
, or of any given Borel subset of
Z_{H}
.

We prove Claim 2 by induction on
|H|
. For
|H|=0
,
Z_{H}
is already
X
-free, so any
Z\subset Z_{H}
is maximally

X
-free in
Z
.
Assume
|H|=n
. Let
Z^{\prime}=Z_{H}
. For
b\in Z^{\prime}
, we have
b^{-1}Z^{\prime}\cap X\subset H
. We can thus divide
Z^{\prime}

into finitely many sets, according to the value of
b^{-1}Z^{\prime}\cap X
. For any proper subset of
H^{\prime}
, the induction hypothesis
applies to
Z^{\prime}_{H^{\prime}}
. Thus it suffices to deal with
Z^{\prime}_{H}
. If
Z^{\prime}_{H}=(Z_{H})_{H}
is empty, there is nothing to prove.
Otherwise,
H
is a finite subgroup of
G
. To see this, let
a\in(Z_{H})_{H}
and let
x,y\in H
.
Then
ax\in Z_{H}
, so
axy\in Z
. It follows that
xy\in a^{-1}Z\cap X
; since
a\in Z_{H}
, we have
xy\in H
.
So
Z_{H}
is a union of
H
-orbits,
y^{-1}z\in X
iff
y,z
lie in the the same
H
-orbit. So given a Borel
W\subset Z_{H}
,
it suffices to find a set of representatives for the
H
-orbit equivalence relation restricted to
W
.
(for instance we can fix a Borel linear ordering on
G
, and choose the least element of
W
in each
H
-orbit.)
This proves the Claim for
(Z_{H})_{H^{\prime}}
for any
H^{\prime}\subset H
, and hence by Claim 1, it is valid for
Z_{H}
.

Now
X
, being
a discrete subset of a second-countable space, is countable; so there are only countably many possibilities for the finite set

b^{-1}Y\cap X
. We thus have a partition of
Y
into countably many Borel sets
Y_{H}
. By Claim (2), any
Borel subset of each of these
has a Borel maximally free set, and hence by Claim 1, so does
Y
. ∎

Lemma A.4
.

If an approximate lattice is contained in a set
\Lambda
with
\Lambda^{4}
discrete,
they are commensurable, and
\Lambda
is an approximate lattice too.

Proof.

Let
X
be an approximate lattice contained in
\Lambda
.
Let
U
be a symmetric compact neighborhood of
1
, small enough so that
U^{2}\cap\Lambda^{4}=(1)
.
Let
\{c_{i}:i\in I\}
be a maximal collection of elements of
\Lambda
, such that the sets
Xc_{i}
are disjoint.
If
x,x^{\prime}\in X,i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
u,u^{\prime}\in U
and
xc_{i}u=x^{\prime}c_{i^{\prime}}u^{\prime}
, then
u^{\prime}u^{-1}\in\Lambda^{4}
so
u=u^{\prime}
; then by the disjointness
of the sets
Xc_{i}
we have
i=i^{\prime}
; so finally
x=x^{\prime}
. In particular, the sets
c_{i}U
are disjoint; and
multiplication is 1-1 on
X\times(\cup_{i\in I}c_{i}U)
. Hence, using Proposition
A.2
(3),

|I|\,{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm vol\,}}}(U)=\sum_{i\in I}\mu(c_{i}U)=\mu(\cup_{i\in I}c_{i}U)<\infty.

So
I
is finite. From maximality of
I
, it follows by the usual argument
that
\Lambda\subset\cup_{i\in I}X^{-1}Xc_{i}
: if
d\in\Lambda
, then
Xc_{i}\cap Xd\neq\emptyset

for some
i
, so
d\in X^{-1}Xc_{i}
. As we assumed
X
is an approximate subgroup,
\Lambda
and
X
are commensurable.
Using condition Proposition
A.2
(2) it is clear that
\Lambda
is an approximate lattice.

∎

A.5.
Complementarity

Let
f\vdots G\to H
be a partial map between topological spaces, i.e. a map
f:X\to H
where
X\subset G
.
We will say that
f
is
discrete-on-compact
if the pullback of any (pre)compact set in
H
is discrete in
G
.

f
is
precompact-to-discrete
if whenever
C\subset G
is compact, then
f(C\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})
is discrete in
H
.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
G,H
be locally compact groups,
\widetilde{\Lambda}
a subgroup of
G
, and
f:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to H
a group homomorphism.

(1)

If
f
is discrete-on-compact, and
X\subset H
is compact, then
f^{-1}(X)
is uniformly discrete.

(2)

If
f
is discrete-on-compact, it is precompact-to-discrete.

(3)

Assume
G
is second countable, and
f
has finite kernel. Then the converse to (2) holds:
if
f
is precompact-to-discrete, then
f
is discrete-on-compact. In fact if
f(U\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})
is discrete in
H
for one neighborhood
U
of
1
in
G

then
f
is discrete-on-compact.

Proof.

(1) Clear since
f^{-1}(XX^{-1})
is discrete.

(2) In a locally compact space, to prove discreteness of
f(C\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})
, it suffices to show that
f(C\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})\cap C^{\prime}
is finite for every
compact
C^{\prime}
; this is clear since
f^{-1}(C^{\prime})
is discrete in
G
, so
C\cap f^{-1}(C^{\prime})
is finite.

(3) Reversing (2), given a compact
C^{\prime}\subset H
, we must show that
f^{-1}(C^{\prime})
is discrete; it suffices
to show that no 1-1 sequence
(c_{i}:i\in{\mathbb{N}})
of elements of
f^{-1}(C^{\prime})
converges to any
c\in G
. Indeed if
c_{i}\to c

then
\{c,c_{i}:i\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
is
compact, so
\{c_{i}:i\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
is precompact, and

\{f(c_{i}):i\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
is discrete in
H
; hence intersects
C^{\prime}
in a finite set; since
f
is finite-to-one,
\{c_{i}:i\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
is finite,
in contradiction to the assumption that the
c_{i}
are distinct.

To prove the stronger version, we may assume
that
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is dense in
G
. Let
U
be a nonempty open set with
f(U\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})
discrete; we will show that

f(D\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})
is discrete for any compact subset
D
of
G
. For each point
d\in D
,
let
\gamma(d)\in\widetilde{\Lambda}\cap Ud^{-1}
. Then
D\subset\cup_{d\in D}\gamma(d)^{-1}U
; so a finite number of translates
\gamma_{i}U

cover
D
, with
\gamma_{i}\in\widetilde{\Lambda}
. Hence
f(D\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})\subset\cup_{i}f(\gamma_{i})f(U\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})
; so
F(D\cap\widetilde{\Lambda})
is a finite union of discrete subsets of
H
and hence discrete in
H
.

∎

A.7.
Discrete-on-compact homomorphisms and Meyer sets

Let
G,H
be locally compact groups; let
\pi_{1}:G\times H\to G,\pi_{2}:G\times H\to H
be the projections.
Given a discrete subgroup
\Gamma\leq G\times H
such that
\pi_{1}
is injective on
\Gamma
, let
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
and
{f}=\pi_{2}\circ(\pi_{1}|\Gamma)^{-1}:\Lambda\to H
. Then
{f}
is discrete-on-compact. Indeed if
C\subset H
is compact,
and
U
any compact set in
G
, then
{f}^{-1}(C)\cap U
is finite since
(U\times C)\cap\Gamma
is finite;
hence
{f}^{-1}(C)
is discrete in
G
.
Conversely, if
{f}:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to H
is discrete-on-compact, then the graph of
{f}
is a discrete subgroup of
G\times H
.

The uniform part of the proposition below is Prop. 2.13 of
[
5
]
. We do not assume in advance that
H
is unimodular,
so that if
G
has an approximate lattice and
f
exists, then
H
is automatically unimodular.

Proposition A.8
.

Let
G,H
be second countable locally compact groups,
\widetilde{\Lambda}
be a subgroup of
G
,
f:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to H
a discrete-on-compact homomorphism with dense image. Let
\Gamma
be the graph of
f
, viewed as a subset of
G\times H
.
Let
\Lambda=f^{-1}(C)
, where
C
is a compact, symmetric subset of
H
with nonempty interior. Then:

(1)

\Gamma
is a lattice iff
\Lambda
is an approximate lattice.

(2)

\Gamma
is a uniform lattice iff
\Lambda
is a uniform approximate lattice.

Proof.

Assume
\Lambda
is an approximate lattice. Let
U
be a Borel subset of
G
of finite volume, with
\Lambda U=G
.
We will show that
\Gamma(U\times C^{2})=(G\times H)
. As
U\times C^{2}
has finite volume (and is compact if
U
is),
it follows that
\Gamma
is a lattice, and is uniform if
U
is compact.

To see that
(U\times C^{2})\Gamma=(G\times H)
, let
(g,h)\in G\times H
. By density,
f(a)\in hC
for some
a\in\widetilde{\Lambda}
. So
f(a)C\subset hC^{2}
.
Now
a\Lambda\cap gU^{-1}\neq\emptyset
since
g\in aG=a\Lambda U
.
Let
b\in a\Lambda\cap gU^{-1}
. Then
g\in bU
; and
f(b)\in f(a)f(\Lambda)\subset hC^{2}
, so
h\in f(b)C^{2}
.
Thus
(g,h)\in(b,f(b))(U\times C^{2})\subset\Gamma(U\times C^{2})
.

Now assume
\Lambda
is not a lattice. By Proposition
A.2
(3), there exists a
Borel
U\subset G
with
\mu(U)=\infty
, and
\Lambda^{2}\cap UU^{-1}=(1)
. In other words

f^{-1}(C)^{2}\cap UU^{-1}=(1)
, so
(U\times C)(U\times C)^{-1}\cap\Gamma=(1)
. Thus
\Gamma
is not a lattice.

In the uniform case, it remains to show that if
\Gamma
is a cocompact lattice,
then there exists a compact set of the form
U\times C
, with
(U\times C)\Gamma=(G\times H)
;
this is clear since any compact subset of
G\times H
is contained in a compact rectangle.
∎

Let us call a closed, symmetric neighborhood of
1
in a totally disconnected locally compact group
H

sensible
if it is contained in some compact open subgroup; more generally for locally compact
H
, call
C
sensible if the image
C/H^{0}

in
H/H^{0}
is sensible. In particular if
H
is connected, then any symmetric compact neighborhood of
1
is sensible.

Proposition A.9
.

Let
G,H
be locally compact,
\Gamma\leq G\times H
a lattice projecting densely to
H
and 1-1 to
G
. Let
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
,
f=\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1}^{-1}:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to H
.
Let
C
be a compact symmetric subset of
H
with nonempty interior,
\Lambda=f^{-1}(C)
, and
X\subset\widetilde{\Lambda}
a symmetric set commensurable with
\Lambda:=f^{-1}(C)
. Then
X\subset f^{-1}(C^{\prime})
for some compact
C^{\prime}\subset H
.

In case
C
is sensible,
\langle X\rangle
contains a subgroup of
\langle\Lambda\rangle
of finite index. Thus the commensurability class of
\Lambda
belongs to
\langle\Lambda\rangle
but not to any infinite index subgroup.

In particular
for any two sensible
C,C^{\prime}
, not only the approximate subgroups
f^{-1}(C),f^{-1}(C^{\prime})
but also the groups generated by them are commensurable.

Proof.

We have
X\subset\cup_{i=1}^{m}\Lambda a_{i}
, for some
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\in\widetilde{\Lambda}
; let

C^{\prime}=\cup_{i=1}^{m}Cf(a_{i})
. Clearly
X\subset f^{-1}(C^{\prime})
. At this point it is also clear that

X
is an approximate lattice in
G
. (
X^{2}\subset f^{-1}((C^{\prime})^{2})
and the later is commensurable with
f^{-1}(C)
, so
X

is an approximate subgroup. On the other hand
\Lambda\subset\cup_{i=1}^{l}Xb_{i}
so if
\Lambda M=G
with

M
Borel of finite measure, then
X(\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}\}M)=G
and
(\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}\}M)
has finite measure.)

We have
\Lambda\subset\cup_{i=1}^{l}Xb_{i}
. Since
C\subset\cup_{i=1}^{l}cl(fX)b_{i}
,
it follows from Baire category that
cl(f(X))
has nonempty interior.
Thus
cl(f(X))^{2}
contains a neighborhood of
1
in
H
.

Now assume
H
is connected. Then
H=\cup_{n}cl(f(X))^{n}
. So
f(\langle X\rangle)
is dense in
H
.
By Proposition
A.8
(1), the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
f|\langle X\rangle
is a lattice in
G\times H
.
By the (easier lattice case of) Lemma
A.4
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
has finite index in
\Gamma
. Projecting to
G
we see that
\langle X\rangle
has finite index in
\widetilde{\Lambda}
.

Finally, assume more generally that
C
is sensible. We may replace
X
by the commensurable set
X^{2}\cap\Lambda^{2}

(
5.7
); so we may assume that
X\subset\langle\Lambda\rangle
. By assumption,
C/H^{0}
is contained in an compact open subgroup
O/H^{0}
. For statements about
\Lambda=f^{-1}(C)
, and subsets of
\langle\Lambda\rangle
, we may as well replace
H
by
O
; so
we may assume
H/H^{0}
is compact. In this case every open subgroup of
H
has finite index in
H
. In particular

H^{\prime}:=cl(f(\langle X\rangle))
has finite index in
H
. As above, the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
f|\langle X\rangle
is a lattice in
G\times H^{\prime}
,
hence also a lattice in
G\times H
; and we conclude as in the previous paragraph. ∎

Remark A.10
.

It can also be shown that if
\Gamma\leq G\times H
is a lattice, with dense projection to
H
, then
there exists a Borel
M\subset G
of finite volume and a
compact

C\subset H
with
(M\times C)\Gamma=G\times H
.
(The proof starts similarly to Lemma
A.3
, then invokes 4.2, 4.5 of
[
40
]
.)
Thus the lack of uniformity in an irreducible lattice of a product can be relegated to any one of the factors.

This gives an alternative proof of Proposition
A.8
(2).

The same fact on representative sets is moreover true for approximate lattices.

A.11.
Borel-Wang density

Borel density is addressed in
[
6
]
for strong and for uniform approximate lattices. We will give a direct proof for approximate lattices in the sense we use here.

If
G=\Pi_{i}G_{i}
is a product or restricted product of algebraic groups over possibly different fields (viewed with this structure, disregarding possible isomorphisms between the fields), we will say that a subset
X\subset G
is
Zariski dense
if
the projection to each
G_{i}
is Zariski dense.

Proposition A.12
.

Let
G
be an algebraically connected
semisimple algebraic group with no anisotropic factors over a local field. Then every approximate lattice in
G
is
Zariski dense.

Likewise for finite or restricted products of such groups over possibly different local fields; an approximate lattice is not commensurably contained in
a product of Zariski closed subgroups, other than the entire ambient group.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda
be an approximate lattice in
G
, and let
C
be the Zariski closure. We have
1\in\Lambda=\Lambda^{-1}
so
1\in C=C^{-1}
. We have

\Lambda^{2}\subset\Lambda F
for some finite set
F
, so
C^{2}\subset CF
. Let
C_{1}
be an irreducible component of
C
of maximal dimension; then
C_{1}^{2}\subset C_{2}F
for some component
C_{2}
of
C
, and it follows that
C_{1}^{2}=C_{2}f
for some
f\in F
;
from this it follows easily that
C
is contained in a finite union of cosets
Hg_{i}
of a Zariski closed subgroup
H
of
G
,

g_{i}\in N_{G}(H)
, and that
\Lambda
is commensurable to
H
.

If
\Lambda
is a closed approximate subgroup of a locally compact group
G
, say
\Lambda
has
property S
if
for every symmetric neighborhood
U
of the identity in G and every element
g\in G
, there is a positive integer
n
such that

g^{n}\in U\Lambda^{2}U
. Equivalently, the sets
g^{n}U\Lambda
are not pairwise disjoint.
For closed subgroups, this agrees with the definition in
[
71
]
.

Now if
\Lambda
is an approximate lattice, (S) holds. To see this take
U
symmetric, with
U^{2}\cap\Lambda^{2}=(1)
.
If the sets
g^{n}U

are not pairwise disjoint, the condition holds trivially (using only
1\in\Lambda
). Thus we may assume they are
pairwise disjoint, so
\cup g^{n}U
is a set of infinite measure.
By Proposition
A.2
(3),
(\cup g^{n}U)^{-1}(\cup g^{n}U)\cap\Lambda^{2}\neq(1)
; so for some
n\geq m
,

(g^{n}U)^{-1}g^{m}U\cap\Lambda^{2}\neq(1)
. We have
n>m
since
U^{2}\cap\Lambda^{2}=(1)
.
Thus
Ug^{n-m}U\cap\Lambda^{2}\neq(1)
and
g^{n-m}\in U\Lambda^{2}U
, proving (S).

Of course this implies
that (S) holds for any subgroup
H
containing
\Lambda
.
According to the Borel-Wang density theorem for groups,
[
71
]
Corollary (1.4), if
H
is Zariski closed this implies that
H=G
.

In case
G
is a finite or restricted product of such groups
G_{i}
, in a product
\Pi_{i}G_{i}
, since
\Lambda
has property (S), it is
clear that each projection
\pi_{i}(S)
has property (S) too; it follows by definition that
\Lambda
is Zariski dense.
∎

A.13.
Product decomposition

The hypotheses of the following Proposition hold notably (using Proposition
A.12
) when
G_{1},G_{2}
are centerless, semisimple groups with no anisotropic factors over a local field.

Proposition A.14
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be centerless locally compact groups, both satisfying the chain condition for centralizers, and such that an approximate lattice is not contained in any proper centralizer subgroup.
Let
\Lambda
be an approximate lattice in
G=G_{1}\times G_{2}
.
Let
\pi_{i}:G\to G_{i}
be the projection, and let
\Lambda_{i}=G_{i}\cap\Lambda^{2}
(we identify
G_{1}
with
G_{1}\times 1
, notationally.)
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\pi_{2}(\Lambda)
is discrete in
G_{2}
.

(2)

\Lambda_{1}
is a approximate lattice in
G_{1}
.

(3)

C_{G_{1}}(\Lambda_{1})=1
.

(1’)

\pi_{1}(\Lambda)
is discrete in
G_{1}
.

(4)

\Lambda
is commensurable with a product of an approximate lattice in
G_{1}
and an approximate lattice in
G_{2}
.

Proof.

(
1\to 2
) Certainly
\Lambda_{1}
is discrete in
G_{1}
, since
\Lambda
is discrete. It is an approximate subgroup of
G_{1}

by §
5.7
. To show that it is an
approximate lattice, it remains to show that conditions (2) or (3) of Lemma
A.2
hold, i.e. that
\Lambda_{1}
has finite covolume.
We will check this at the same time for
\pi_{2}(\Lambda)
. Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be Haar measures on
G_{1},G_{2}
, and let
\mu=\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
be the product Haar measure on
G
.

Let
E_{i}
be any Borel subset of
G_{i}
, with
E_{1}^{2}\cap\Lambda_{1}=(1)
,
E_{2}^{2}\cap(\pi_{2}\Lambda)^{2}=(1)
.
Then
(E_{1}E_{2})^{2}\cap\Lambda^{2}=(1)
: if
e_{i}\in E_{i}^{2}
and
e_{1}e_{2}\in\Lambda^{2}
, then
e_{2}\in E_{2}^{2}\cap(\pi_{2}\Lambda)^{2}
; by assumption
e_{2}=1
, so
e_{1}\in\Lambda^{2}\cap G_{1}=\Lambda_{1}
, hence
e_{1}=1
.
Thus
E_{1}E_{2}
has finite (indeed bounded) measure. As
\mu(E_{1}E_{2})=\mu_{1}(E_{1})\mu_{2}(E_{2})
, by fixing some small open neighborhood
E_{2}
of
1
, we see that there is an absolute bound
on
\mu_{1}(E_{1})
and in any case it cannot be infinite. Hence
\Lambda_{1}
is an approximate lattice in
G_{1}
.

(
2\to 3
) Assume (2). By assumption, if
a\in G_{1}
and
C_{G_{1}}(a)\neq G_{1}
, then
C_{G_{1}}(a)

cannot contain
\Lambda_{1}
; so
a\notin C_{G_{1}}(\Lambda_{1})
. Thus
C_{G_{1}}(\Lambda_{1})
consists only of central elements of
G_{1}
, hence equals
(1)
.

(
3\to 1^{\prime}
) By the chain condition on centralizers in
G_{1}
and (3), there exists a finite
F\subset\Lambda_{1}
with
C_{G_{1}}(F)=(1)
. We now use
the argument of Lemma
5.31
. Let
a_{i}\in\pi_{1}(\Lambda)
, and suppose
a_{i}\to a\in G_{1}
, and let
f\in F
. We have

a_{i}fa_{i}^{-1}\in\Lambda^{4}
, and
a_{i}fa_{i}^{-1}\to afa^{-1}
.
Now
\Lambda^{4}
is discrete in
G
, so
a_{i}fa_{i}^{-1}=afa^{-1}
for large
i
. Thus
a^{-1}a_{i}\in Z_{G}(f)
for each
f\in F
.
So
a=a_{i}
for large
i
. This proves the discreteness of
\pi_{1}(\Lambda)
.

By repeating the circle, it is now clear that (1-3) are equivalent, and also equivalent to their duals (1’-3’) replacing
G_{1},G_{2}
.
Thus
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
are both approximate lattices, hence so is
\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}
. Clearly

\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}\subset\Lambda^{2}
. Since
\Lambda^{8}
is discrete, by Lemma
A.4
,
\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}
is
commensurable with
\Lambda
. (As is
\pi_{1}\Lambda\times\pi_{2}\Lambda
, incidentally.)

This gives (4). The implication (
4\to 1
) is clear.
∎

By Proposition
A.12
, the assumption that proper centralizer subgroups not contain a lattice is true for groups
G_{1},G_{2}
satisfying the hypothesis there.

In case only
G_{1}
satisfies the chain condition on centralizers, we still have the implications
1\to 2\to 3\to 1^{\prime}\to 2^{\prime}\to 3^{\prime}
,
and
2+2^{\prime}=4
. (So
2\to 4
.)

The Proposition justifies a definition. Let
G
be a connected, centerless, semisimple groups with no anisotropic factors over a local field.

Definition A.15
.

An approximate lattice
\Lambda
of
G
is
irreducible
if there is no closed normal subgroup
N
of
G

such that the image of
\Lambda
in
G/N
is discrete.

Corollary A.16
.

Let
G
be a finite product of noncompact simple algebraic groups over local fields. Then every approximate lattice of
G
is commensurable to a product
\Pi_{i=1}^{k}\Lambda_{i}
,
where
G=\Pi_{i=1}^{k}G_{i}
and
\Lambda_{i}
is an irreducible approximate lattice in
G_{i}
.

A.17.
Commensurator in algebraic groups

Lemma A.18
.

Let
L
be a field,
K
a perfect subfield,
{H}
a centerless, connected algebraic
group over
K
. Let
g\in H(L)
and assume
g^{-1}H(K)g\,\cap H(K)
is Zariski dense in
H(K)
. Then

g\in H(K)
.

Proof.

Let
D:=g^{-1}H(K)g\,\cap H(K)
. As
D
is Zariski dense,
C_{H}(D)=(1)
; hence
C_{H}(F)=(1)
for some finite set
\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}\}\subset D
;
we have
gd_{i}g^{-1}=b_{i}
with
d_{i},b_{i}\in{H}(K)
; this uniquely determines
g
in
{H}(L^{alg})
, so
g
is definable in

ACF_{K}
and hence lies in
{H}(K)

∎

Lemma A.19
.

Let
H
be a connected algebraic group over a perfect field
K
. Let
\Lambda
be
an approximate lattice, Zariski dense in
H(K)
. Let
L
be a field extension, and let
C={\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)

be the commensurator of
\Lambda
in
H(L)
.
Then
C\leq H(K)
.

Proof.

As noted in §
5.7
,
if
A,B
are commensurable approximate subgroups, they are both commensurable to
AA\cap BB
, hence
using connectedness of
H
,
AA\cap BB
is Zariski dense in
H
. Let
g\in{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)
.
Let
A=\Lambda
, and
B=g^{-1}\Lambda g
; since

A\subset H(K)
we have
A^{2}\subset H(K)
, and similarly
B^{2}\subset g^{-1}H(K)g
; so if

g\in H(L)
commensurates
\Lambda
, then
H(K)\cap gH(K)g^{-1}
is Zariski dense in
H
.
By Lemma
A.18
, this
implies that
g\in H(K)
. ∎

Question A.20
.

If
G_{1}\leq G_{2}
are locally compact groups, and
\Lambda
is an approximate lattice of
G_{2}
contained
in
G_{1}
, is
G_{1}
unimodular? Equivalently, is
\Lambda

an approximate lattice of
G_{1}
? The case
G_{1}\trianglelefteq G_{2}
is easy (in this case, carring a finite Haar measure,
G_{2}/G_{1}
is compact). But the general case seems to require a little care (if not a surprise).

Question A.21
.

Formulate an adelic version of Corollary
6.11
.

Question A.22
.

Clarify the relation to the Björklund - Hartnick definitions of approximate lattice.

N.B.: Simon Machado has shown that a strong approximate lattice is also a finite covolume approximate lattice; and
in turn a finite covolume approximate lattice is an approximate lattice in the sense of
[
5
]
. It follows from
[
5
]
and the results here
that in semisimple groups, the notions of strong and finite covolume approximate lattice coincode; but the general case remains open.

Question A.23
.

Let
\Lambda
be an approximate lattice in a non-amenable locally compact group.
Could
\Lambda
be definably amenable in a reasonable language, or even for that matter outright amenable in the sense of
[
46
]
? For
co-compact
\Lambda
,
[
5
]
answer negatively for metric amenability.

Appendix B
Categories of approximate spaces.

We will use no category theory in this paper, at least not explicitly.
Nevertheless it seems useful to describe a number of categories that clarify the difference between the automorphism group of the core, and the two Lascar groups.

B.1.
The categories
\mathsf{CorSp}
and
\mathsf{ApprSp}

Let
X,Y
denote locally compact, Hausdorff topological spaces. A
proper correspondence
is a closed subspace

S\leq X\times Y
such that the projections
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm pr\,}}}_{S,X}:S\to X
,
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm pr\,}}}_{S,Y}:S\to Y
are proper maps. If
S\subset X\times Y

and
T\subset Y\times Z
are proper correspondences, we let
S^{tr}=\{(y,x):(x,y)\in S\}
,
T\circ S=\{(x,z)\in X\times Z:(\exists y\in Y)(x,y)\in S\wedge(y,z)\in Z\}
. In case
X=Y=Z
and
S=T
, we write this as
S^{\circ 2}
, and similarly define higher
iterates
.

The objects of
\mathsf{CorSp}
are pairs
(X,A_{X})
with
X
a locally compact space, and
A_{X}\subset X^{2}
a
symmetric, reflexive proper correspondence. A
morphism

(X,A_{X})\to(Y,A_{Y})
is a
proper correspondence
S\leq X\times Y
such that
{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm pr\,}}}_{S,X}=X
and

S\circ A_{X}\circ S^{tr}\subset A_{Y}
.

B.2.
The category
\mathsf{ApprSp}

In
\mathsf{ApprSp}
, the objects are the same as in
\mathsf{CorSp}
;
the second condition on a morphism is weakened to:
S\circ A_{X}\circ S^{tr}\subset A_{Y}^{\circ n}
for some
n
;
and two morphisms
S,T:(X,A_{X})\to(Y,A_{Y})
are
identified
if
S\subset A_{Y}^{m}\circ T
for some
m
, and vice versa.

The map
X\mapsto(X,Id_{X})
extends to a fully faithful functor of the usual category of locally compact topological spaces, into
\mathsf{ApprSp}
. The more general objects of
\mathsf{ApprSp}
, of the form
(X,S)
, can be viewed as the space

X
where the points
x\in X
are only known with some ‘experimental error’; if
x
is the result of a measurement,
the actual point may be any
x^{\prime}
with
(x,x^{\prime})\in S
. The traditional approach in model theory, in this situation, was to factor
out the equivalence relation generated by
S
, losing track of the topology; or the smallest closed equivalence relation containing
S
, retaining a locally compact space but usually a much smaller one. Viewing
(X,S)
as an object in
\mathsf{ApprSp}
is an alternative way of ‘factoring out
S
’; it preserves, in general, much more information. Only in case
S^{\circ m}
is an equivalence relation
E
for some
m
, does the object
(X,S)
of
\mathsf{ApprSp}
reduce to the quotient space
X/E
.

In some cases we can do better, obtaining an object of
\mathsf{CorSp}
canonically.

B.3.
The categories
\mathsf{CoGr}
and
\mathsf{CApprGr}

Here the objects are pairs
(G,K)
where
X
is a locally compact topological group
and
K
is a compact, normal, symmetric subset of
G
. A morphism
(G,K)\to(H,L)
is a subset
M\leq G\times H
,
with
(1,1)\in M
,
M=M^{-1}
and
M^{2}\subset M(K\times L)
.

We have a natural functor
\mathsf{CoGr}\to\mathsf{CorSp}
, mapping
(G,K)
to
(G,A_{K})
where
A_{K}=\{(x,y)\in G^{2}:xy^{-1}\in K\}
.

The category
\mathsf{CApprGr}
is obtained from this in a similar way to
\mathsf{ApprSp}
.

An example is the object
(T,K)
of
\mathsf{CApprGr}
, where
T={\mathbb{R}}^{2}/\Lambda
is a torus,
\Lambda\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
a torus in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
and
K=[0,1]\times\{0\}
is a one-dimensional interval. Typically,
K
generates a dense subgroup of
T
,
and so
K/\langle K\rangle
is an indiscrete space, and
K/cl(\langle K\rangle)
is a point. On the other hand
(T,K)
retains considerable information, in fact
T
may be recovered from it.

Appendix C
Group-theoretic terminology

We collect here a few group-theoretic definitions and statements that we will need.

Definition C.1
.

Let
G
be a topological group.

(1)

We denote the connected component of the identity in
G
by
G^{0}
.
[x,y]
is the commutator
xyx^{-1}y^{-1}
.
For
X,Y\subset G
, the commutator
set

[X,Y]
is defined as
\{[x,y]:x\in X,y\in Y\}
.
The group generated by
X
is denoted
\langle X\rangle
. (The commutator subgroup is thus
\langle[G,G]\rangle
.)

C_{G}(A)
denotes the centralizer of
A
in
G
,
C_{G}(A):=\{g\in G:(\forall a\in A)(ga=ag)\}
.

Z(G):=C_{G}(G)
. A
centralizer subgroup
is one of the form
C_{G}(X)
for some
X
; equivalently, a subgroup
H
with
C_{G}(C_{G}(H))=H
. We say that a subset of a group is
normal
if it is conjugation invariant.

(2)

We will denote the image of a subset
X
of
G
modulo a normal subgroup
N
by
X/N\subset G/N
.

(3)

The
commensurator

{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Comm\,}}}(\Lambda)
of a symmetric set
\Lambda\subset G
is the group of elements
g\in G
with
g\Lambda g^{-1}
commensurable to
X
. When
\Lambda
is an approximate lattice, the commensurator always contains
the group
\widetilde{\Lambda}
generated by
\Lambda
.

(4)

A
vector group
is a topological group isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for some
n
. A
Lie group

is a group with a real analytic structure. (We will consider
p
-adic groups too, as algebraic groups with their natural
p
-adic topology, but will not use the term Lie groups for them.)

(5)

By a
basic abelian group
we mean a compactly generated locally compact Abelian group with no nonzero compact subgroups. Such a group has the form
A={\mathbb{Z}}^{l}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
l,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

(6)

A normal subgroup
N
of
G
will be called
rigid
if it is closed in
G
,
and can be written
N=DE
with
D\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
,
D
central in
G
, and
E
a Euclidean space on which
G
acts by rigid motions; thus the action of
G
by conjugation factors through
O_{n}=Aut(E_{n})
.

(7)

A precompact subset of
G
will be called
rigid
if it is contained in some rigid normal subgroup.

(8)

G
is
compactly generated
if some compact
X\subset G
generates
G
as a pure group.
Given such a compact generating set
X
, let
f:Free(X)\to G
be the natural map from the free group generated by
X
to
G
; then
G
is
compactly presented
(via
X
) if
\ker f
is generated (algebraically) by words in
X
of some bounded length
r
.

(9)

cl(S)
dentoes the closure of
S
, when
S
is a subset of a given topological space.

(10)

We will say that a set
W\subset G
is discrete in
G
if it has no accumulation points in
G
.

W
is
uniformly discrete
if
1
is not an accumulation point of
W^{-1}W
.

(11)

A subset
W\subset G
is
precompact
if the closure of
W
is compact.

(12)

Let a group
G
act on a metric space
X
by isometries.
We say that a subset
W
of
G
is
bounded
(with respect to this action) if for some
a\in X
,
Wa:=\{w(a):w\in W\}
is
contained in a ball of finite radius. Equivalently, for
all

a\in G
,
Wa
has finite radius. (Since
d(a,b)=d(ga,gb)
, if

d(a,ga)\leq\beta
we have

d(b,gb)\leq d(b,a)+d(a,ga)+d(ga,gb)\leq\beta+2d(a,b)
.)

(13)

A quasimorphism is an quasi-homomorphism
f:G\to{\mathbb{R}}:K
for some compact
K\subset{\mathbb{R}}
. It is

homogeneous
if
f(x^{n})=nf(x)
. Any quasimorphism is uniquely a sum of a bounded function into
{\mathbb{R}}
and a homogeneous quasimorphism (
[
41
]
.) Note that a homogeneous quasimorphism
f
is symmetric, i.e.
f(x^{-1})=-f(x)
, since
f(x)+f(x^{-1})
is also homogeneous, and bounded. We also have
f(y^{-1}xy)=f(x)
, since
|f(y^{-1}x^{n}y)-f(x^{n})|
is bounded.

(14)

Let
G_{i}
be a topological group, for
i\in I
.
Let
C_{i}
be an open subgroup of
G_{i}
. The restricted product
\Pi^{\prime}G_{i}
is defined to be the subgroup
G
of
\Pi_{i}G_{i}

consisting of elements
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
with
a_{i}\in C_{i}
for all but finitely many
i\in I
. If each
G_{i}
is locally compact
and almost every
C_{i}
is compact, then
G
is locally compact.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
A
be a basic abelian group, and
H
a group of automorphisms of
A
.
Let
D
be a dense subset of
A
, and assume
Hd
is precompact, for each
d\in D
.
Then
H
preserves an innder product on
A^{0}
; and one can write
A
as a direct sum
A=A^{0}\oplus B
, with
B\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
fixed pointwise by a finite index subgroup
H_{1}
of
H
.

Proof.

We have
A^{0}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Since
D
is contained in no proper subspace of
A^{0}
, one can find a basis
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in D
for
A^{0}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
So
Hc_{1},\cdots,Hc_{n}
are precompact. Let
G
be the subgroup of
GL_{n}({\mathbb{R}})=End(A^{0})

preserving
cl(c_{i}^{H})
for each
i
. Then it is clear that
G
is a compact subgroup of
GL_{n}({\mathbb{R}})
; hence
G

preserves an inner product on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(namely the integral over
G
of the
G
-conjugates of the standard inner product).
Thus
H
too preserves this inner product; and so every
H
-orbit on
A^{0}
is precompact.

Next consider the action on
A/A^{0}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
. Since
A/A^{0}
is discrete, the image of
D
is all of
A/A^{0}
.
Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-basis for
A/A^{0}
. Then the orbit
E_{i}
of
e_{i}
is finite;
we have restriction maps
H\to Sym(E_{i})
; the common kernel of these maps is a
finite index subgroup
H_{1}
of
H
, fixing each
e_{i}
and hence fixing
A/A^{0}
. Lift
e_{i}
to a point
d_{i}
of
D
.

Claim .
Some point
b_{i}
of
d_{i}+A^{0}
is fixed by
H_{1}
.

Proof.

Since
H_{1}
fixes
A/A^{0}
, it leaves invariant the
A^{0}
-coset
d_{i}+A^{0}
. Also,
H_{1}d_{i}
is precompact. The
closed convex hull of
H_{1}d_{i}
is a compact, convex,
H_{1}
-invariant subset of
d_{i}+A^{0}
.
Since the Euclidean distance is preserved, so is Lebesgue measure, and so also the center of mass of this convex set; it is a fixed
point for the action of
H_{1}
. ∎

Let
B=\langle b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}\rangle
. Since the image of
B
modulo
A^{0}
is
{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
, we also have
B\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
. The decomposition we promised is achieved.

∎

Discrete groups whose every conjugacy class is finite (called FC-groups)
were studied by I. Schur, R. Baer, and B.H. Neumann, see
[
56
]
.
This was later extended to locally compact groups, and precompact conjugacy classes, by Ushakov
and Tits
[
67
]
, with emphasis on the connected case, and by

[
24
]
,
[
69
]
in general. Roughly speaking, such groups are extensions of abelian groups by compact ones.
We require a version describing a single precompact conjugacy class.
Thanks to Yves de Cornulier for explaining the relevant literature; previously, the deduction of this local version was a little more elementary but considerably more elaborate.

Proposition C.3
.

Let
H
be a locally compact topological group.
Let
K
be a normal precompact subset of
H
.
Then there exist closed normal subgroups
N\leq A\leq H_{1}\leq H
with
H/H_{1}
finite,

N
compact, with
A/N
basic abelian and rigid in
H_{1}/N
, such that
\langle K\rangle\cap H_{1}\leq A
.
Moreover,
N,A\leq cl(\langle K\rangle)
.

Proof.

Let
G=cl(<K>)
be the closed subgroup of
H
generated by
K
. Then
G
is normal in
H
.

By
[
15
]
2.C.6, a locally compact group is compactly generated iff every open subgroup
U
generates it, once it is augmented by some finite set.
This condition clearly holds for
G
, since the compact set
cl(K)
generates a dense subgroup;
it suffices to add to
U
a finite set
F
such that
cl(K)\subset FH
.
Thus
G
is compactly generated.

Let
B(G)
be the union of all precompact conjugacy classes of
G
. Then
B(G)
contains
<K>
, so it is dense.
By
[
16
]
, Theorem 2.8, B(G) is closed; hence must equal
G
.

Now
[
24
]
, Theorem 3.20, applies; by this theorem,
G
has a characteristic compact normal subgroup
P
, with
G/P
basic abelian. Now
H
acts on
G/P
by conjugation. Let
D
be the image of
<K>
in
G/P
; any
d\in D
has pre-compact orbit
under the action of
H
. By Lemma
C.2
, a subgroup
H_{1}
of finite index in
H
acts rigidly on
G/P
. Let
A=G\cap H_{1}
,

N=P\cap H_{1}
; then all the statements are clear. ∎
